- **Page side** (`content/shell-main.js`, manifest content script, `world: "MAIN"`, all frames, `document_start`; does nothing outside the card): the page's `pushState`/`replaceState` entries go on the top window and Back/Forward between them are replayed as `popstate`/`hashchange` in the card (needs the card's unique `name`, else WebKit refetches the page as a frame on Back without the page cache); `document.referrer` is the real one; `window.close()` closes the tab.
- **Skipped loads keep the push layout:** not the top frame, not `text/html`, quirks or limited-quirks mode, not UTF-8 (a written frame is always UTF-8, so forms would send the wrong bytes), injected after parsing started or after the load (e.g. pages already open when the script gets registered), no Navigation API (Safari < 26.2), popups (`window.opener`), sandboxed (opaque origin) and cross-origin-isolated pages, and opted-out URLs. Each skip logs `[margin] fitted layout: skipped: …` at debug level.
- **Sidebar in the fitted layout:** no push. The card's geometry follows the sidebar (`Margin.shell.setLayout`): inset in the frame while docked; the whole window when Keep open is turned off on a fitted page (the sidebar then floats over it, and hover reveal reads the card's pointer events).
- **Chrome builds keep the push layout** (`platform.shell: false`): Chrome runs a content script's `document.write` under the extension's CSP, so the page's scripts wouldn't run (research: a page-world writer would be needed, which Trusted Types block).
- **Accepted losses** (research `reviews.md`): Reader, Hide Distracting Items and top-frame-only extensions see the shell; File → Print prints the shell's view; streaming pages render only once their HTML is complete; typed form fields aren't restored on Back without the page cache; Navigation-API single-page apps become full loads; "Leave page?" can be asked twice; text fragments; content-blocker rules keyed to the page URL; `target=_parent` from cross-origin frames; WebKit's third-party redirect blocking of page `top.location` calls; framing is detectable.
- **Verified:** unit tests (`tests/shell.test.js`) for the skip rules, loop breaker, doctype and attribute serialization, POST rebuild, bust detection and exclude patterns. A scratch harness in Playwright's WebKit 26.6 (macOS) injected the shipped `shell-logic.js` + `shell.js` at `<html>` creation and passes 42/43 checks (after both review rounds below; 29/30 before them): card viewport 1012px in a 1280px window (innerWidth, fixed header, 100vw, media query), nonce scripts run once, card `load` fires, no page script in the top, one document request on an X-Frame-Options DENY + `frame-ancestors 'none'` page, title/icon/theme-color mirrored, links / GET / POST (body intact) / `location.assign` go top-level, Back, `pushState` + `popstate`, fragment links and Back, the bust guard (1 request), keyboard focus, scroll restored after reload, live title. (The one failure was the harness injecting late into a single-chunk response, correctly skipped.) That harness can't model extension isolated worlds or registered scripts; the real-Safari checklist is in TESTING.md. E2E (Playwright Chromium) not run for this change, per Tirth.

## Removing a registered script's files breaks all registered scripts (2026-10-25)

- **Seen in Safari:** after the probe was removed, loading Margin showed "Unable to find “shared/probe.js” in the extension’s resources", and the fitted layout never ran (no `[margin] fitted layout` logs).
- **Cause (WebKit source):** Safari keeps `registerContentScripts` registrations across reloads. At load, `loadRegisteredContentScripts` validates all stored scripts together; one missing file makes it return before adding any of them. That dropped the docked placeholder and the fitted layout too, and left `getRegisteredContentScripts` empty, so the background's cleanup couldn't see the probe's entry to remove it (and `unregisterContentScripts` refuses IDs it doesn't know).
- **Fix:** `shared/probe.js` and `content/probe-registered.js` are back as empty stubs, so the stored entry validates and everything loads. The background then unregisters `margin-probe`, retried on every registration sync (Safari loads stored registrations asynchronously after the background starts). Rule from now on: never delete a file a registered script uses in the same release that stops registering it; leave a stub for a release or two.

## Fitted layout: closed shadow roots, CSP diagnostics (2026-10-25)

- **Reported (Safari 27):** Google's AI Overview rendered unstyled in the card (visually hidden headings showing, an icon at full size, raw light-DOM text) while the rest of the results page was fine; YouTube showed no scrollbar; "Refused to execute a script because it does not appear in the script-src directive" in Google's console and on some other sites.
- **Closed declarative shadow roots are serialized now.** The shell used to keep only open roots: a closed one can't be reached from page APIs, so its host went into the card without its shadow tree and styles, which is exactly that symptom. Safari 26+ gives extensions `browser.dom.openOrClosedShadowRoot()` (WebKit `WebExtensionAPIDOMCocoa.mm`; MDN BCD Safari 26), and `getHTML({ shadowRoots })` serializes any root passed in, closed ones as `<template shadowrootmode="closed">`. Checked in WebKit 26.6: without the root the host serializes as bare light DOM; with it, the card rebuilds the closed root with its styles (and it stays closed to the page). Whether Google's AI Overview uses closed roots couldn't be confirmed here (Google serves this machine its bot check), so this is the most likely fix, not a verified one. The log line now says how many shadow roots, and how many closed ones, went into the card.
- **The CSP message** is WebKit's `'strict-dynamic'` early check refusing an inline script with no valid nonce (`ContentSecurityPolicy::allowScriptForStrictDynamic`). Google's policy has the same shape (nonce + `'strict-dynamic'`); in the WebKit harness with that exact policy, the page's nonce scripts run in the card and nothing is refused, because the parser's nonces are intact in the detached tree (WebKit hides nonces only on insertion into a connected document). One likely source that isn't the page: the shell's empty `srcdoc` card inherits the page's CSP, so another extension that injects an inline script into every frame (password managers, dark-mode tools) is refused there, which doesn't happen without the shell, and is harmless. To tell for sure, the shell now logs every violation in the card and in the shell at debug level: `[margin] fitted layout: the page's CSP blocked … from <source>:<line> "<sample>"`.
- **YouTube's scrollbar:** not reproducible headless (WebKit's overlay scrollbars aren't in screenshots). YouTube's scrollbar CSS (`scrollbar-color` on `<html>`, `::-webkit-scrollbar` on `<body>`) and its body attributes are identical in and out of the card, and WebKit's macOS scrollers ignore `scrollbar-color`. Waiting on details from Tirth's Safari.

## Fitted layout: window.parent is the page itself (2026-10-25)

- **Google's AI Overview rendered unstyled in the card.** Found with Tirth's Safari: the plain page had 17 `<style data-ck data-css-ids data-hstd>` in `<head>`, the card none. They come from Google's AI Mode module (`gstatic …/search-next.aim…/m=b,dpf`): it gathers the component styles streamed with the answer, builds one `<style data-hstd>` per CSS key and appends it to `Oha()`'s document, which is `window.parent.document` (the module also runs as a guest in an iframe), then removes the originals. In the card, `window.parent` was Margin's shell, so the AI Overview's CSS ended up in the shell's head and styled nothing.
- **Fix (`content/shell-main.js`):** in the card, `window.parent` is the page's own window and `frameElement` is `null`, as for a top-level page (`parent` is `[Replaceable]`; `window.top` is unforgeable and stays the shell). Margin's isolated scripts keep their own view. Safety net (`content/shell.js`): any `<style>` or stylesheet `<link>` that isn't Margin's and lands in the shell's `<head>` or `<body>` is moved into the card's `<head>`. The page-side script now logs `page-side script active in the card`, so its injection into the card is visible in Safari. Harness: a page hoisting styles Google's way, and one appending to `window.top`, both keep their styles applied; 44/45 checks.
- **The CSP refusals were Google's own:** the same four "Refused to execute a script…" errors appear with the fitted layout off (Tirth's check). They come from inline scripts Google's runtime builds while streaming the AI answer, not from the transplant. The `[margin] fitted layout: the page's CSP blocked …` log line stays; the heavier per-script diagnostics were removed. The closed-shadow-root change stays (it removes a real gap) but wasn't the cause.

## Fitted layout: navigations refused by the page's frame-src (2026-10-25)

- **Reported (GitHub):** every link in the card failed with "Refused to load … because it does not appear in the frame-src directive". WebKit checks each cross-document navigation of the card against the shell's CSP (the page's own header policy: GitHub sends `default-src 'none'` with a short `frame-src` list) in `PolicyChecker::checkNavigationPolicy`, before the Navigation API `navigate` event exists, so the router never saw it. Affects any site whose CSP limits frames: links, forms, `form.submit()`, script navigations, meta refresh and reloads. The policy can't be loosened (it's the response header, and Safari can't change response headers).
- **First attempt, rejected by an adversarial review before shipping:** taking clicks and submits over synchronously. Turbo-style routers that re-register their window listener to run last lost their clicks on every site, links in rich-text editors navigated away, pings and image-map coordinates were lost, download links opened the file, synthetic submit events submitted, and a page's `form-action` was bypassed for GET forms.
- **Fix: record, don't act** (`content/shell.js` routeCard / redoRefused). The browser still decides what a click or submit does. Margin records trusted link activations (capture phase, modifiers included) and every submission the browser builds (`formdata` event, which `form.submit()` fires too, with the exact data). Without a restrictive frame-src nothing changes: the `navigate` router handles it as before. With one, WebKit refuses the navigation and reports it in the shell (the card is the shell's only frame of its own), and the refusal is matched to a recorded activation by how WebKit reports frame-src URLs (`createURLForReporting`: another origin as just its origin, the page's own without fragment or credentials, data:/blob: as the bare scheme; `shellLogic.reportKey`). Then it's redone with the full recorded URL: a plain link at the top (referrer policy kept), ⌘- or middle-click as a new tab after the page in click order (background unless ⇧; ⌘⌥ a new window; background `openLink`), ⌥-click as a download, ⇧-click (Reading List, which extensions can't reach) left alone, a form rebuilt at the top with its method, body and files (GET as a form too, so form-action still applies). A refusal nothing matches is redone only when it's a same-origin URL reported in full (script navigation, reload); anything else is left alone rather than guessed. Untrusted (forged) refusals and refusals of other frames page code put into `window.top` are ignored. Download links to the page's own origin or a blob:/data: URL are saved from the shell right away (the CSP refuses them before the download starts, and pages often revoke blob: URLs straight after the click).
- **Verified (WebKit 26.6 harness),** each with a GitHub-like CSP and without: links, GET/POST forms, `form.submit()`, `location.href`, reload, client-side routers (pushState and a Turbo-style window router), hash links, contenteditable links, pings, image maps, same-origin and blob: downloads, synthetic submit events, a cross-origin link (full URL kept), ⌘-click on it (tab stays), a same-origin link with a #fragment (kept), an iframe put into `window.top`, a forged refusal; the full harness 44/45 with the default, Google (`strict-dynamic`) and GitHub-like policies. Not reproducible headless: Safari opening the ⌘-click tab and the ⌘⌥ window.

## Option+S, pinning from the tab menu (2026-10-25)

- **Option+S did nothing:** it was the `toggle-sidebar` manifest command's suggested key until 9862fcd gave the four suggested keys Chrome allows to the tab and space commands. Safari kept the old binding until the extension was reinstalled. Now a page listener handles it, like the palette's Option+K, as a setting (`sidebarShortcut`, Settings → Keyboard shortcuts → "Show or hide the sidebar"). A 200ms guard stops a `commands` key and the page listener from toggling twice.
- **Pin Tab, Move to, Close Tab in the right-click menu did nothing on a real click:** the menu closed itself on any `pointerdown` whose `composedPath()` didn't include the menu, but that listener was on the document, outside the sidebar's closed shadow root, where `composedPath()` stops at the host. So the press closed the menu, and its click found nothing. The tests clicked with `element.click()` (no `pointerdown`), so they passed. Fixed: presses inside the sidebar are judged by a listener on the sidebar root; the document listener only handles presses outside it.
- **Pinning also falls back** to the tab's window space when Margin hasn't placed the tab in a space yet, and logs `[margin] pin tab …: pinned / not pinned (…)` in the background console.

## Smoother page loads and tab switches (2026-10-25)

- **Reported:** (1) with the fitted layout, every new page went blank for a moment before the page and sidebar appeared; (2) switching to a tab not used in a while showed the sidebar flickering and a blank page before the content.
- **(1) Cause (WebKit source and a WKWebView probe with Safari's engine, isolated-world injection):** Safari keeps showing the previous page until the new one is visually non-empty or its main frame has finished loading (`completePageTransitionIfNeeded`). The hidden shell is never visually non-empty, and its load finished at `DOMContentLoaded`, before the page was even in the card: the card's frame had finished loading its empty `srcdoc`, and WebKit doesn't count the requests of a frame that's already done (`DocumentLoader::isLoadingInAPISense`). Safari then painted the hidden shell until the page's stylesheets loaded (measured: finished at ~160ms, shown at ~460–980ms on the test pages). The uncommitted first attempt (a hidden frame with a `document.open()` that's never closed) was measured to change nothing: an open parser that's idle doesn't count as loading either, and neither did a `<video>` or MediaSource load.
- **Fix (`content/shell.js`):** each of the page's pending stylesheets is also linked from the shell's own head, `media="not all"` (no effect there). WebKit counts those requests for the shell, so its load can't finish before the page's CSS is in, and they share the page's requests (WebKit reuses its preloads, "Always use preloads"; checked with a request log: one fetch per stylesheet). The page is written into the card in the `DOMContentLoaded` task itself when the card is ready (it usually is), and shown in that same task when nothing is pending. Measured in the probe: Safari finishes loading only after the page is shown, on every test page (fast, slow CSS, busy script, slow image, `<base>` and `crossorigin` stylesheets, three CSP modes). Also fixed: a stylesheet the page's CSP blocks fires neither `load` nor `error` in WebKit, so the shell waited 1.5s for it; its violation now counts as settled (shown at ~170ms instead of 1.7s).
- **Side effect handled:** the shell's `load` event now waits for the card's (all its images), and until then WebKit gives a navigation without a user gesture no history entry of its own (`NavigationScheduler::mustLockBackForwardList`), so redoing a link or GET form the page's CSP refused in the card (which happens after the click) made Back skip the page. An earlier review found the same regression in the first attempt. Fixed: redone links always go through a hidden link click (not locked); a refused GET form is redone as a link while the shell is loading (the card already checked it against `form-action`; WebKit `FrameLoader::submitForm`). POST forms go through the `navigate` router during the click, with its user gesture. Harness: links, GET and POST forms keep their entry with a slow card-only image, with GitHub-like, Google-like and default CSPs.
- **Adversarial review before committing** (4 lenses, a skeptic per finding; 7 of 18 confirmed and fixed): the holds were made before the page's `<meta>` CSP reached the shell, so they could fetch a stylesheet the page blocks and keep Safari loading until it timed out (now the CSP goes first, and no early holds on pages with a `<meta>` CSP); a report-only or non-style CSP violation counted as a blocked stylesheet (now only enforced `style-src`/`default-src` ones); the hidden-link path also took navigations scripts started, which natively get no history entry of their own during load (now only what the user activated, by `navigator.userActivation`); a GET form redone as a link skipped `form-action` on redirects (now only when neither the refusing policy nor a `<meta>` one has `form-action`, so on GitHub-like sites a GET form submitted before the page's images finish still gets no entry of its own: accepted, the page's rule wins), sent textarea line breaks as LF (now CRLF, as a form does) and ignored `accept-charset`; the ready signal fired on the hold timeout and on failure, before the page was in the card (now only once it's shown); and leaving the page only cancelled a switch once the next page arrived (now at `beforeunload`, unless "Leave page?" is asked). Also seen, not caused by this: a `<meta http-equiv=refresh>` in the card gets a history entry of its own (natively it replaces), as at HEAD.
- **Rejected:** writing the card at the shell's `load` event instead (no lock at all): by then WebKit has dropped the page's preloads, so the card fetched `no-store` stylesheets again and revalidated `no-cache` ones, with a blank meanwhile. Opening the card in its own `load` handler: the frame still counted as done. A card without `srcdoc` (the initial `about:blank`, available at once): WebKit may still navigate it to `about:blank` over the page.
- **Known gap:** when a page's whole HTML arrives before the card's `srcdoc` has loaded (~15ms, so a small cached page), the write waits a task as before and a page without stylesheets can still show the hidden shell for that moment (measured 3–9ms, same as before).
- **(2) Sleeping tabs: the background wake was built, then removed.** Safari unloads tabs it hasn't shown in a while and loads them again when shown. Margin tried to load such a tab in the background (`tabs.reload`) behind a veil on the current page and switch once it was drawn. **Safari's own WebKit log (read with `/usr/bin/log show`, passively) showed it never worked: all 21 unloaded tabs restored between 20:05 and 20:57 got their page created only at the moment they were shown (`restoreFromSessionState`, then `view visibility state changed 0 -> 1` within 1–5ms), never in the background. Safari ignores `tabs.reload` for a tab it has unloaded** until the tab is shown, so the veil only added Margin's 1.5s "not loading in the background" wait before the same in-front load. (The WebKit test host had misled: there, its own tab code loaded hidden tabs.) Switching through Margin is a plain switch again, at once.
- **What the switch to an unloaded or long-hidden tab looks like, and why** (WebKit source and the same logs; research in the session): when a tab's view enters the window, WebKit hides its content until the page process commits a frame, and keeps the previous frame on screen for up to 250ms meanwhile only if the view was in a window before and still has its layers (`WebPageProxy::dispatchActivityStateChange`, `RemoteLayerTreeDrawingAreaProxy` sync wait). A tab hidden for ~4 minutes has its process suspended, which drops the layers, and an unloaded tab is restored with no frame at all, so Safari shows its empty (dark) view and then the page building up; the log shows 143–613ms blanks on plain Safari switches with no Margin involvement. No extension API can keep a background tab's view in a window or make Safari wait for its paint. What Margin can do is make its own part cheap and quick (it already draws in ~10–20ms), and keep the area behind the card the page's color (`Canvas`) so the moment before the page paints isn't gray.
- **Waking a tab's process before showing it, and showing a restored tab at once** (after Tirth's 21:13 recording; every black switch in Safari's log went to a tab whose process Safari had suspended 2–19s earlier, plus six tabs Safari restored after relaunching at 21:12:57). WebKit keeps the previous frame on screen while a shown tab paints only if the tab's layers are attached (`hasVisibleContent()` in `WebPageProxy::dispatchActivityStateChange`); suspension detaches them (`sendPrepareToSuspend` → `hideContentUntilPendingUpdate`, root set to null), and waking the process reattaches them right away (`processDidResume` → `unfreezeAllLayerTrees` runs the pending redraw on a 0s timer, not a hidden-page frame). Safari can re-suspend a process the instant nothing needs it (seen: 0s after a script ended), so before every switch Margin makes (sidebar, palette, pins, shortcuts) a script in the target keeps its process awake for 0.5s and the switch follows after 70ms; a tab with no page fails the script at once and is switched to without waiting. And `content/shell.js` asks the background as a page starts whether Safari is loading, as it's shown, a tab it had unloaded (shown in the last 3s, same URL); then there's no previous page to keep on screen, only Safari's empty view, so the shell shows at once (sidebar and frame, the card filling in) instead of after the page's stylesheets. Verified in WebKit's source and in the simulation and the off-screen host (a restored tab shows after 36ms, ordinary loads still wait); not yet seen in Tirth's Safari. **What stays out of reach:** the moment before a restored tab's document exists (Safari fetches it as it's shown; Margin's scripts can't run before that), and the first return to a tab after a Safari relaunch (WebKit only holds the frame for a view it saw enter a window while its process ran, `m_viewWasEverInWindow`).
- **Earlier mistakes on the way, for the record:** a ping to the content script (fails after a reinstall, WebKit bug, see below), a per-tab record of where Margin ran (emptied on reinstall and on quitting Safari), and an `executeScript` probe (correct, but pointless once the background load turned out not to happen).
- **Pages that were open when Margin is installed or updated:** WebKit adds the new copy in a fresh content-script world, while the old copy's sidebar stays in the page and its scripts stay alive (measured in the WebKit host: a reinstall gives the new copy no `Margin.content`, but the old sidebar is in the DOM; a change of Margin's site permissions instead re-runs the content scripts in the same world, next to the live copy). So: a copy that finds `Margin.content` does nothing (as before); a copy that finds only another sidebar takes over at once (removes it, and leaves swipe and pull-to-refresh to the old copy, whose handlers act on the page directly, so both would go back twice) and the page loads again to start clean when that can't lose anything: once it's hidden (a few seconds after the update for background tabs, staggered; when the user leaves it for the tab in front), never while it has text in any field or editor (in any same-origin frame; judged generously, since React keeps `defaultValue` equal to the typed value), a changed choice, media playing or a call going, and at most once per 30s (it tries again after). The background loads the page's URL again (`tabs.update`, a GET from the browser's side: a reload would send a form again, and the old copy's fitted layout cancels a page's own navigation to its URL; WebKit adds no history entry for the same URL; checked in the WebKit host), after registering the fitted-layout script, so the page comes back fitted. A page whose URL has a #fragment keeps running as it is (loading it again would only scroll). Pages that never had Margin just get it. Where a browser doesn't add content scripts to open pages itself, the background does, two seconds after install or update, to loaded pages it may access whose Margin isn't current (it said hello to this run of the background, or started after it). Sleeping tabs aren't woken for this. History: the original copy stopped when it found the old (dead) sidebar, so every open tab needed a manual reload; a version in between hid the old sidebar and left its listeners running; the next reloaded every such page at once, which two review rounds showed also fired on every permission change, could lose typed input (including React forms and iframe editors), resent form POSTs, cut off media, and gave up for good after a second update within 30s.
- **Verified:** unit tests; a simulation of `background.js` with a fake browser API (switches at once, no script run in the target; install injection, browser update, reload for update as a GET, #fragment URL left alone); the WebKit host, off-screen: direct switches, install takeover with a typed draft kept; Safari's own log for the wake (above). E2E not run, per Tirth.

## One bundled script per context (2026-10-25)

- **Why:** every page loaded ten content scripts (129KB with comments) before Margin could draw, the background ten through `importScripts`, and the new-tab page eleven. Tirth asked for a faster, better-structured codebase without a framework; React was rejected (Margin's UI is small, it would add a runtime to every page, and the slow parts are Safari's, not rendering).
- **What:** `npm run build` now bundles each context with esbuild (pinned dev dependency): `content/content.bundle.js` (the manifest's content scripts, in order: one 78.5KB file), `background.bundle.js` (the `importScripts` files, then `background.js`), `content/shell.bundle.js` (the fitted layout, registered by the background) and `newtab/newtab.bundle.js` (the new-tab page's scripts, as one module). Classic scripts, whitespace and syntax minified, names kept so errors stay readable. The dist manifest and `newtab.html` point at the bundles; the background registers the shell bundle when it was bundled itself (`MARGIN_BUNDLED`), the two source files otherwise. `extension/` still loads unbuilt.
- **Then real ES modules (same day):** the shared files export what they used to attach to `globalThis.Margin`, and each context imports what it uses (`content/main.js`, `background.js`, `content/shell.js`, `newtab/newtab.js` are the entry points). esbuild drops what a context doesn't use: the background bundle went from 44KB to 33KB, the new-tab page's from 65KB to 47KB, the content scripts' to 76KB. `extension/` no longer loads unbuilt (content scripts and Safari's service worker can't load modules); browsers load `dist/`. Still global, on purpose: `Margin.content` and `Margin.shell` (state shared between scripts injected separately into one page, and the guard against WebKit running the content scripts twice) and `Margin.platform` (for the console and the E2E tests). Fixed on the way: when WebKit ran the content scripts again in the same world (a change of Margin's site permissions), the sidebar stopped early but the palette's wiring ran again, adding a second Option+K listener (the key then opened and closed the palette); `content/main.js` now wires the palette only when the sidebar started.
- **The source files stay in dist too:** Safari keeps registered content scripts across launches and drops all of them when a file one lists is missing, so a registration from an earlier version (the two shell files) must still resolve until the new background re-registers. Loaded as plain scripts, those modules now fail to parse and do nothing, which is harmless for that moment.
- **Verified:** unit tests; headless Chromium (new-tab page and sidebar from the bundles, no errors); the off-screen WebKit host with Safari's extension engine: switching, the install takeover keeping a typed draft, a restored tab shown early, the fitted layout ("page ready"). Not yet seen in Tirth's Safari.

## Type checking with JSDoc (2026-10-25)

- **What:** `tsc --checkJs` (TypeScript 7, a pinned dev dependency; `tsconfig.json`, `npm run typecheck`, and first in `npm test`) checks `extension/` as it is: JavaScript with JSDoc types, no TypeScript source and no compile step, nothing added to the bundles. Not strict yet (no null checks).
- **The message protocol is typed from its one source:** `types/margin.d.ts` derives each message's payload and result from the background's `handlers` (exported for this only), and the pages' `send()` (content/sidebar.js, newtab/newtab.js, shared/ui.js) is typed with it, so a misspelled message or a missing field fails the check (verified with a deliberate typo and a missing `archiveId`). Each handler documents its payload, optional fields included. The stored state (`State`, `Settings`, `TabRecord`, …) is written out there too and used by the model, the view helpers, the background's `load`/`mutate` and the pages.
- **Turning it on found no bugs:** the 75 first errors were all DOM lookups typed as a generic `Element` and optional parameters inferred as required. They were fixed with annotations only; two spots where a "cleaner" check would have changed behavior were left as they were (`nodeType === 1` rather than `instanceof Element`, which is false for nodes a page script makes in the card's realm; `el.src`, the resolved URL, rather than the attribute).
- **Not typed:** the browser extension APIs (`browser`/`chrome` are `any`; no maintained types cover Safari's) and messages sent outside `send()` (`runtime.sendMessage` in content/shell.js, background → page commands).

## The background split by job (2026-10-25)

- `background.js` (770 lines) is now its listeners and startup, in the same order as before (a service worker must register listeners at the top level), plus `background/`: `store.js` (load/mutate), `handlers.js` (the message protocol), `tabs.js`, `search-engine.js`, `site-icons.js`, `registered.js` (docked placeholder, fitted layout), `archive.js`, `navigation.js`. The modules only define things; nothing runs on import, so startup does what it did, in the same order. Code moved as it was; the one change is `whenDockedSynced()` in place of reading the `dockedSync` variable from another file.
- **Regression found and fixed on the way:** since the bundling commit, `injectIntoOpenTabs` looked up the sidebar's content script by the file name `content/sidebar.js`, which the built manifest no longer lists, so it did nothing (Chrome; Safari adds content scripts to open pages itself). It now takes the manifest's content script that isn't in the page's own world; a build test checks that this finds the bundle.
- **Not split:** `content/sidebar.js` and `content/shell.js` are each one closure whose functions share many local variables; splitting them means passing that state around explicitly, which is the riskiest change for the least gain. Left for when one of them needs real work.
- **Verified:** type check and unit tests; headless Chromium calling each kind of message (hello, activateTab, pinTab, createSpace, moveTabToSpace, setSpace, closeTab → archive, restoreArchive, updateSettings) and next-tab navigation; the off-screen WebKit host (switching, including to a sleeping tab; install takeover keeping a draft; fitted layout; restored tab shown early).

## Themes: Margin's look as the Arcadia theme (2026-10-25)

- **Asked (Tirth):** a themes folder holding everything visual (components, structure, styles, icons, fonts), the current look named Arcadia; themes that may look entirely different, each with settings of its own; only the code in use loaded; themes downloadable and deletable, possibly from a server such as Cloudflare.
- **Not possible as asked, and why:** MV3 forbids running code an extension downloads (no remotely hosted code; `registerContentScripts`/`executeScript` take packaged files only), and App Store review forbids apps downloading executable code, so a theme with its own components must ship inside the extension. A future downloadable theme could only be data (CSS, colors, fonts, images) applied by code that ships. A shipped theme's files can't be deleted from the installed app; what's possible is that unused themes cost nothing (their code never loads). Download and delete were therefore not built.
- **What:** `extension/themes/<id>/` per theme, `themes/index.js` the registry. Arcadia holds the sidebar component (`sidebar.js`, from `shared/ui.js`), the chrome around the page (`chrome.js`: frame, card edge, loader pill, pull and swipe badges, zoom readout, until now built in `content/sidebar.js`), the palette view (`palette.js`), the new-tab page (`newtab.js` + `newtab.html`), its CSS (`styles/`), its first-paint placeholder template and generated placeholders, and `theme.js` (UI-free: defaults, its settings, bundle paths, the width range). Core keeps all behavior: `content/core.js` (was `content/sidebar.js`) decides when to show, dock, push, follow gestures and shortcuts and time the loader, and drives the theme's view through a small contract (`themes/register.js`: `mount()` → `setLayout`, `setWidth`, `setLoading`, `setPull`, `setSwipe`, `setZoom`, `setColorScheme`, `ready`, `update`, `isBusy`, `width`); the palette's shortcut and wiring stay in `content/palette.js`; `shared/dom.js` has the DOM helpers both use. The fitted layout takes the theme's frame inset and corner radius through `setLayout` instead of hard-coding Arcadia's.
- **Only the active theme loads:** the manifest injects core only (`content/content.bundle.js`, 27KB). The active theme's content bundle (`themes/arcadia/content.bundle.js`, 55KB) is a content script the background registers at runtime (`margin-theme`, swapped when the theme changes) and runs right after core in the same world; either may run first (core mounts the theme when it arrives). Pages that miss the registration (Safari loads stored registrations a moment after it starts; pages open when Margin is installed) ask the background (`injectTheme`, `executeScript` with `injectImmediately`, accepted by WebKit), and ask again on the next state change if that fails. The new-tab page is a bare host: it starts loading the active theme's module (`import()` of `themes/<id>/newtab.bundle.js`) alongside the ⌘T check and mounts it unless the background is closing the tab (it waits at most 300ms for that answer, and draws anyway if the tab survives). About 5KB of shared helpers are in both content bundles.
- **Settings:** `settings.theme` ('arcadia') and `settings.themes[id]`, normalized by each theme's `settingsOf`; Arcadia's is `{ sidebarWidth }` (moved from the top-level `sidebarWidth`, migrated on load and written back; pages reading older storage still get the old value). `updateThemeSettings` merges a theme's settings; `updateSettings` refuses unknown theme ids. Settings has a Theme row (Arcadia only for now).
- **Safari's stored registrations:** new registered paths (the theme bundle, `themes/arcadia/docked/w-*.css`) must stay in every later build; the old placeholder paths (`content/docked/w-*.css`) stay as copies, like `LEGACY_SHELL_FILES`.
- **Review before committing** (5 lenses, a skeptic per lens): confirmed and fixed: the new-tab page waited for the whole ⌘T round trip before drawing (and could stay blank if no answer came); the background's "is Margin here?" ping answered before the theme (and palette) were ready, so a ⌘T hand-off could close the tab with no palette (it now answers only once the palette is wired); ⌘N's palette opened before the state loaded (no space color); the theme request didn't retry; the fallback injected at document idle; E2E tests read the new-tab page before the theme drew it (`gotoLauncher` helper); unit tests didn't check the theme bundles' wiring. Also fixed: the smoke test still expected `background.js` since the bundling commit.
- **Verified:** type check and unit tests (104); headless Chromium: sidebar, palette, new-tab page, background messages, the fallback injection with the registration removed, Arcadia's width setting, docking at that width (placeholder path, push), unknown theme ids refused, ⌘T and ⌘N hand-offs; the off-screen WebKit host: switching (including a sleeping tab), install takeover with a draft kept, fitted layout, restored tab shown early, the new-tab page (dynamic import works in WebKit; pixel-identical to the previous build except a letter avatar whose color comes from the random extension host), a page open at install getting the theme through `injectTheme`. E2E not run, per Tirth.

## content/core.js split by job (2026-10-25)

- `content/core.js` (743 lines) kept startup, mounting the theme, state and rendering, the hello handshake, site icons and messages (251 lines). The rest moved as it was into `content/layout.js` (show/hide/dock, the push, the placeholder hand-over, hover, the show/hide key, live resizing), `content/trackpad.js` (gesture guard, pull-to-refresh, swipe, pinch zoom), `content/loader.js` (the page-load pill's timing) and `content/takeover.js` (taking over from an earlier copy). What they share they read through a `page` object (`view()`, `theme()`, `state()`, `setState()`, `firstRender()`, `host`, `send`, `onCard`), so nothing reads a stale copy.
- A review (a reviewer and a skeptic) found one difference, fixed: the show/hide key's capture listener had moved ahead of the navigation shortcuts' (both stop the event), so a key matching both would have toggled instead of navigating. Registration order is now as before.
- **Verified:** type check and unit tests; headless Chromium: hover reveal and hide, the show/hide key, Escape, click-away, pull-to-refresh (armed, reloads), swipe back and forward (badge), the pinch readout (shown, hidden after), the theme checks and the ⌘T/⌘N hand-offs; the off-screen WebKit host: switching, install takeover with a draft kept, fitted layout, restored tab, the new-tab page.

## The WebKit test host in the repo (2026-10-25)

- `tools/webkit-host/` (was a scratch tool of the sessions above): a macOS program on `WKWebExtensionController`, the extension engine Safari uses, that loads `dist/safari` into an off-screen window and plays scenarios. It now checks results itself (`check`/`checkPage` steps wait until a condition holds, then PASS or FAIL; the run exits 1 on a failure), and `npm run test:webkit` runs every scenario: switching (including to an unloaded tab, and pages open at install getting Margin), an update with a typed draft, the fitted layout, the new-tab page, a screenshot. It can also open the extension's own pages (`ext:` URLs, with the extension's web view configuration, which is what gives them `browser.*`).
- **Limits** (README): the tabs, window, sleeping and relaunching around the engine are the host's own code, which has differed from Safari before; no trackpad. Tab and window behavior is still confirmed in Safari's own log or in Safari.

## The converter's "world" warning (2026-10-25)

`scripts/make-xcode.sh` prints "The following keys in your manifest.json are not supported by your current version of Safari: world". It's the `"world": "MAIN"` of `content/shell-main.js` (the fitted layout's page-side helper), there since the fitted layout (43858ea). The converter's key list is older than Safari 26, which runs it: the fitted layout depends on it and works in Tirth's Safari, and the WebKit host logs it running ("page-side script active in the card"). Harmless; nothing to change.

## Fitted layout: pages that don't want a frame, and what the frame broke (2026-10-26)

- **Reported (Tirth, Safari, Keep open on):** docs.new loading in a loop; Outlook reloading on its sign-in page; Teams without its top bar (search, profile, settings); GitHub navigation broken between a commit's files; YouTube's scrollbar sometimes missing; claude.ai console errors. Asked for a generic fix: Margin must never block or hide a site's content.
- **How it was found:** the WebKit host now loads real sites, logs every navigation, and keeps each page's own errors (tools/webkit-host). Five investigations ran in parallel, each reproducing one group fitted vs not.
- **Outlook (reproduced, fixed):** login.microsoftonline.com detects the frame and navigates the top to itself with a new `iframe-request-id` each time, about every 250ms. Margin fitted every load again; its loop breaker counted by full URL, so it never tripped, and its frame-bust guard *cancelled* same-URL busts (blocking the page). Now a script navigation of the top to the same page (origin and path: `shell-logic.js pageKey`), without the user's input in the page, is let through, and that page isn't fitted again in the tab. One extra load, then it stays.
- **Loop breaker:** counted per page (pageKey: a looping page often changes its query each time), 3 builds within 20s, cleared once the user interacts with the page or it stays 3s (was: per full URL, 5s, 1s). A failed fit turns off just that URL; a bust or loop the page. (Google Docs reloads itself after an error with a new `usp=` parameter, the likely docs.new loop, not reproducible signed out; Docs is on the never-fit list below.)
- **GitHub (reproduced, fixed; generic):** WebKit freezes a `srcdoc` document's base URL at the tab's first URL, even after `document.open` and `pushState`. After a site navigated in-page, its relative URLs (`#…`, `?…`, relative paths, requests) resolved against the first page: a commit's file-tree link (`#diff-…`) took the tab to the repository. The card is now loaded as `srcdoc` and then switched to `about:blank` before the page is written in: an `about:blank` document's base follows the page, but a frame's *first* `about:blank` has the Navigation API switched off (the card's router stopped redoing links; caught by the host's navigation checks before committing), while a later one has it. Verified: links, POST forms, Back, no extra history entry, relative URLs after pushState, the GitHub flow.
- **Google Docs presence (reproduced, fixed):** shell-main.js made `window.parent` the page itself, while `window.top` (unforgeable) stayed the shell: `parent === self` but not `top`, a state no browser produces. Docs hides its collaborator presence when `parent != top`. The fake is gone: in the card, `parent === top === the shell`, as for a page in a frame of its own site. Stylesheets a page puts into `parent.document` (Google's AI Mode) are still moved into the card (stray-stylesheet observer; checked).
- **YouTube address bar (found, fixed):** YouTube calls a `pushState` taken from another frame, past shell-main's wrapper, so the top URL (address bar, ⌘R) went stale. After the card's `currententrychange` (in a microtask, once shell-main's wrappers have updated the top themselves), a top URL that still differs is replaced with the card's.
- **Review before committing** (two lenses, a skeptic each): confirmed and fixed: the address-bar sync first ran synchronously, rewriting the history entry being left on every page `pushState`, so Back stopped working on single-page sites (new host scenario `history`: two pushes, two Backs, a bypassed pushState); the loop breaker's first settings (15s, 60s) took pages that refresh themselves every few seconds, and quick repeated searches, for loops; a script paginating to the same path with another query was taken for a bust (now only without the user's input in the page); a failed fit turned off the whole path. Left: a page adding elements other than stylesheets to `window.parent.document` now puts them in Margin's top document (visible, outside the card); moving them all would also move other extensions' overlays, which belong there. None seen on a real site.
- **Teams and other frame-aware apps (not detectable):** Teams checks `self !== top` and then uses its embedded mode (`core.isHeaderDisabled` for `viewportContext: iFrame`, popup sign-in); no extension can hide or observe that check. Tirth chose: a built-in list never fitted (`shell-logic.js FRAME_AWARE`: Teams, Outlook/Office/SharePoint, Microsoft and Google sign-in, Google Docs, claude.ai), excluded from the registration and checked by the shell too; plus a one-click switch in the sidebar footer (▢ "Show this site normally" / ▣ "Fit this site again", Keep open only; the same `setFitSite` as the palette).
- **YouTube scrollbar (WebKit bug, left for now, Tirth's choice):** after a web view leaves and re-enters its window (a tab switch), a subframe's scrollbar stops drawing; reproduced with no extension (a page with a full-size iframe). Every fitted page's scroller is the card's, so after switching back the bar is gone until the next load; scrolling works. Possible later: Margin's own scroll indicator.
- **Headers (checked, not used):** Safari's extension engine can read response headers with `webRequest`, but they arrive at or after the shell's document_start decision (sometimes seconds late, never for pages a service worker serves), and `X-Frame-Options`/`frame-ancestors` predict breakage poorly (48 of 50 popular sites send one; Teams and YouTube allow 'self').
- **claude.ai:** not reproducible signed out; its app isn't cross-origin isolated (so it was fitted) and some of its routes redirect when framed (`window.top !== window.self`), so it's on the list.

## Chrome: "Margin full screen", full screen without a toolbar (2026-10-26)

- **Cause of the toolbar flash (verified in Chromium source, stable 156):** in its own full screen with the toolbar set to auto-hide, Chrome reveals the toolbar for exactly one second on every change of the active tab (`BrowserView::OnActiveTabChanged` → `RevealTopContainerIfNeeded`, a reveal lock released by a task posted 1s later; meant as "a visual clue as to what tabs are affected"). No pref, flag or policy gates it; any way of activating a tab (click, ⌘-click into a new tab, `tabs.update`, `tabs.highlight`, opening behind then switching) goes through it. It's skipped only with Chrome's vertical tabs on, or between the two halves of a Chrome split view. Tirth confirmed it in Chrome: switching to an existing tab from the sidebar flashes it too. (An attempt to open Margin's tabs behind and then switch to them was dropped: the switch itself is what reveals.)
- **Change (Chrome build, profile `marginFullscreen`):** a palette action, "Margin full screen (no toolbar) on/off" (`toggleFullscreen`, `background/fullscreen.js`). Margin puts the window into full screen itself (`windows.update({ state: 'fullscreen' })`), which Chrome treats as full screen with no toolbar at all (no reveal, so no flash on tab changes; none on hover either). From Chrome's own full screen it leaves that first (the mode has to start from a window that isn't in full screen), and turning it off restores the window's earlier state (normal or maximized), remembered per window in `storage.session`. Margin's sidebar (tabs) and palette (URLs, search) stand in for the toolbar; ⌘[, ⌘], ⌘R still work.
- **Verified:** unit test (the palette offers it only where the profile allows); headless Chromium: on, off, and on from full screen, with the remembered state. Not verifiable headless (no toolbar): that the toolbar stays hidden, hover, Chrome's exit notice, Esc. Tirth to try.
- **Automatic (Tirth's request, after trying it):** whenever a window goes full screen another way (green button, Globe-F, View → Enter Full Screen, ⌃⌘F), Margin switches it to Margin full screen (`followFullscreen` on `windows.onBoundsChanged`; setting `marginFullscreenAuto`, on by default, Settings → "In full screen, hide Chrome's toolbar"). Chrome's full screen can't be intercepted and the mode has to start from a normal window, so entering costs an extra step out and back in (about a second of animation). A page or video in full screen (the Fullscreen API; checked in the active tab) is left alone, and leaving Margin full screen another way (Esc, the menu) is noticed. Headless: Chrome entering full screen by itself becomes Margin's; leaving another way is forgotten; a page's full screen is left alone; with the setting off, Chrome's own stays.
- **Not done (yet):** showing the current URL and downloads in the sidebar.

## Chrome: Margin only in full screen (2026-10-26)

- **Request (Tirth):** in Chrome outside full screen, Margin shows nothing (no sidebar, nothing else), only a message offering full screen.
- **Change (Chrome build, setting `fullscreenOnly`, on by default; Settings → "Use Margin only in full screen"):** outside full screen, pages get no sidebar, peek, loader pill or navigation shortcuts. Reaching for Margin (the left edge, ⌥K, ⌥S) shows a toast instead: "Margin works in full screen.", with "Go full screen" (Margin full screen) and "Use in windows too" (turns the setting off). The new-tab page shows the same offer as a card instead of the launcher, and ⌘T doesn't hand off to the palette. Everything comes back as soon as the window is full screen, and goes away when it leaves (resize).
- **Full screen, as a page sees it:** first by the window's size covering the screen; wrong on Tirth's MacBook Pro: macOS keeps a full-screen window below the notch, so it's shorter than the screen (and exactly as tall as a Window → Fill window under the menu bar, so no size rule can tell them apart). Margin always said "works in full screen" there. Now the background says: `hello` and a `windowState` request answer from `windows.get().state` (`background/fullscreen.js isFullscreen`; while Margin switches a window, where it's going), and the window's pages are told on `windows.onBoundsChanged` and after Margin's own switches. A page doesn't draw Margin until it knows (one round trip), so Keep open stays docked from the first paint in full screen. Pages ask again on resize and when shown; the new-tab page asks on load and reloads when it changes. Headless: a full-screen window smaller than the screen (as headless keeps it) gets Margin, a normal window covering the screen gets the toast, Keep open never undocks on load, the new-tab page flips both ways. A review (three lenses, each finding checked by a skeptic) found nothing that held up; two hardenings taken (the switch is claimed before its first await; the new-tab page falls back to the card if the background can't answer).
- **Verified (headless Chromium):** windowed: the edge shows the toast, not the sidebar; palette and toggle key blocked; the toast's "Go full screen" enters Margin full screen; the new-tab card, both of its buttons; with the setting off, the sidebar opens windowed. Headless Chrome doesn't resize a window that goes full screen (it stays 800×600 with state "fullscreen"), and Playwright's viewport emulation hides resizes from the page, so the switch back on entering full screen was first checked by sizing the window to the screen (which hid the notch bug above). Tirth to try.

## Chrome: the fitted layout (2026-10-26)

- **Problem (Tirth):** in Chrome, "Keep open" cut pages off. Chrome had only the push layout (`html { margin-left }`), and the viewport keeps the window's width, so 100vw, fixed bars and wide layouts run under the sidebar or past the right edge (headless: YouTube's logo and menu under the sidebar; Figma, Stack Overflow, Maps, BBC, ChatGPT cut).
- **Options compared** (headless Chromium on real sites; a critic re-checked): (1) every page in a frame of an extension page with X-Frame-Options/CSP stripped (Chrome applies response-header rules): pages that load fit, but Google sign-in shows "Error 401" in the frame, Microsoft sign-in takes over the tab, frame-aware apps break, other extensions' scripts don't reach the pages, and the address bar, history and bookmarks get `chrome-extension://` URLs; the rule also strips protection from frames nested in each site. (2) Chrome's side panel: the only option that leaves pages untouched (Chrome narrows the tab), but Chrome's own 40px header, at least ~386px wide, right side unless the user changes Chrome's setting, opens only on a gesture, no peek; Tirth declined it. (3) Margin's fitted layout, ported: chosen. Header stripping doesn't help it (the card is a same-origin about:blank frame; no framing headers apply).
- **Why Chrome had no fitted layout:** a content script's `document.write` runs under the extension's CSP in Chrome, so the page's inline scripts never ran in the card. Now a page-world script writes it (`content/shell-writer.js`, Chrome only: `platform.shellPageWrite`). It's bundled into the manifest's page-world script in the Chrome build (scripts/build.js; the Safari build doesn't have it): Chrome runs registered page-world scripts after the isolated ones at document_start, whatever the registration order (measured), so a registered writer wasn't there yet when the shell asked. Before taking a page over, shell.js asks it (`margin-shell-probe`) whether it can write there; it creates its Trusted Types policy then. A page that requires Trusted Types and lists its policies refuses Margin's: that site isn't fitted in the tab, with no reload (before: every page loaded twice, and a POST result was sent again; reproduced). Then shell.js asks it to write (`margin-shell-write`); each listener is removed before it acts. On pages that aren't taken over a page script could trigger them, for nothing it couldn't do itself (the policy is one the page's CSP lets any script create; the write goes only into Margin's card). A `<meta>` Trusted Types CSP the probe can't see: the write fails, the page reloads once and the site is skipped.
- **Card setup:** Chrome doesn't run document_start scripts again in the card after the write (Safari does), so the card's history/referrer/name/close handling moved to `content/shell-card.js` (`setUpCard(card, top)`), used from inside the card in Safari (`shell-main.js`, as before) and from the top by the writer in Chrome. Both page-world scripts are now bundled (`content/shell-main.bundle.js`, `content/shell-writer.bundle.js`).
- **Chrome differences:** the card's switch to `about:blank` uses `location.replace` (Chrome gave it a history entry of its own, so the first Back went nowhere); the scroll to a URL's fragment is done by the writer with the popstate Chrome fires for it held back (the page without the shell gets none); the card's `window.name` is handed to the top when Margin redoes a navigation (the card's pagehide comes too late in Chrome, so the next page got the previous name); `<link rel=stylesheet>` without an `href` (GitHub's lazy ones) is no longer waited for (it never loads or fails; both browsers).
- **Only in full screen:** Margin works only in full screen in Chrome (`fullscreenOnly`), so the shell is registered only while a window is in full screen (registered.js; re-synced on full-screen changes and window close), with a flag (`content/shell-fullscreen-only.js`) that makes it skip windows still showing Chrome's toolbar (outer minus zoomed inner height over 40px; the registration is browser-wide, another window may be in full screen). In other windows pages load normally.
- **Review before committing** (three lenses, each finding checked by a skeptic): fixed the Trusted Types double load and POST resubmission, the shell running without its writer (now impossible: the writer comes with the manifest), fitting in normal windows while another is in full screen, the stray popstate and the late `window.name` (all reproduced, then rerun after the fix). Refuted: realm of the wrapped functions, persisted registrations across restarts.
- **Stack Overflow** navigates itself out of the card (as in Safari): one extra load, then it stays pushed in that tab.
- **Verified headless (Chromium):** GitHub, YouTube, Wikipedia, Reddit, Figma, Notion, BBC, Google Maps fitted, page width = card width, no overflow; a link, an in-page anchor, Back, Back, Forward, a search form, YouTube pushState and Back keep the address bar and card in step; Teams/Outlook go to Microsoft sign-in, which stays pushed (never-fit list); bot-check pages skipped by design (Cloudflare: cross-origin isolated; Google "sorry": quirks mode); registrations appear in full screen and go away in a window. WebKit host (Safari): all scenarios pass, including `fitted` and `history`.
- **Open risk:** other extensions' content scripts may not reach the card (a stand-in extension didn't see its fields). Whether real password managers (1Password, Bitwarden) fill logins in fitted pages needs Tirth's check; the sidebar footer's ▢ "Show this site normally" turns fitting off per site.

## Chrome: quiet after an extension reload (2026-10-26)

- **Reported (Tirth):** Chrome's extension errors page filled with `[margin] windowState Error: Extension context invalidated.` and one uncaught `Extension context invalidated.` After Margin is reloaded (or updated), the copies already running in open pages are cut off; the full-screen check (resize, tab shown) kept asking the background from them, and the update reload's `sendMessage` throws synchronously there, past its `.catch`.
- **Fix:** a cut-off copy has no `runtime.id`; `send()` (core.js), the update reload (takeover.js) and the shell's ⌘-click link (shell.js) do nothing then. The new copy (injected into open pages, background/tabs.js) takes the page over as before.
- **Verified:** headless Chromium, reloading the extension under an open page, then resizing and hiding/showing it: 4 warnings before, none after. WebKit host scenarios pass (including `update`).

## A sidebar beside pages without frames or pushing? (2026-10-26)

- **Question (Tirth):** a better way than the fitted layout's frame and the push layout, in Safari and Chrome. Investigated in four parts (Safari/macOS, Chrome browser features, page-level tricks tested on 8 sites in headless Chromium and WebKit, prior art), with a critic re-checking anything that seemed to work.
- **Answer: none from inside an extension.** A page's width comes from the area the browser gives it; page code can only shrink the drawing or misreport the width, so fixed headers, `100vw`/`100vh` and media queries stay tied to the full window. Tested and failing: root CSS zoom (+ push, + tab zoom), transform on the root, a 260px root scrollbar, meta viewport, faking `innerWidth`/rewriting `@media`, the isolation wrapper.
- **Browser features:** Safari has no extension sidebar (compiled out of WebKit; `sidebarAction`/`sidePanel` undefined in Safari 27), legacy app extensions only get popovers, Web Inspector/Responsive Design Mode can't be driven by extensions. Chrome: the side panel (declined); split view (`tabs.createSplit`, Chrome 155+) narrows for real but extensions can only make it 50/50, each tab switch means unsplitting and splitting again, and Margin's tab becomes the active one; `chrome.debugger` device emulation narrows the layout but leaves a strip nothing can draw in (on the right) and shows a "debugging this browser" bar on every tab; Document Picture-in-Picture floats over the page.
- **Prior art:** every pushing sidebar extension (MaxAI, Sider, Hypothesis, vertical-tab extensions) carries per-site patches or turns push off on some sites; the big AI sidebars moved to Chrome's side panel. Browsers with native sidebars (Arc, Edge, Vivaldi) resize the web view natively.
- **macOS full-screen Split View** (Margin's own small window tiled beside the browser, macOS sizing both): Tirth tried it by hand; the divider doesn't go anywhere near 260pt, so it's out.
- **A native Mac app** (Margin's containing app, with the Accessibility permission, outside the App Store): it can't change Safari itself (SIP, code signing), enable the extension or grant site access silently, or resize full-screen windows; it could narrow a normal Safari or Chrome window and dock a native sidebar window beside it (lag while dragging unknown). Tirth: full screen is not negotiable, so it's out.
- **A docked DevTools / Web Inspector as the sidebar** (Tirth's idea: docked left, the browser narrows the page for real; fill it with Margin's panel): it does narrow the page (headless Chrome: 1200px window, DevTools at 260 → page 940px, also in full screen). But no extension or app can open, dock or fake it: Chrome refuses every route (debugger `Target.openDevTools`, synthetic ⌥⌘I/F12, `devtools://` URLs); Safari has no API at all and its inspector is at least 500pt wide. The user would open it by hand in every tab, its toolbar always shows (Chrome ~27px row above Margin's panel), Chrome's minimum is ~251px (no rail), `debugger;` statements pause pages, DevTools-detection libraries close the page, ~200MB per tab, and policy can disable it. Out.
- **So:** the fitted layout (frame) stays the only general way to fit pages; push remains its fallback for sites that can't be fitted.

## The narrow rail on pages that can't be fitted (2026-10-26)

- **Why (Tirth):** the push fallback cuts pages under a 260px sidebar. Sites detect a frame with `window.top`, which browsers make unforgeable on purpose (anti-clickjacking), so frame-aware apps (Teams, Outlook, Docs, sign-in pages) can never be fitted. Tirth's idea: make the sidebar much narrower there instead of pushing by its full width.
- **Change (both browsers; setting `compactWhenPushed`, on by default, Settings → "show a narrow sidebar of icons"):** with Keep open, a page that isn't fitted shows the theme's rail (Arcadia: 52px, `theme.js RAIL`, `geometry.rail`): space chips, pin and tab icons (titles as tooltips), new tab, fit and Keep open buttons. The page is pushed by 52px only. Resting the pointer on the rail for 350ms opens the full sidebar over the page, which doesn't move; leaving folds it back (`content/layout.js`). A quick pass on the way to an icon doesn't expand it; icons work at once.
- **First paint:** pages known in advance to be pushed (the never-fit list, sites turned off by hand, or every page when fitting is off) get the rail's placeholder (`docked/rail.css`), the rest the full one (`registered.js placeholderPlan`, unit tested). Pages the shell skips for other reasons (quirks mode, cross-origin isolated, busting out) start at the full width and narrow to the rail.
- **Trade-off (expected):** it's still a push, so elements pinned to the window's left edge sit under the 52px rail, and full-window-width elements lose 52px on the right (a test page's pinned header: its left label under the rail). Whether Teams' and Outlook's left app bars and top-right buttons stay usable needs Tirth's check, signed in.
- **Review before committing** (three lenses, a skeptic per finding), all reproduced and rechecked after the fix: the rail expanded 350ms after the pointer entered it even while moving (now only on a real rest: the wait restarts while the pointer moves, a few px of jitter ignored); expanding moved the rows under the pointer (chips row vs column), so a click could hit another tab (the expanded rail now keeps the rail's stacking: same positions, only wider); a pending expansion fired after a click, Auto-hide, the toggle key or a tab switch (cancelled by all of those, and re-checked before it opens); in short windows the footer went off screen (spaces and pins scroll, the footer and a few tabs always show); the rail's buttons had no spoken names; the space editor could open hidden from the rail (not in the rail now); the placeholder and shell registrations were redone on every sync because Chrome returns patterns sorted (compared regardless of order). Declined: giving tabs already open when Chrome goes full screen the full push instead of the rail; they're pushed either way, and the rail hides less.
- **Verified:** headless Chromium: rail and 52px push on a site turned off by hand and on Microsoft and Google sign-in; a pass doesn't expand, resting does (page stays at 52px), leaving collapses; fitted pages keep the full sidebar; the setting off pushes by the full width. WebKit host: new `rail` scenario passes with the rest.

## One sidebar across pages (2026-10-26)

- **Reported (Tirth):** back on a tab not visited for a long time, its sidebar still listed the tabs from before (4 instead of 8) until a reload; each page's sidebar kept its own scroll position. Wanted: it should look like one sidebar, not one per page.
- **Cause:** each page redraws only on `storage.onChanged`; nothing redrew it when it was shown again. Safari suspends tabs it isn't showing, and a suspended page (or one restored from the back/forward cache) misses those events. Not reproducible off screen: the WebKit host and headless Chromium keep hidden pages receiving events (checked).
- **Changes:**
  - **Current when shown:** `content/core.js` `shown()` on `visibilitychange` → visible and `pageshow` (persisted): read the state again, whatever was missed. Same on the new-tab page.
  - **One scroll position per window:** the tab list's scroll is reported by the theme (`onScroll`, a new mount option), kept by the background per window (`background/scroll.js`, in memory and `storage.session`), and applied when a page draws first or is shown again (`update(…, { scroll })`). New pages open at it too.
  - **Open stays open:** clicking a tab or pin in a sidebar that's open over the page (hover, toggle, or the expanded rail) opens the next page's sidebar the same way (`activateTab`/`openPin` `show` → `carry-open` command); a page that's left closes an open-over-the-page sidebar (`layout.leave()`), so going back doesn't find it still open.
- **Performance:** measured with instrumented builds (headless Chromium, 40 tabs, 10 switches): the fix adds no measurable work. It showed an older cost: every state change (a tab switch makes two) re-read all of storage and redrew the sidebar in every open tab (40 redraws and reads per switch). Since a page now catches up when shown, hidden pages skip `storage.onChanged` (`!document.hidden`; also the new-tab page): in a real browser a switch costs about one redraw instead of one per open tab. Neither test environment hides background pages realistically (headless Chromium reports all pages visible; the WebKit host's off-screen window makes all hidden), so the saving itself is by reasoning on the standard `visibilityState`. A page that hasn't drawn its sidebar yet still takes every change: one loaded in the background before Margin's background had state otherwise never drew (caught by the WebKit host's `switching` scenario, after the first version of this was pushed as ba4ea13).
- **Review before committing** (two lenses, a skeptic per finding; all reproduced, then rerun after the fix): the restored scroll was applied to the old, shorter list and clamped when its reply beat the state read (Tirth's exact case: now applied after the fresh list is drawn); the last scroll reached the background up to 150ms late (now sent at once before a switch made from the sidebar and when the page is left, plus a second ask 400ms after a page is shown for switches by keyboard or the browser's tab bar); a late reply could undo the user's own scroll (theirs wins); the background's first read of the positions could lose a write (read once); a carried sidebar slid in again (`instantOnce`); a toggled sidebar became a hover one on the next page (the reason is carried); a carry to a page not listening yet was lost (kept 10s and handed over with `hello`); a carry on a Chrome page outside full screen left the sidebar stuck (ignored there); opening a pin in another window left two sidebars open (same window only); closing the shown tab from an open sidebar closed it (it stays open on the next tab).
- **Verified:** headless Chromium: open-over-the-page carries to the next page (old build: closes); on "shown again" a page lists all tabs and takes the window's scroll (A scrolled to 300 → B opens at 300; B to 120 → A at 120; a new page at 120; old build: each page its own). WebKit host suite passes. The real long-idle case needs Tirth's check in Safari.

## ⌘T and "+ New Tab", Arc-style; tabs newest first (2026-10-26)

- **Asked (Tirth):** ⌘T flickered (a tab appearing and vanishing). Wanted Arc's: ⌘T or "+ New Tab" opens the palette over the page you're on, which stays; the sidebar highlights "+ New Tab" instead of your tab; closing the palette without choosing (Escape, clicking outside) highlights your tab again; choosing opens the new tab. "+ New Tab" sits at the top of the tab list, below the pins; tabs newest first.
- **First attempt, dropped:** keeping ⌘T's tab and opening the palette in it. Reviewed: the browsers put keyboard focus in the address bar on a new tab (Chrome documents it; Safari noted in TESTING.md), so typing and Escape went there; choosing an existing tab left an empty tab behind; and the page behind the palette wasn't yours.
- **⌘T now (`background/handlers.js newTabHandOff`):** browsers always create a tab on ⌘T. Margin hands it straight back: the palette opens on the tab you were on (one `show-palette` message, answered true if it could), that tab is shown again without the wake (it was shown a moment ago), the new tab is kept out of the sidebar (`tabs.js handingOff`) and removed. It starts as soon as the browser creates the tab when it says it's a new-tab page (`onCreated` with `pendingUrl` chrome://newtab in Chrome), else when Margin's new-tab page asks, first thing (`newTabPage`). Measured (headless Chromium): the new tab is in front ~10–30ms from the second ⌘T of a session (old hand-off: ~100ms, with the wake and a separate ping); the first ⌘T of a session ~350ms (the browser's first load of the extension page). The tab you were on is the window's active one (Chrome reports the new tab before activating it), else the one most recently left; by `lastActive` alone a switch ties its two tabs and the wrong one won. If it can't (a new window, the tab you were on isn't a web page, or has no Margin, or doesn't answer within 1s), the palette opens on the new-tab page: closing it without choosing (Escape, clicking away) closes the tab again, back to yours (`cancelNewTab`; the window's only tab stays), choosing a tab, pin or "New window" doesn't leave it behind empty, an archived tab replaces it. Before closing the new tab Margin checks it's still the untouched new tab in front: if it was navigated meanwhile (an address typed while the previous page was busy, or another extension that opens a tab and then sends it somewhere) or left, it stays and the palette closes again (`hide-palette`).
- **Known limit:** Margin can't tell ⌘T from other ways of opening a new-tab page in front (Chrome's "New tab to the right", another extension's `tabs.create({})` that stays on it): those are handed off too. Same as before this change; the check above keeps tabs that are used.
- **Highlight:** the palette opened as "new tab" (⌘T, the row) sets `newTabPending` (content/palette.js → core); the sidebar (`view.sidebar(…, { newTabPending })`) highlights "+ New Tab" fully and your tab softly (`behind`: per Tirth, its page is still the one showing); the palette closing without a choice clears it at once, so your tab is fully highlighted again. Choosing something that shows another tab clears it once the page is hidden (at most 1.5s later), so the highlight doesn't come back to your tab for a moment first (`newTabCloser`). On Margin's start page (`#launcher`) the row works the same way and what's chosen opens in a new tab, keeping the start page.
- **Order:** tabs newest first: the browser's order reversed for display only (pins keep binding to the oldest tab with their URL, as `openPin`). The next/previous tab keys follow it. "+ New Tab" is the first row (the footer button went; the footer keeps the fit and Keep open buttons). The new-tab page being shown is that row, not a row of its own.
- **Veil before switching (asked about):** removed earlier (34611e1) after Safari's log showed it never loaded a tab in the background; waiting for Tirth to say which flicker remains before considering anything.
- **Review (two lenses, a skeptic each), confirmed and fixed:** the wrong previous tab after a switch (the tie above); ⌘T from a non-web page sent you to an older web tab; no timeout on `show-palette`; a new tab navigated during the hand-off was closed anyway; choosing flashed the highlight back to your tab; clicking away on the fallback palette kept the empty tab; the start page's row neither highlighted nor kept the start page. Checked: in Chrome the closed ⌘T tab isn't in recently closed, so ⌘⇧T still reopens the tab closed before it (Safari: TESTING.md).
- **Test host:** the WebKit host's `switching` scenario failed twice in about ten suite runs today, both while headless Chromium runs were running or closing; it passes on its own and in quiet suite runs (three in a row). Timing under load, not a regression found so far.

## Command palette sized exactly as Arc's (2026-10-26)

## Switching without black: wait for the wake, wake early, a switch log; ⌘T and Safari's "New tabs open with" (2026-10-26)

- **Asked (Tirth):** "black screen before I see the sidebar and page content at the same time" on switching to a tab that hadn't loaded. Read from his Safari's log (17:56–20:55, 500 switches; scripts in the session's scratchpad): the black is Safari's empty view. Two kinds: **restored tabs** (Safari unloaded them; 39, show to first frame median 137ms, max 614; the document arrives a median 82ms in, before any Margin code can run, so out of reach; 33 of 39 came within 3 minutes of a Safari relaunch; corrected later the same night from a longer log: all 52 such tabs were being shown for the first time since that launch, Safari's lazy restore, none unloaded after being shown) and **suspended tabs the wake didn't reach in time** (23 of 100: their process took 151–1092ms to come back, median 279, while the switch landed ~180ms after the wake, as tabs.update itself takes ~110ms; blank 45–214ms). The wake worked for the other 77 (70 with no blank at all).
- **Wait for the wake (`background/tabs.js wakeTab`):** besides the 0.5s keep-awake script, an instant one answers once the process runs again (its layers are back by then); the switch goes ahead then, at most 400ms after the wake started (the old page stays on screen meanwhile). No page (unloaded): both fail at once, no wait. Measured from his log, ~84% of wakes resume within that cap versus ~66% within the old fixed window. All of Margin's switches go through it (sidebar, palette, pins, next/previous tab, cancelling a ⌘T); the ⌘T hand-off doesn't wake (the tab was shown a moment ago). The keep-awake now lasts 1s (a reused wake, the wait and tabs.update all land inside it). Safari only (`platform.wakeTabs`): Chrome keeps a hidden tab's last frame, and its `executeScript` waits for a loading page by default, which made a switch to a loading tab wait the whole cap (review, measured); the scripts also ask to run at once (`injectImmediately`, which WebKit ignores, running them at once anyway). A held next/previous key doesn't wait for the tabs it only passes through (70ms while more presses are queued), so the switching stops with the key.
- **Wake early:** a press on a sidebar tab or open pin (pointerdown, before the click), the palette's selection resting 120ms on a tab or pin, and, after a next/previous switch, the tab after it (presses come in runs). A switch within 400ms of an early wake picks it up instead of starting another, so the wait is mostly over by the time of the click. The palette wakes each row once per opening (its refreshes repaint the same selection).
- **Switch log (development; remove before release):** each switch Margin makes (what asked, the wake's outcome and time, whether it started early, how long until the tab was in front) and how each page just shown came on screen (the fitted layout's release reason and time, whether Safari was restoring it), in the background's IndexedDB (no storage events for pages), last 300. Start page → Settings → "Switch log (development)" → Copy puts it on the clipboard as JSON.
- **⌘T opening Safari's homepage:** Safari hands ⌘T to an extension's new-tab page only while Safari Settings → General → "New tabs open with" is that extension. This build is ad-hoc signed (no signing identity on the Mac), so Safari turns it off at every launch until unsigned extensions are allowed again, and the setting falls back to Safari's own choice (inferred: Safari's settings file is protected; the behaviour and 5–6 relaunches in the log fit). No extension API reads or sets it. Fix on Tirth's side: set it back to Margin (after each relaunch while the build is unsigned; for good once Margin is signed by an Apple Developer team). Margin's side (`background/newtab-hint.js`, Safari only): tabs that open in front from nothing (no opener, not opened by Margin) on the same web page twice in a Safari run, while Margin's new-tab page doesn't show, get a 20s note on that page saying where to set it, once a run, with "Don't show again" (`settings.newTabHint`). Margin's new-tab page showing up (or a new-tab tab being created, Chrome-style) resets it. Not a new window's first tab (⌘N, windows restored at launch); an about:blank first address doesn't lose the tab. The page itself also checks it was opened from nothing (no referrer, its first history entry) and says whether it showed the note; only then is it done for the run. A bookmark opened twice in new tabs looks the same, hence twice and the off switch, which the start page's Settings also has (Safari). Unverified: what Safari reports as `openerTabId` (WebKit asks Safari's closed-source side); the switch log records each created tab's opener and index so Tirth's Safari can confirm it.
- **Review before committing** (functional checks in headless Chromium plus an adversarial review): all switching paths, the early wakes (press, palette rest, the next tab), the cap on a busy page, the log's Copy/Clear and the ⌘T hand-off timing (median 25ms vs 22ms before) checked; fixed from the review: the Chrome loading-tab wait, held keys, the keep-awake length, repeated palette wakes, the note marked done before it was shown, about:blank first addresses, new windows, the missing reset when the background hands ⌘T off, no way to turn the note back on, and the Copy button (Safari refuses a clipboard write that starts after an await: it now starts in the click with the log as a promise).

## Instant feedback while a switch waits (2026-10-26)

- **Asked (Tirth):** with the wait for the wake (up to 0.4s), nothing showed that a click had registered. Now, as Arc: the tab being switched to is highlighted at once and the page still on screen softly (`behind`, as for ⌘T), and Margin's loading pill shows if the switch is still pending after 150ms (quick switches never flash it).
- **How (`shared/switching.js`, used by `content/core.js` and the new-tab page):** sidebar clicks (tabs, open pins: the tab the background will show, `view.pinTabId`) and palette choices (`content/palette.js reportingSwitches`) start it locally, with their request; next/previous keys get it from the background (`{ type: 'switching', tabId, from }` to the tab on screen, not waited for; Margin's own new-tab page also gets the runtime broadcast, and only the page `from` names acts).
