- **The trade-off, asked first:** each page draws its own sidebar, so a switch replaces the page the press was on, and a drag started there ends with it (a drag can't carry over: its data is only for the page it started in). Switching on the press, a tab that isn't in front can't be pressed and dragged in one motion. Tirth chose switching on the press over keeping that drag (the alternatives: highlight on the press and switch on the release; or switch on the press unless a drag starts first, which loses the race to the switch most of the time).
- **Changed** (`themes/arcadia/sidebar.js`, "a press switches"): the main button of a mouse or trackpad going down on a tab's row, or on a pin whose tab is open, switches to it at once, as its click did (highlight, cover, the same request); the click after it does nothing more. Not with ⌃ (the Mac's right-click: the menu), not on a row's close button, not a touch or a pen (a press can start a scroll there). A pin not open yet still opens on click, so pressing it to drag it elsewhere doesn't open it; folders still open and close on click. A second press on a tab already on its way (a double-click) asks nothing more. A press that takes the page away (to another tab, or during a switch to one) starts no drag, whatever row the redraw has put under it (the drag would go on in a page no longer shown, with nowhere to drop). A click from the keyboard or a script (no press) still switches.
- **Still drags:** the tab in front, pins not open, folders. Another tab: press it (it comes to the front), then drag it; or its menu (Move to a space, Pin Tab). On Margin's new-tab page every row is another tab's, so tabs there move by their menu.
- **Host** (`tools/webkit-host`): a `mouse` step (a real press or release of the main button in a web view), its test hook reaching the sidebar's closed shadow root (`__shadowOf`, test copy only), scenario `press`.
- **After review** (three reviewers, each finding checked by another; 9 held up, several the same): whether a press takes the page away was read from the row's highlight, which during a switch is on the tab being switched to, not the one in front: it's the tabs' ids now; the redraw could put another row (or a folder) under the press, which then dragged: any drag after such a press is refused; a second press on an open pin sent its request again; the end-to-end test that drags a tab onto a space chip pressed a tab not in front: it sends the drag's events alone now.
- **Verified:** unit tests (307); WebKit host: `press` (a real press: the row highlighted at once, the tab in front with the button still down, the release asking nothing more; a press on the tab in front and a ⌃-press switch nowhere) and the full suite (28 scenarios) passed. Single `press` runs back to back stalled in the host's background at times, as other scenarios do (the page's half passed in every run). The end-to-end tests weren't run (not to be, for now). Not yet in Tirth's Safari: TESTING.md.

## Margin installed in Safari with one command (2026-10-29)

- **Asked (Tirth):** no installing Margin again after every Safari relaunch, and updating it without removing the old copy: a command.
- **Why it was needed:** the Xcode build was ad-hoc signed (no signing identity on this Mac), so Safari treated it as unsigned: off at every launch until "Allow Unsigned Extensions" again, its data apparently cleared too (the entries above).
- **Changed:** `npm run safari` (`scripts/safari.sh`): `npm run build`, then `xcodebuild` of `xcode/` signed with Tirth's team (automatic signing, `-allowProvisioningUpdates`; the team from `MARGIN_TEAM`, `xcode/.team`, or the first one Xcode's accounts know), installed over `~/Applications/Margin.app` (same app, same bundle IDs: Safari takes it as an update), registered (`lsregister`, `pluginkit -a`), other Xcode builds of Margin unregistered (DerivedData and the script's own build folder: one copy in Safari). The first install opens the app (its set-up window). `--unsigned` builds without a team.
- **Verified:** run once on Tirth's Mac (2026-10-29): signed with team VSNN5UW99X (Xcode created the "Apple Development" certificate), installed, the only `io.tirth.margin.Extension` registered; Xcode's DerivedData build unregistered. An unsigned build of a copy of the project also passes. Not yet seen in Safari: that it stays on across a relaunch and takes the next build as an update (TESTING.md).

## Rows press and drag as in Arc (2026-10-29)

- **Asked (Tirth, with a screen recording of Arc):** the same press animation (the tab shrinks a little), and moving tabs from one place to another with the same look.
- **Read from the recording** (frame by frame, 48 fps, measured): the press is a slight shrink while the button is down (too small to measure in the recording: under 1% of the row's width; taken as 0.97); a drag leaves the row where it is while a translucent copy follows the pointer; where it goes is a line in the space's color, 2px, with an 8px ring at its start, 8px in from the row's edge and out to the sidebar's; let go, the copy glides into the new place in about 0.3 s while the rows around it slide to theirs (about 70 ms), then turns into the row.
- **Changed** (`themes/arcadia/sidebar.js`, `styles/sidebar.css`): a press shrinks the row to 0.97 (100 ms) and lets it spring back (300 ms, a slight overshoot), kept through a redraw. Dragging is now the pointer's own events, not the browser's drag and drop: that can't animate a drop, and its picture of the row vanishes as it's dropped. Past 4px with the button down a copy of the row follows the pointer (the row stays), the drop line as in Arc, the list scrolls near its edges; let go, the copy waits for the list's new order and glides into the row's new place while the other rows slide (`element.animate`); nowhere to go, Escape, the page hidden, or no new order within 1.2 s: it glides back (a row scrolled out of view: it fades). New: a tab dragged between tabs moves there, and Safari's own tab order follows (`tabs.move`, `background/handlers.js` `moveTab`: the list shows the newest at the top, the reverse of the window's order; not a tab the browser pinned, kept apart). Pins, folders, a tab to the pinned area or a space as before. What drags is unchanged: the tab in front (others come to the front on the press), pins not open, folders.
- **After review** (three reviewers, each finding checked by another; 12 held up, all small): a drag whose release went elsewhere (another page, another app) stayed on: it ends with no button down, the page hidden or left; a drop where the pin or folder already is showed a line and then hung: it's no place to drop; a pin into a closed folder fades in; a nested pin's copy was drawn 16px off; a tab dropped on the empty pinned area's box collapsed it before the rows were measured (they jumped); the list scrolled with the pointer over the bottom bar; the drop line before the first pin was clipped; a redraw mid-drag lost the line; the copy's opacity jumped; the tab dropped next to is found by its address too (ids can change); a refused move glides back at once.
- **Verified:** unit tests (307); WebKit host, a real press, drag and release (`mouse` drag step, a `tabs.move` of the host's, its test hook reporting the button held: WebKit reads the real hardware's): `dragtab` (the row shrinks as it's pressed; a copy follows, the row stays, the line above the target; Safari's order follows; the copy glides in and is gone; the tab stays in front) and `press`; screenshots of each stage; the full suite (29 scenarios: all passed but `switching` once, whose background stopped answering, as the host's does under load; 3 of 3 alone). The end-to-end drag test now moves the tab by its menu (a drag needs the tab in front: its own page's closed sidebar there); not run. Not yet in Tirth's Safari: TESTING.md.

## `npm run safari` adds Margin's extension again until it's listed (2026-10-31)

- **What Tirth saw (2026-10-30, on the native-panel branch):** after `npm run safari`, Safari → Settings → Extensions didn't list Margin at all.
- **What was wrong:** the system dropped Margin's extension record right after `pluginkit -a` added it. The script unregisters other copies of Margin.app just before, and the system handles that late, taking this copy's extension with it. Running `pluginkit -a` again by hand brought it back.
- **Changed** (`scripts/safari.sh`): `pluginkit -a` is run again, 0.5 s apart and up to 10 times, until `pluginkit -m` lists this copy's extension; if it never does, the script warns that opening Margin.app once makes Safari see it. Brought over from the native-panel branch (`feat/native-panel`, never merged), where it was found.
- **Verified:** the shell syntax (`sh -n`); in Tirth's Safari it fixed the missing extension on that branch. Not yet run from main: TESTING.md.
- **Then (2026-10-31, after a run from `feat/closed-frame`):** Margin was gone again. The record had been listed once, ending the loop, and dropped later. The script now adds it again whenever it's gone, until it has stayed listed for 5 seconds (15 seconds at most, then the warning). `pluginkit -a` by hand brought it back and it stayed. The new loop is checked with `sh -n` only; the next `npm run safari` is its first run.

## Pages open while Safari reloads Margin are loaded again (2026-10-31)

- **What Tirth saw (2026-10-30, on the native-panel branch):** after Margin.app opened, switching to another tab showed a spinner that never went away, sidebar rows sometimes went missing, and only ⌘R brought a page back.
- **Why it applies to main too:** nothing panel-specific causes it. Safari reloads Margin whenever it's updated (`npm run safari`) and whenever the system registers Margin.app again, which it does each time the app is opened (Safari's log, 2026-10-30: `Uninstalled plugins` / `Installed plugins`, Margin's background closed and a new one started). Safari keeps Margin's content-script world in the pages that were open, still bound to the copy that's gone: its `browser` keeps the old address, and its storage calls and messages neither arrive nor fail, so the old copy's switches and covers wait for good. The new copy's content script finds the old `Margin.content` in the same world and stops (`content/core.js`), and the repair paths took the old `Margin.content` for the current one (`ensureCurrent`). Margin's new-tab page left open is at the old copy's address and answers nothing either.
- **Changed** (brought over from `feat/native-panel` without its panel code; the lease-only changes to `content/core.js` and the new-tab theme stay there):
  - `background/cut-off.js`: `cutOffInPage` runs in the page through `scripting.executeScript`, which still works there. It compares the world's `runtime.getURL('')` (or no `runtime.id`) with this copy's address, and checks whether anything is typed, chosen or playing, judged as `content/takeover.js` does. `oldOwnPageAddress` gives a tab on the old copy's new-tab page this copy's address, marked `?again`.
  - `background/tabs.js repairCutOff`: a cut-off page with nothing in use is loaded again (a GET of its address, or the browser's reload when it has a `#fragment`), the tab in front too; one after another, 150 ms apart, at most once per 30 s per tab. A page in use keeps its page; its dead sidebar, palette and covers are put out of sight, and so is the page under the old copy's arrival cover. It runs on every open tab when the open tabs are repaired (install, update, a fresh session), on each tab brought to the front (`ensureCurrent`), and before a switch to a tab that was in use or didn't answer (waited for at most 150 ms). Pages found bound to this copy aren't asked again in this run of the background. `repairOldOwnPage` sends a tab on the old new-tab page to this copy's.
  - `newtab/newtab.js`: a page opened with `?again` isn't taken for a new tab (⌘T's hand-off would close it).
  - WebKit host: the `uninstall` step's `keepWorld` holds Margin's content-script world through the unload, as Safari does. A tab's web view is made again when it moves between a web page and an extension page or between copies. Its web views ignore the window server's occlusion and App Nap is off for the run (long runs failed now and then without that). New scenarios `reloaded` and `reloaded-newtab`.
- **After review** (two reviewers, each finding checked by another; one held up): a page whose world is bound to another copy that's still running (Safari keeping an older copy beside the newer one: `shared/owner.js`, the host's `twins`) was taken for one cut off, as the probe only compared addresses. The newer copy then loaded such pages again for nothing (they'd be bound to the same copy again), and after each restart of its background, or hid that copy's sidebar on a page in use. The probe now also asks the world's `storage.local.get`: one that answers within 200 ms is another copy's, still running, and is left alone (`otherCopy`); only a world that's silent or fails is cut off. `twins` now checks that the older copy's page isn't loaded again. (It sometimes fails its existing check that one copy runs B or stands back; main does too, 3 of 10 runs, so that's not from this.)
- **Verified, off screen:** `npm run build`; `npm test` (318, all pass, 11 new: the page check run from its source alone as executeScript sends it, cut off or not, another copy still running, busy, quiet, what's done; the new-tab page's address; a GET or the browser's reload, the cooldown, a page in use loaded again before a later switch to it, a bound page not asked again). `npm run test:webkit`: 31 scenarios, all pass (after the review's fix too). `reloaded` alone: 8 of 9 runs passed; the first, right after a fresh build, had the press from the tab Safari had just loaded to the one kept with its draft not switch (no switch request in the log), and the next eight passed. The end-to-end tests weren't run.
- **Needs Tirth:** that Safari 27.2 reloads the pages as the host does: TESTING.md.

## `npm run safari` makes the Xcode project again when it's out of date (2026-10-31)

- **What Tirth saw:** back on main after the native-panel branch, `npm run safari` failed: `Build input files cannot be found: …/app/safari/panel/StateMirror.swift` and the rest of the panel's Swift files.
- **What was wrong:** `xcode/` (git-ignored) still held the project the panel branch made, which lists files main doesn't have. Main's script only made a project when there was none. And main's `make-xcode.sh` couldn't have made one with Xcode 27.2 anyway: its converter writes `project.xcproj` (Xcode's own format, settings as `"KEY": "value"`), and the script only knew `project.pbxproj`.
- **Changed:**
  - `scripts/safari.sh`: keeps a fingerprint of what made the project (`app/safari/` and `make-xcode.sh`, by content) in `xcode/.made-from`, and makes the project again when there's none or it differs, as after switching branches either way.
  - `scripts/make-xcode.sh`: finds either format, sets the bundle IDs and the macOS 14 deployment target in both, stops if the converter made no project, and keeps `xcode/.team` and `xcode/build` when it makes the project again. These format changes were brought over from the native-panel branch; its sandbox and panel settings stay there.
- **Verified:** in a scratch copy of the repo with Xcode 27.2: the project made (`project.xcproj`, bundle IDs `io.tirth.margin` and `io.tirth.margin.Extension`, the app's deployment target 14.0, `.team` kept), then built unsigned (the app with its extension; that build was unregistered and deleted after). `sh -n` on both scripts. Not yet run in place: `npm run safari` on Tirth's Mac.

## The sidebar costs less while pages scroll, update and get wheel events (2026-10-31)

- **Asked (Tirth):** make the HTML sidebar efficient: stop redrawing when nothing changed, make wheel handling light, and find why scrolling cost more with the sidebar open, without new flashes.
- **Found** (measured off screen in the WebKit host on real sites; each finding checked by a second reader):
  - **Scrolling.** WebKit draws every `position: fixed` box that shows something again on each step of a scroll that a page's script makes (smooth-scrolling sites; the benchmark's scroll). Margin's sidebar, frame and card edge were three such boxes. With Keep open, Hacker News took 541 ms of graphics work per 5 s of scrolling, against 19 ms without Margin, and memory rose from 91 to 173 MB while scrolling. With the sidebar closed, the hidden fixed overlays still cost script-side work on every step. A box positioned absolutely inside Margin's host, which is fixed but has no size, costs nothing. CSS smooth scrolling and arrow keys didn't show it. Whether a real trackpad's scrolling does wasn't settled: the host's synthetic wheel events didn't scroll the page.
  - **Redraws.** A visible page read all of storage again for every change (about 300 KB, mostly site icons), although the change event carries the new values. The sidebar then rebuilt every row and every icon. The background also wrote a tab's record again when nothing in it had changed (a page finishing loading again): mutate compared by reference. Safari and Chrome send no change event for an equal value, so pages didn't redraw for those writes, but the background still did the work.
  - **Wheel.** For each wheel event, the swipe check read every ancestor's scroll sizes before asking whether the event moved sideways at all. On a page whose layout was out of date, that forced a layout per event (0.8 to 1.8 ms an event in the benchmark). Where a pull or swipe applies was worked out for every event, even when the gesture had already been judged. The cancelable wheel listener, which makes WebKit hand each wheel event to the page's main thread first, stayed in place with the gestures setting off.
- **Changed:**
  - `themes/arcadia/styles/sidebar.css`, `content.js`: nothing in the sidebar's shadow root is `position: fixed` any more; the sidebar, frame, card edge, covers and badges are absolute inside the host. The window's size, which `inset: 0` gave them, is `--vw` and `--vh`, measured from one empty, invisible fixed box with a ResizeObserver (so classic scroll bars are left out, as before). The values are written to a style sheet of the shadow root's own; until the first measurement, and on Margin's new-tab page, the viewport's size is used. Each element keeps the stacking context a fixed box had (`z-index: 0`). Idle badges (the loading pill, pull and swipe badges, zoom readout) are now `visibility: hidden` and fade out as before. Without that, the host kept a surface the size of the window and closed mode cost 6 MB more per tab.
  - `content/core.js`, `themes/arcadia/newtab.js`: a page that's drawn takes a change from the event itself (`shared/stored.js` `withChanges`, as hidden pages already did) and draws it. It still reads all of storage before its first drawing, whenever it's shown, while another read is under way, for a removal, while the tabs are still under their old names, and after a change it took while hidden during a read (that read could have put the old value back). The new-tab page ignores the note of a tab being shown, as web pages do.
  - `themes/arcadia/sidebar.js`: rows are kept by key with the values each was drawn from, and are built again only when one of those changes; the space title and the space icons likewise. An unchanged row keeps its icon, its hover and its press. Rows drawn for a swipe's preview of the neighbouring space aren't kept. A row whose icon failed to load is built again on the next draw, so the icon is tried again, as before.
  - `background/store.js`: a key is written only when its stored value (its JSON, which is what Safari compares) changed. A real write still checks first that this copy owns the state.
  - `shared/gestures.js`, `shared/pull.js`, `content/trackpad.js`: the scroll check asks the cheap questions first (no movement that way, then the overflow style, then the sizes). The guard and the swipe check share one answer per event. Where a pull or swipe applies is asked only while it matters, at most once per event. The guard's cancelable wheel listener is there only while the gestures setting is on, and the pull and swipe watcher only while one of them is on. Both follow the setting when it changes, on hidden pages too. A trade-off: turned off and on again, they run after the page's own window wheel listeners on pages already open, until those are reloaded.
- **Measured** (WebKit host, 1440×900, a script scrolling 5 s, 3 runs each, medians; main → this branch; without Margin in brackets):
  - Hacker News, Keep open: graphics 541 → 22 ms [19], web process 911 → 498 M instructions [270], memory after idle 91 → 73 MB [43], while scrolling 173 → 73 MB.
  - Hacker News, closed: web process 588 → 405 M instructions, memory 52 → 52 MB.
  - jvns.ca, Keep open: graphics 767 → 179 ms [209], web process 1071 → 667 M instructions [416], memory 156 → 136 MB [151].
  - jvns.ca, closed: web process 767 → 587 M instructions, memory 159 → 156 MB.
  - Sidebar redraw, 40 tabs (headless WebKit): about 5 → 2 ms for a title change, and 4 → 0 ms for an update with nothing visible in it. Wheel events on a page with its layout out of date: 0.8–1.8 ms → 0.06–0.16 ms each (vertical scrolling).
- **After review** (four reviewers, each finding checked by another; five held up, then one more in a second review of the fixes): a change taken while hidden could be overwritten by a read already under way, and the in-place path never read again (now it does); the new-tab page read all of storage when the shown-tab note was removed; an icon that failed once stayed a letter; the sidebar's raised parts (resize handle, menu, dragged row) lost their stacking context and showed over the startup cover; with Reduce Motion on, the swipe badge eased instead of following the fingers.
- **Verified:** `npm test` (326, 8 new: what the wheel check asks for no movement or overflow visible, one walk per event, the listener added and removed with the setting, lazy eligibility equal to the boolean one over random gestures; changes taken from events equal to a full read over random writes, when a page reads again, equal stored values; no write for nothing new, a failed write still retried). The sidebar's DOM is identical to main's over 1,500 random updates in WebKit and Chromium, with 91% of rows kept. In the WebKit host, the rectangles of the sidebar, frame, hole, grips, card edge, covers and badges match main's in hover and Keep open at page zoom 0.75, 1, 1.5 and 2, with overlay and with always-shown scroll bars. The one change: with always-shown scroll bars, the loading pill, pull badge and zoom readout are now centred over the card (the old formula counted the scroll bar). Snapshots are pixel-identical but for faint anti-aliasing on the card's corners in one case (always-shown scroll bars at 100%: 77 pixels, at most 26/255). `npm run test:webkit`: 31 scenarios, all pass on the final build. An earlier run had one failure: `press` "the release asked for no second switch", while a review was running on the same Mac. It then passed 5 of 5 times alone, and main 3 of 3. The end-to-end tests and Chrome weren't run.
- **Needs Tirth:** a look in Safari (TESTING.md).

## The frame stays when the sidebar is hidden, and the sidebar slides away (2026-10-31)

- **Asked (Tirth):** with the sidebar closed, keep Margin's border around the page instead of a full-bleed page, stretched to the left with a little space left, so it still looks like Margin; and when the sidebar closes, it goes off to the left while the border stretches left. No more CPU or memory, no lost features.
- **Changed:**
  - `content/layout.js`: hidden, the page is inset 8px at its left and right, through the push's adopted sheet in a light form. On a page already fitted, the card stays in the frame. Hovering still moves nothing, though it's a new exception to "hover mode never pushes": SPEC.md.
  - `themes/arcadia/chrome.js`, `styles/sidebar.css`: at rest the frame is four thin strips (`.rims`), each its own small surface, each clipping a copy of the card's edge and frame. Clicks go through them to the page. The full `.frame` and `.card-edge` show only while the edge travels.
  - Turning Keep open off: the page takes its size at once and isn't laid out again; the card's edge and the sidebar slide left over 200 ms. Not on a page hidden or just shown, and not with Reduce Motion.
  - `themes/arcadia/closed.template.css`, `background/registered.js`: a first-paint frame while the sidebar is hidden, so it doesn't arrive late on every load. None where Margin works only in full screen (Chrome's default).
  - New setting `frameWhenHidden`, on by default: off is the old behaviour exactly. It's there because no other mode leaves a page untouched now.
- **Tried and dropped:**
  - The frame over the page with no inset: it cut off text at the page's left edge.
  - The Keep open push as it is, in every hidden tab: `overflow-x: hidden` on `<html>` stops sideways scrolling, and stops body's overflow from reaching the window (sticky headers, modal scroll locks). A top margin gave full-height pages a stray 8px scroll. The review found these.
  - The full frame at rest: 18 MB more per tab (Hacker News 52 → 70 MB), for a surface the size of the window.
- **Accepted:** the frame lies over the top and bottom 8px of pages, and over fixed elements at the window's edges, as with the Keep open push. With no overflow rule, a page with `100vw` sections can scroll about 16px sideways. The page jumps to its full size at the start of the slide, under the sidebar; animating it would lay it out on every frame.
- **Measured** (as in the entry above, hidden mode, without → with the frame): Hacker News memory 52 → 51 MB, web process 405 → 445 M instructions, graphics 20 → 19 M instructions. jvns.ca memory 156 → 157 MB, web process 587 → 628 M instructions, graphics 519 → 484 M instructions. So about 7–10% more script-side work during a script-driven scroll, and nothing more at rest. Pixels: the strips match the full frame exactly (0 differ, one page at 100%).
- **After review** (two reviewers, each finding checked by another): the slide was cut short by the setting's own write coming back, always with the shortcut (fixed, and `closed-frame` now goes through it); the overflow and top-margin problems above; the frame would print; Chrome ignored "Use in windows too" for the first-paint frame.
- **Verified:** `npm test` (326); `npm run test:webkit`: 32 scenarios, all pass, with a new `closed-frame` (the page inside the frame, the pointer through it, the edge on its way mid-slide, strips at rest, hover moves nothing, the setting off). Not rerun after the type declaration, which changes no code. The end-to-end tests and Chrome weren't run. Not yet in Tirth's Safari: TESTING.md.

## The sidebar comes in the way it goes out (2026-10-31)

- **Asked (Tirth):** the sidebar's radius should match the page card's. Pressing Keep open on the floating sidebar should send it off to the left and bring the docked sidebar in from the left, as closing does the other way.
- **Changed:** the floating sidebar's corners are 6.25px, the card's (they were 12px). `themes/arcadia/content.js setLayout` runs the arrival: the floating sidebar leaves (170 ms), the sidebar takes its docked shape off the left edge, then comes in with the card's edge (200 ms). `content/layout.js` hands it `place`, what moves the page: a pushed page moves as the sidebar comes in; a fitted page's card moves when the edge has arrived, or the shell's bare canvas would show beside it. The animation runs only on the click: nothing runs at rest. Not on a page hidden, just shown or drawn first, and not with Reduce Motion (it docks in place, as before).
- **Also found:** the press only took effect when the pointer left the sidebar: a hover kept it floating. `layout.settle` now docks at once.
- **After review** (two reviewers, each finding checked by another; four held up): the fitted page's strip above; Reduce Motion got the slide out and a gap; a zoom or width change during the slide was lost.
- **Verified:** `npm test` (326); `npm run test:webkit`: 32 scenarios, all pass; `closed-frame` now presses the button and checks both stages. The fitted page's order and Reduce Motion aren't covered by a scenario. Chrome and the end-to-end tests weren't run. Not yet in Tirth's Safari: TESTING.md.

## One bare tag in the page (2026-10-31)

- **Asked (Tirth):** the tags Margin adds looked bulky in the inspector, all that inline CSS and those attributes. A plain tag at the top with everything inside it, nothing lost, no extra cost.
- **Changed** (`shared/dom.js`): the page now gets `<margin-app>` under `<html>` (no box: `display: contents`, its children through a slot), with the hosts in it, both bare.
  - The hosts' styles moved from `style` (`all: initial` expands to hundreds of properties there) into a rule in their own shadow root. `!important` from inside beats the page's rules, `!important` and all, so they're as protected. Checked in WebKit and Chromium.
  - Zoom is a small sheet per host for the same reason. That also keeps a page's own `--page-zoom` out.
  - `all` from inside also beats an inline `display: none`, so hiding a host adds `data-margin-off`, which its rule respects. That's the one attribute it can get, only on a host hidden.
  - The `data-*` state was for tests and is written only under a test harness (`mark`, `__marginMarks`: the WebKit host's hook, the end-to-end helpers). The palette's open and short states, which its styles need, moved inside its shadow root.
  - `data-margin-v` went: a host in `<margin-app>` is by that a copy that stands back for itself. Copies scan `<margin-app>` and `<html>`'s children, for older copies' hosts.
- **After review** (two reviewers, each finding checked by another; found while testing too): `all` from inside wiped the inline `zoom` (the sidebar grew with the page); a page's `--page-zoom` reached the host; a host taken out of `<margin-app>` wasn't put back; a page's own `<margin-app>` could be taken for Margin's; the end-to-end palette check read a mark the new-tab page doesn't get.
- **Measured** (as in the entries above, hidden with the frame, before → after): memory unchanged (Hacker News 51 MB, jvns.ca 157 MB); web process during a script-driven scroll 445 → 455 M instructions and 628 → 632 M, graphics about the same (19 → 20 M, 484 → 484 M).
- **Verified:** `npm test` (326); `npm run test:webkit`: 33 scenarios, all pass, with a new `clean-tags` (the structure, hostile page styles, zoom, put back); element rectangles and snapshots identical to the build before at zoom 0.75 to 2; without the test hook the page has `<margin-app><margin-sidebar-host></margin-sidebar-host></margin-app>`. Chrome and the end-to-end tests weren't run (the end-to-end helpers changed too). Not yet in Tirth's Safari: TESTING.md.

## The Arcadium theme is Arcadia (2026-10-31)

- **Asked (Tirth):** rename the theme everywhere. Earlier entries here were renamed too; they say Arcadia where the theme had its old name then.
- **Changed:** the folder (`extension/themes/arcadia/`), the id, the name shown, and every mention. Saved settings carry over: `settings.theme` falls back to the default, now Arcadia, and `model.js normalizeThemes` and `theme.js settingsOf` take `settings.themes.arcadium` into `settings.themes.arcadia` (pages read storage directly, so the theme reads both until it's saved).
- **Stays under the old name, on purpose:** Safari keeps registered scripts across launches and loads none if a file one lists is missing, so simply moving the folder would have left pages without Margin after the update. `scripts/build.js LEGACY_THEME_FILES` writes empty files at the old paths into `dist/`, not `extension/`. The background replaces the registrations when it starts; a page that loads a stub meanwhile asks for the theme (`handlers.js injectTheme`). Delete them after a release or two.
- **Verified:** `npm test` (327, new: the settings carried over, the stubs in both builds and empty). `npm run test:webkit`: 32 of 33 scenarios passed; `introduce` stopped when the test host crashed. Alone it then passed 6 of 6, and on main the same crash (exit 133) hit 2 of 4 runs, so it isn't from this. The first update in Safari, with its stored registrations, can't be run off screen: TESTING.md. Chrome and the end-to-end tests weren't run.

## Pinch zoom is Safari's own again (2026-10-31)

- **Asked (Tirth):** pinch should be smooth and follow the fingers, as in Safari and Arc, with no percentage, and apart from ⌘+ zoom. Margin was stepping Safari's page zoom through fixed levels with a readout.
- **What was read** (Safari.framework and WebKit in macOS 27.2, statically; WebKit source; off-screen tests with real magnify events handed to a web view; no Safari run). This corrects the earlier entries and comments saying the rule couldn't be read and the direction wasn't known:
  - Safari opens the tab overview from one event: `-[SearchableWKView _shouldBeginVisualTabPickerTransitionForMagnification:phase:]` needs phase Began, a negative delta and the view at its minimum magnification, on an event the page left unhandled. Later events never open it.
  - Its smooth zoom also starts only from an unhandled Began, and runs in the UI process: the page gets no more events, not even `gestureend`, and can't limit it.
  - `gesturestart`'s `scale` is 1 + the first delta, so the page sees the sign Safari tests, before it decides.
  - Cancelling the synthetic ⌃+wheel after a gesture event also counts as handled, so that branch of the guard had to go.
  - Viewport meta and `touch-action` have no effect on it, and a page can't start it. Native zoom holds about 200 MB more at 3× on a heavy page, as Safari always has.
- **Changed:**
  - `shared/gestures.js`: on a page, only an inward first event at normal scale is cancelled. Everything else is Safari's, with nothing of Margin's running. On the new-tab page and over Margin's UI, nothing zooms.
  - Removed: the stepped zoom, its levels, the percentage readout, the `setZoom` message. ⌘+ / ⌘− are Safari's alone.
  - `content/shell.js`, the fitted card: Safari's route fails there. A native pinch leaves a frame's gesture state set, since WebKit resets only root frames, so every other pinch was dead, and `event.scale` can come out with the wrong sign. It would also magnify the sidebar. So the card has its own: a transform of the iframe, inside a new clip element that holds the card's place and corners. Up to 3×, with a little give and a spring at the limits, around the fingers. Two fingers pan it, then scroll the page at its edges, with pull and swipe stood down. Reset by full screen.
- **Trades:**
  - Sharpness on fitted pages: see the next entry.
  - The card's zoom follows the page's main thread: about 0.7 ms an event, 10 ms to the screen on Wikipedia and The Verge, slower on a busy page. No memory, nothing between pinches.
  - On a plain page Margin's sidebar and frame are in the page, so Safari's zoom magnifies them too.
  - A pinch out whose first movement is slightly inward does nothing, and can't be recovered.
  - A pinch that starts over an embedded frame isn't seen, so it can still open the overview, as before.
- **After review** (two reviewers, each finding checked by another; 13 held up, two of them the same in both): the clip element broke Chrome's fitted layout (its page-world writer looked for the frame directly under `body`: every page would have loaded twice and ended up unfitted; the clip now has a mark of its own, and a test ties the two together); a scroll over the sidebar or palette panned the card; gestures turned off left a zoomed card stuck; hover and hide read the pointer unscaled; a pinch ending next to 100% left swipe and pull off; a left-over transition lagged panning; a page's handlers ran as well as the card's zoom; pinching in full screen. A page's own pinch on a fitted page (a map) still loses to the card's, as it lost to the stepped zoom.
- **Verified:** `npm test` (328, new: which pinches are cancelled in each mode, three stages in the card, the wheel, the writer's selector). `npm run test:webkit`: 33 of 34 passed on the last run; `update` failed one check once ("selecting it cancels the hidden cleanup reload"), then passed 6 of 6 alone, as main did. `pinch` passed 3 of 3 alone. Real magnify events go to the host's web views (new `pinch` step, `allowsMagnification` on as in Safari): new scenario `pinch`. Not run: real Safari or a real trackpad (the predicate was read, not watched), Chrome, the end-to-end tests. Tirth to judge the feel and the fitted page's sharpness: TESTING.md.

## A zoomed fitted page is drawn sharp once the fingers stop (2026-10-31)

- **What Tirth saw:** zoomed on a fitted page, pixels showed: the transform stretches what was drawn.
- **Why nothing simpler works:** a layer's raster scale is the page's scale × a root-relative factor WebKit only uses for an experimental SVG engine (`GraphicsLayerCA`). Measured at 2.5×, every CSS route stayed at 0.40 surface pixels per device pixel against 1.00 native: `will-change`, `scale`, 3D, filters, flips of compositing, repaints.
- **Changed** (`content/shell.js`): the transform stays as the live preview. About 120 ms after the fingers stop (280 after a spring) an adopted sheet in the card lays the page out at the zoom, at the width it has, and the transform goes in the same task. `font-optical-sizing: none` holds variable fonts' widths. Two fingers are then the page's own scrolling. Fixed elements are marked (`data-margin-fixed`) and keep their size, or a bar across the page would run out of reach. A new pinch, a press in the page, a resize, full screen or leaving goes back to the transform: one layout (12–29 ms on The Verge, about 170–200 ms on a 22,000-element Wikipedia article). Safari builds only (the pinch is).
- **Measured** (off screen, on real pages, 2.5×; pixel variance, stretched → sharp, then native): Wikipedia 361 → 1581, native 1534; The Verge after the swap 1381, native 432 at the lab's smaller crop, so compare within a run only: lab transform 65 against 426 sharp against 432 native. Layers about 10 MB more. No resize events; `innerWidth` and media queries stay. Lines: The Verge 0 of 155 moved, Wikipedia 21 of 1174; all back afterwards. GitHub and MDN were looked at in the review run, those figures weren't kept.
- **Limits:**
  - After a press the page is stretched again until the next pinch. While sharp, a page's measures are × the zoom, so what it places by them would land far off (a test's tooltip landed far off).
  - Hover popups (Wikipedia's previews) will land wrong while sharp (inferred, same cause).
  - Frames inside the page (videos, tweets, maps): the box grows, the content doesn't.
  - Pages with nothing that scrolls, that are scroll locked, or with a fixed element over half the card really on top (a lightbox, an app drawn in a fixed shell) stay stretched; canvas too (inferred).
  - Fixed elements keep their size rather than growing. Those in shadow roots aren't found.
  - A page's scroll-driven code sees `scrollY` × the zoom.
  - The reading spot moves 1–5 device pixels at the swap.
- **Not chosen:**
  - `tabs.setZoom` as the commit: the cleanest for the page, but it writes Safari's saved per-site zoom (read from the binary, inferred to sync), and snaps to fixed stops.
  - Native magnification on the card through a hit area in the top document: one dead pinch after any pointer move, and the sidebar can't follow it live.
- **After review** (two reviewers, each finding checked by another; 25 held up, several found by both, and the first version of this had none of the fixes):
  - Going back near the page's end jumped the view, and the press then missed: what the page's end won't hold is now the transform's (`shared/shell-logic.js`, with a test).
  - A second pinch zoomed around the wrong point.
  - A body that scrolls for the window lost the reading spot for good.
  - Right-to-left pages landed on the wrong side and went blank.
  - Percent heights and elements placed against the page's edges kept the unzoomed size: `html` has a box of its own now.
  - Fixed elements inside fixed ones shrank twice; marks were a snapshot, so they follow the page now (a mutation observer and `scrollend`, while sharp only).
  - A press in the delay didn't stop the swap. A page replacing its own sheets left state wrong. Printing took the zoom (the sheet is `@media screen`). A page's own `html` zoom was replaced. Classic scroll bars gave the wrong width. A wide page lost its own sideways scroll.
- **Verified:** `npm test` (329, new: the scroll there and back). `npm run test:webkit`: 34 of 35 passed; `introduce` stopped with the test host's crash, which also hits main. New `pinch-fitted`, 3 of 3 alone, splits from `pinch` (the host's background stops after about 5 s, so Keep open has to go first): sharp at the same width, lines as they were, a fixed bar's size, the page as it was after, a press, what's pressed staying under the pointer at the page's end (within 1 px), a body that scrolls for the window, back to the same spot. Final build on saved real pages with real magnify events: Wikipedia 361 → 1602 (native 1534), pinched back to exactly where it was (scrollY 20000), The Verge likewise (3000). Not run: real Safari or a trackpad, Chrome (the pinch is Safari builds only), the end-to-end tests. Tirth to judge how the swap feels: TESTING.md.
- **Then (Tirth, in Safari, on a Google page with sources beside the answer):** still stretched. The build was the right one. Google's AI Mode needs a login, so it couldn't be opened off screen, and logged-out results went sharp. Two guards were too wide for such a page: any fixed box over half the card counted as a lightbox, though sites keep invisible full-window holders for dialogs (now only one really on top at the card's centre), and a page with an inner pane that scrolls counted as having nothing to read (now it goes sharp). Checked by `pinch-fitted` on fixtures of those shapes, not on the real page.
- **Verified then:** `npm test` (329); `npm run test:webkit`: 34 of 35, `unloaded` failing "D: the sidebar was carried open". That one was looked at as a possible regression (4 of 14 here, 0 of 14 on main, run in turns), but in a longer run main failed it 3 of 16 and the commit before the sharp stage 2 of 16: it's intermittent, already there, and still unexplained. A sidebar carried open onto a tab Margin loaded behind sometimes isn't open; worth its own look.

## One layout: the page's box, no iframe (2026-11-01)

Tirth tried the frameless test build in real Safari, found most pages right, and approved it for every page, in both browsers, Keep open and hidden alike. It replaces the fitted iframe layout and the push. Decisions before this one about the shell, the card, the rail and sharp zoom are history.

- **One layout, with values.** Keep open: the box at the sidebar's width. Hidden with the frame: at 8. No box means the page is untouched (the frame off, or Chrome outside full screen). So toggling is live, with no reload.
- **Registered, not in the manifest.** The page-world bundle (`content/page.bundle.js`, one file replacing four) runs only while pages have a box, top frame only. `at-<L>.js` before it says where to start, because a registered script can't be given a value. Width changes re-register, and WebKit reruns scripts in open pages, so the bundle guards against running twice. A page that missed it asks `injectPage`.
- **The bridge is a synchronous `margin:box` event** with a JSON string. A cancelled event means the page's world heard it, which is how the isolated side knows the script is there. No attributes on `<html>`. Proved on the real engine: strings cross worlds, and the event arrives synchronously. Keep this protocol stable: an updated Margin talks to an older copy in open pages.
- **Page locks are read from computed style.** A sheet taken off to look costs a layout (69 ms on Reddit). html's and body's overflow rules have no weight (`:where`), so what a page says shows. A page's own `body { overflow: visible }`, even `!important`, beats that, which left the Washington Post with nothing scrolling: then the rule is said again with weight. A body lock added later on such a page can't be read; that's the price.
- **`overflow-x` is `auto`, unless the page says otherwise**, where the test build hid it. A wide page scrolls sideways as it does in a window.
- **body's margin becomes padding only if it has one.** The test build wrote padding on every page, with `!important`, so a class adding padding later did nothing.
- **Reddit's header** was a bug in the rewrite: a loop over the live style while setting it, and WebKit moves a set property to the end, so `calc(50vw - 50%)` rules had some properties rewritten twice and others never. Now over a copy, and a value already rewritten stays.
- **Teams with the sidebar off**: the test build still used the light push when hidden. Hidden now has the box. Signed-out pages match in the lab; signed in needs Tirth.
- **Sheets a page can't read** (about one in ten, 16 of 22 on Substack): the background fetches them with no cookies, a plain `<style>` goes after the link, and the page's world rewrites it. The test build's `crossorigin` copy fetched twice and failed without CORS headers.
- **Paging keys stay a handler.** `tabindex=-1` on body works only after a click (WebKit and Chromium, headless).
- **Wheel with `overflow: clip` on html** scrolls body (WebKit and Chromium, headless). The risk the plan flagged didn't show.
- **The pinch is Margin's on any page with a box.** A transform on `<html>`, with the opposite on the sidebar's host, frame by frame; `will-change` only while fingers move, so WebKit should draw it sharp at rest. Not provable off screen: Tirth to compare. This replaces laying the page out at the zoom, and with it that mode's limits, and fixed bars now scale with the page. The spring runs in script rather than a CSS transition, to keep page and host in step.
- **No per-site off switch.** The fit settings, the footer switch, the palette actions and the never-fit list went with the iframe. Tirth should say if he wants an escape hatch; it would be one setting that sends `off`.
- **Stored settings stay** (`fitPages` and the like are ignored). Every path ever registered stays in `dist/` as an empty stub (`LEGACY_LAYOUT_FILES`), and the old registration ids are dropped on every sync.
- **Hidden tabs:** the observer's passes wait until the tab is shown; DOMContentLoaded and load always run, so a tab prepared behind is ready as it comes to the front.

Measured off screen, in the extension engine (two rounds each, against main and the test build): scrolling costs the same, within the noise (Hacker News: 311 ms of page CPU against 345 on main; graphics 18 ms against 18; jvns.ca 400 against 348, its range overlapping). The page-world script is one 25 KB bundle where main loaded 28 KB plus 2 KB. The cost is memory: Hacker News with the sidebar hidden takes 75 MB against 53 on main, and with Keep open 110 against 72, the same as the test build, because the page now has its own scroller and layers. Pages with no box pay none of this.

## The box after review (2026-11-01)

Reviewers tried the box against fixtures and real sites (each finding reproduced by another). What came of it:

- **`<body>` keeps what the page says about its size.** Width, max-width, box-sizing and heights are in a sheet of their own (`fitSheet`), switched off with the padding's to read the page's values, computed (`computedStyleMap`, no layout) and in pixels only for a body that needs them. Margins and what a fixed width leaves over become padding, so `<body>` still fills the box and scrolls, with its scrollbar at the box's edge. Measured again after a drag rests and on a window resize, only for a body that depends on the width. The placeholders no longer say anything about `<body>`, or they'd be in the way of that read.
- **A body that grows stays `height: auto`**, held to the box by min and max, so a child's percent height is auto as in a window; as a flex column its children don't shrink. Fixed by reading the computed height, not by guessing from content. Not chosen: a Margin-owned scroller around `<body>`, for what it would do to `html > body` selectors and frameworks that hydrate the whole document. The price: an absolute footer at `bottom: 0` of such a body lands at the end of the first screen, and a stretched flex row keeps the box's height.
- **Body scroll locks with `position: fixed`:** the box's rule has `inset: auto`, so `top: -Ypx` can't move the scroller, and an inline `position: fixed` or negative `top` locks `<body>` where it is. A class setting `position: fixed` with no inline top isn't seen: reading it needs a sheet off on every class change.
- **IntersectionObserver is a stand-in** (`content/page/in-view.js`) holding two of the browser's: rooted at `<body>` for the page's margin, at the window for what's fixed. The answer is per target at each delivery, and only changes go to the page. The body-rooted one follows `<body>` swaps and exists only with a box, so nothing runs away when the box goes off. Entries are translated through the same `toPage` as rectangles.
- **Dialogs and popovers are placed one by one** (`content/page/top-layer.js`), not by a rule. `computedStyleMap` gives `auto` and percentages, which is what says how each is anchored. The move goes in `translate`, composed with the page's, so left and top a script writes later still land. This is an inline style on an open element, the one exception to adopted sheets (CSSOM passes a strict CSP), put back on close. A dialog stretched with both sides set and `width: auto` keeps the window's width.
- **Full screen is read live** (`box.boxed()`, `seenW`, `seenH`), since a player's resize handler can run before `fullscreenchange`.
- **Keys:** a pane that was pressed and can still move keeps them; Shift and an arrow is the selection's; ⌘↑↓ and ⌥↑↓ are handled, since WebKit gives them to the root. Tried and not chosen: focusing `<body>`, which needs an attribute the page sees and disturbs its focus logic.
- **Places are per history entry** (`navigation.currentEntry.key`), not per address, with nothing added to the page's history state. Saved on `navigate`, because by `popstate` the entry has changed.
- **Fetched sheets follow their link.** The link is held off with `media="not all"`, not `disabled`, which stays the page's to set. A page removing the link or changing its address or media drops the copy. The background refuses a local network's sheet unless the page is on one, after redirects too, and anything not `text/css`: the page can read the text. A hostname check can't stop DNS rebinding. Cross-origin redirects stay allowed (CDNs use them), checked at their end. Limit raised to 8 million characters, with 8 million cached in all. Reddit's layout depends on this fetch; how long a wrong breakpoint shows on a cold start wasn't measured.
- **Hidden tabs:** added subtrees aren't kept, only which root to look through once on show. Inline `vw`/`vh` a script sets are done in the observer callback, before paint. Sheets with text that has no units skip serializing rules. With 62,000 rules, time to the first frame after load: WebKit 410 ms (no box 167, was 484), Chromium 138 (no box 126, was 227).
- **Page zoom:** the hosts' and the probe's shifts are multiplied by `--page-zoom`, because the host's own zoom scales them.
- **Pages an old copy laid out** (`data-margin-shell`, `data-margin-frameless`) stay out of the page's script and the placeholder, and are offered a reload.
- **Chrome's full-screen-only mode has no placeholder at all:** CSS can't tell windows apart.
- **The `unloaded` scenario's intermittent failure** (entry above) was the host's background stopping about 5 s after it starts: the switch began at 5.3 to 5.9 s. Started sooner, it passed 6 of 6.
- **Verified:** `npm test`, `npm run test:webkit` (new `body-size`, `in-view`, `answers`, `keys`, `history`, `top-layer`, `linked-sheet`, `update-old-layout`, more checks in `lock` and `zoom`). Fixtures of each finding in headless WebKit and Chromium with the real page bundle, full screen included. Not run: real Safari, a trackpad, signed-in sites, the end-to-end tests, Chrome as an extension.

## Reading `<body>` again after `load` (2026-11-01)

Tirth's Outlook showed 8 px gaps at the left, top and right and a large one at the bottom once mail had loaded. Margin read what a page says about `<body>` at its arrival, DOMContentLoaded and `load`, and Outlook's loader adds its sheets after that: the browser's default margin stayed as padding, and "no height" stayed too, which kept the app's percent heights auto. Replaying Outlook's real sheets after `load` gave the same picture and, with this change, a shell that fills the box. Signed-in Outlook wasn't run.

- **Read again when styles change:** new or removed sheets, a link or a `<style>`'s `@import` loading, a script's rule edits (`rule.style`, `rule.styleMap`, `selectorText`), `document.adoptedStyleSheets` assigned or edited in place (`push`, `splice`), `media` on a sheet, `mediaText`, `appendMedium` and `deleteMedium`, `disabled` on a sheet or on a `<style>` or `<link>`, the text of a `<style>`, `addRule` and `selectorText` (a `:has()` in either is noted too), any attribute that really changed on `<html>` or `<body>`, the first children, and `:has()` answers. Also when the box rests, on a window resize and in and out of full screen: a media query or `vw` can make a body that isn't `sized` read differently. Before a paint, and only once a hidden tab is shown.
- **Not by asking for computed margins.** The first version of this fix did, and WebKit answers `margin-*`, `width` and `height` with a full layout, in `getComputedStyle` and `computedStyleMap` alike, even for a margin in px (timed: 66 ms on 20,000 rows against 0 for `color`).
- **`<body>` itself, style only.** `::backdrop` has no box, so a rule that makes it inherit `<body>`'s margins, padding and sizes reads back what the page wrote with no layout. It's the real `<body>`, so `body:has(...)` and `head + body` count, and the stand-in `<body>` of the first version is gone.
- **Arithmetic for the numbers:** the CSS width equation on the box's width. `calc()`, `min()`, `max()` and `clamp()` go to the browser's `CSSNumericValue`. `zoom` on `<body>` is worked in its own pixels, and `zoom` on `<html>` divides the box's own rules. Negative margins stay margins, since padding can't be negative.
- **An element for the length of a read, after `<body>`.** Margin's rules come off with a selector on `<html>`'s children: `body:nth-last-child(1 of body:first-of-type, margin-read)` holds until a `<margin-read>` follows `<body>`, and `<margin-hold>` does the same for holding it still. Both are put in and taken out in the same task, so nothing a page keeps, hydrates or serializes has them in it. On 20,000 rows and 3,000 rules a read took WebKit 4 ms and Chromium 7.2 (`sheet.disabled`: 52 and 16.4, because WebKit rebuilds every rule of the page), the same as the custom state below.
- **The custom state this replaced.** The first version kept a `<margin-read>` first in `<html>` for good and flipped a `:state()` on it, so no observer saw a read. Being there at all was the trouble, and every one of these was reproduced: React 18's `hydrateRoot(document)` failed (error 418) and threw the server DOM away, typed text included; a new `<html>` had no flag, so `<body>`'s rules stopped matching; a document serialized and parsed back lost `<head>`; `head:first-child + body` and `:nth-child` on `<html>`'s children stopped holding; a patched `customElements.define` left a half-built box that couldn't scroll; `:state()` set a floor nothing declared; and any page could read the class. All of that is gone with no guard added: no `define`, no `attachInternals`, nothing to re-insert, and on pages Margin refuses no element at all.
- **What it costs instead:** a page that watches `<html>`'s children gets one observer callback for a read, and one for a reading already known (`<margin-hold>` only). The element is gone before the callback runs. An observer that answers those callbacks by writing a new value to `<html>` or `<body>` would make reads run on, at the 1 s cap; none of the fixtures do. See the costs below.
- **A new `<html>` is followed.** `document.write`, `replaceChild` and a framework rendering the whole document all leave the rules matching, since they go by selector. The document's own observer notices the new root, and the box re-observes it and reads, so what comes after it counts (main keeps watching the old one).
- **Not `:has()`.** `html:has(> margin-read:state(read))` was tried first and looked free, because the probe's loop styled the list and not a new child. In Chromium any live `:has()` took a prepend from 0.35 to 1.3 ms, 2.4 in the real rules, and a flip from 0.2 to 1.2 ms. A first bisect blamed an observer; that variant had a syntax error, so it was simply running no Margin.
- **Held still, harder.** Five ids of weight on the hold, because `html body#b { transition: all !important }` outweighed it; `::before` and `::after` with it; new padding laid out before the hold ends (let go first, WebKit keeps children's old width), and only if something changed.
- **What inherits from `<body>`.** Chromium styles the whole page for a read, so a child with `padding: inherit` and a transition starts one. Holding every element costs a whole-page restyle, so it's only turned on for a page where a read was seen to do this: 8 events on the first read, then 0 (one sample, 4 reads).
- **`min-height` and `max-height` stay through a read.** They aren't read, and with them `<body>` keeps the box's height if a browser lays out mid-read, which a size container directly under `<body>` makes it do. That lost the scroll position; now it stays at 3000 in every shape reviewers tried, and at the very bottom of a page with margins or a centred column. A smooth scroll on a page that needs the layout also finishes (6000 in both engines; cabe711 190 in Chromium). The scroll isn't touched unless it moved.
- **A length on its way isn't believed.** A read that finds a finite transition or animation running on a length is dropped and made again when it ends, so the page's slide plays out and lands at the right value (drawer: 300 in both engines, was 143 and 137). That covers `<html>`, `<body>` and the first children, and what lengths move with: `font-*` under `em`, a registered variable, `zoom`. A first child's `margin-top`, 1em under a font-size transition and a registered variable behind `margin-left` all land where a window puts them (60, 40, 90; main 68, 16, 0).
- **Top margins read properly:** the rule on `::backdrop` names no sibling (`:not(dialog, [popover], :fullscreen)`), so it costs no invalidation and stays off a closing dialog's real backdrop (0px while closing, was 90px). The margin is followed down through first children, up to 8 of 32 looked at, and added up as CSS collapses it; text ahead of a child at any depth, layout containment, a border, and a flex or flow-root `<body>` stop it, and out-of-flow children are stepped over. Stepped-over children are watched like the rest, so a banner unhidden later counts. The whole walk is kept, so a `<script>` or a fixed header first in `<body>` doesn't count as a change on every DOM mutation: 100 unrelated changes, 0 frames asked for and 0 `getComputedStyle`, as main. Text appearing ahead of a child later isn't watched.
- **Readings known by attributes.** A reading is kept per value of `<html>`'s and `<body>`'s attributes, up to 16, and forgotten when anything else changes. A class that comes and goes costs two reads and then nothing, and doesn't lengthen the back-off, so a real change during it is read at once. A reading carries `<html>`'s `zoom` too, and a known reading counts as the last read: `zoom` from a class holds on every toggle (Z in `read-shape`, 4 toggles).
- **Reads that find nothing back off,** from 200 ms to the 1 s cap. Anything that isn't an attribute resets that and cancels the wait: a new rule during class churn applied in 9 ms in WebKit and 17 in Chromium (one sample), was about 920.
- **`:has()` asked directly.** Selectors with `:has()` whose subject can be `<html>` or `<body>` are collected from the rules as they're seen. Only on such a page, an observer asks those selectors once a frame after a DOM change, and a read follows only if an answer changed.
- **Rules are judged together.** After a script's turn, in one selector, so 5,000 rules cost one match, and what the script did to the page in that turn counts.
- **A read queued before another one ran is dropped,** and a plain page reads at parsing's end and `load` only if something changed since the last read.
- **Costs on the final code,** headless on an M1 Pro, 20,000 rows, fresh browser per run, main / cabe711 / now. A stray review browser spun one core throughout, on all three alike.
  - Load, median of 5, CPU in s. WebKit plain 0.94 / 1.55 / 0.65, nested 0.64 / 0.78 / 0.55, 2,000 rules 0.97 / 1.58 / 0.66, `min()` 1.29 / 1.67 / 0.64, `fit-content` 1.28 / 1.70 / 1.14. Chromium plain 0.35 / 0.38 / 0.33, nested 0.32 / 0.32 / 0.30, 2,000 rules 0.34 / 0.37 / 0.35, `min()` 0.35 / 0.43 / 0.32, `fit-content` 0.53 / 0.57 / 0.58 (a later run of those three 0.56 and 0.55 for cabe711 and now).
  - Load, RSS in MB. WebKit 254 to 265 on main and 256 to 265 now, level. Chromium plain 209 / 213 / 213, nested 209 / 209 / 210, 2,000 rules 212 / 213 / 215, `min()` 213 / 224 / 213.
  - Class on `<body>` toggled every 100 ms for 6 s, median of 3, CPU in s: WebKit plain 0.13 / 0.55 / 0.20, nested 0.15 / 0.19 / 0.15. Chromium plain 0.04 / 0.09 / 0.09, nested 0.04 / 0.06 / 0.05, tasks 24 / 61 / 72 ms and 24 / 40 / 34. Two reads, then none.
  - Idle: 0 to 0.01 s, the same as main. A first child's style written every frame for 6 s, a shape now watched like the rest: WebKit 2.45 / 2.39 / 2.50, Chromium 1.53 / 1.54 / 1.53, within the spread.
  - Observers: a page watching the whole document, 6 s. Idle 0 / 0 / 0 callbacks. Class toggled every 100 ms, Chromium 60 / 90 / 62, WebKit 57 / 86 / 59. A new attribute value every 100 ms, Chromium 60 / 90 / 67, WebKit 57 / 86 / 64. 
  - Prepend and remove with style forced, median of 5, ms, main / now: WebKit in `<body>` 0.905 / 0.908, in `<main>` 0.89 / 0.90. Chromium 0.345 / 0.35, 0.338 / 0.348. The selector costs DOM changes nothing.
  - µs per call, main / now, median of 5: `insertRule` of unique rules WebKit 1.8 / 2.0, Chromium 1.32 / 2.16 (spreads 1.28 to 2 and 1.56 to 2.24); complex 2.6 / 2.6, 2.1 / 2.32. With the judging after a script's turn: unique 2.8 / 3.6 and 1.58 / 3.3, complex 4.6 / 6.8 and 2.72 / 5.18. `rule.style` over 5,000 rules with its judging 0.2 / 1.6, 0.12 / 1.52; one rule repeatedly 0.03 / 0.07 and 0.03 / 0.06. A full restyle 63 / 65 ms and 14.2 / 14.2.
  - One flip and its style, a probe on 20,000 rows and 3,000 rules, not the bundle, median of 31: WebKit 4 ms plus 68 of layout after it with the rows directly in `<body>`, 0 and 5 under one wrapper. Chromium 7.2 plus 4.4, and 0 and 0. The layout is the page's own width changing and back; the custom state took the same.
- **Measured again, by a separate harness, alone on the machine, after the code was final.** Main / cabe711 / now, median of 5, 20,000 rows. The load, RSS, prepend and observer figures above held. The churn figures ran higher, because this harness counts the page's own toggling and frames, so use these:
  - Class toggled every 100 ms for 6 s, CPU in s: WebKit plain 0.48 / 0.74 / 0.40, nested 0.33 / 0.45 / 0.36. Chromium plain 0.13 / 0.32 / 0.20, nested 0.14 / 0.23 / 0.16. 2 reads.
  - A new `data-*` value every 100 ms for 6 s: WebKit plain 0.04 / 0.70 / 0.54, nested 0.04 / 0.39 / 0.47. Chromium plain 0.02 / 0.32 / 0.36, nested 0.02 / 0.23 / 0.21. 7 reads, so 3 to 9% of a core while it lasts, about twice the figure below.
  - After a script's turn, with the judging, µs per call: `rule.style` walk WebKit 0.2 / 2.2, Chromium 0.38 / 2.46, and 0.08 / 5.96 on a page with 20,000 rows. `insertRule` unique in Chromium 2.48 / 4.04.
- **A read on show.** A tab shown with a read owed makes that read before its first content commit in 12 of 98 host runs, with 42 layers in that commit against 39 (main 0 of 100). Left in: waiting a frame would show stale padding. `unloadedheavy`, alternating with main's build: 1 of 40 failed against 0 of 40 (p = 1.0), pooled with patched timing builds 4 of 100 against 0 of 100 (p = 0.12). Not shown to be a regression, not cleared either.
- **What's left, plainly:**
  - **An attribute with a new value every time is still a cost.** A `data-*` on `<body>` set to a new value every 100 ms for 6 s, main / cabe711 / now: WebKit plain 0.01 / 0.53 / 0.27, nested 0.01 / 0.18 / 0.21. Chromium 0 / 0.09 / 0.19, 0 / 0.07 / 0.07. That's 7 reads, so 2 to 5% of a core on this size of page, while it lasts. Any attribute can select `<body>`'s margins, and no reading can be reused for a value never seen. Reading less often means a real change waits longer than the 1 s cap: that's a choice for Tirth, not a bug fix.
  - **A `fit-content` page in Chromium holds more until a GC runs.** RSS at 1.5 s, 7.5 s and 19.5 s after load, MB: main 296 / 294 / 294, cabe711 330 / 328 / 311, the previous tree 333 / 331 / 330, now 372 / 369 / 344. After a forced GC it's garbage, not retained: main 164, cabe711 172, previous 175, now 167 (median of 5). The width follows the content, so the layouts can't be skipped.
  - **A page watching `<html>`'s children sees reads,** as above. If it answers each one by writing a new value to `<html>` or `<body>`, reads run on at the 1 s cap. Not measured on a real site.
  - **A page guard that removes unknown children of `<html>`** still fights Margin's sidebar host, as it does on main (`core.js` puts it back with no limit): 20,000 removals in 1,636 ms against main's 1,624 in Chromium. The flags add nothing to that: they're gone before the guard runs.
  - **The floor is lower, not declared.** `:nth-last-child(of)` is old (Chrome 111, Safari 9, from memory of compat tables). A read needs `::backdrop` to inherit (about Chrome 122, Safari 17.4, also from memory). No old engine was run; with those rules stripped from the bundle in current engines, a read falls back to a layout and every fixture but one matches main, with no errors. The exception is a first child's margin collapsing with `<body>`'s, 48 against 40. Same result with `CSSNumericValue` gone.
  - **A `style` object kept and written to more than a read later isn't seen.** Catching it means wrapping every style setter, which inline styles would pay for. Fidelity, not speed.
  - **`position: absolute` on `<body>`** keeps its left and top at 0 (real 30). Reading them means lifting `position`, and a scroll lock (`fixed`, `top: -Ypx`) would then read as an offset, moving the page. Left alone on purpose.
  - An animation with no end on a length of `<body>` reads what shows at that moment, as main does. A transition held by an inline `!important` can't be outweighed. `body:hover` and the like change with nothing to watch.
  - A script's wrappers on `MediaList` still show as such in `toString`, as `insertRule`'s always have.
  - 8 transition events on the first read in Chromium on a page with `padding: inherit` and a transition, as above.
  - `WebKit` drops the first `rule.styleMap.set` on a rule, with no Margin in the page; nothing to do with this.
  - The `r` column of the reviewers' fidelity script reads `clientWidth`, which Margin answers for `<body>`, so `zoom`, a negative margin and side borders show a right gap that isn't real. Left and width match a real window, and `read-limits` checks those.
  - Window resize across a query was run in Playwright, not in the host.
  - `unloadedheavy` fails now and then: "H: its content there as it's shown" and the three checks after it. A race in the sleep-and-activate steps shows in the log, and the cause wasn't found. On this tree, once in a full run, then 29 of 30 alone. On main's build 30 of 30. Too few to say whether they differ.
  - `behindfocus` and `introduce` sometimes end with no result: the test host itself crashes in its run-loop observer (`MainActor.assumeIsolated`, EXC_BAD_ACCESS or a trap), before any check fails. `introduce` run 8 times on each: main 2, this tree 1. Crash reports go back to 2026-10-30. Host's bug, not fixed here.
  - Hydration, checked with real React 18 and 19 (hydration and client-render recovery, typed text, clicks), plain `hydrateRoot` only: not Remix or Next. Page guards, `define` and root replacement in headless Chromium and WebKit, level with main.
  - Not measured: Safari or Chrome themselves, any real site, GPU.
- **Better on the way:** a centred, scrolled page no longer goes to the top when the sidebar's width changes, and a pinch no longer records its own clip as the page's scroll lock.

## Switching as fast as Safari: shown at first paint, fewer dark frames (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching should be as fast as plain Safari, with no flicker. Some switches took 2–3 s.
- **Found** (his Safari's log, 00:32–00:52 on this build, read with `/usr/bin/log`; the code; the WebKit host):
  - The 2–3 s was Margin's own wait. A tab Safari hadn't loaded (every tab after a relaunch, and tabs Safari dropped to save memory) was loaded behind the page and shown once its load event fired, at most 2.5 s. 11 such switches took 1.56–4.73 s (median 3.68 s) from the first selection to the show. The page had drawn something a median 0.55 s in, so Margin held back a median 2.3 s of page already painted, and most were shown still loading anyway. Plain Safari in the same log showed such tabs at their first layout, 281–1331 ms (median 600).
  - The same wait in two more places: a tab shown without loading it behind first kept the switch's cover over itself until its load (at most 2.5 s; one page painted at 130 ms and loaded at 2334 ms), and a new tab from ⌘T, the palette or a search waited for its load, up to 5 s.
  - From an introduction to the show took a median 370 ms, not the 60 ms meant: the wake, the arrival cover and the preparation went one after another on a page busy loading.
  - Dark frames: 14 of 27 shows that could be measured had more than a frame of Safari's empty view (31–372 ms). Four were the first return to a tab WebKit never saw come into the window with a process (the tab in front at a launch, one Safari loaded as it was shown): WebKit only waits for a tab's paint if a change it sent to a running process saw the view enter the window. Two went out before the introduction's paint had landed (the fixed 60 ms). Two were tabs never shown that weren't introduced, most likely because their wake timed out on a busy page. Four ran past WebKit's 250 ms hold on a slow first commit (not fully in an extension's reach).
- **Changed:**
  - **Shown once painted** (`shared/page-ready.js` `readyInPage`, run in the tab with `scripting.executeScript`): a page counts as ready once its document is parsed, it has a first contentful paint, and something of its own is drawn in view (text, a loaded image, an svg, a canvas, a video or a field inside `<body>`, laid out in the window, not hidden by its styles), or once it has loaded. WebKit records that paint for a page loading hidden (about 180 ms in the host). The background asks again every 50 ms until the question reaches the new document (before that it finds no page, an error, or `about:blank`), and polls the tab's status beside it. Only in Safari (`platform.hiddenPaints`): Chrome records no paint for a hidden page, and there a tab loading behind still waits for its load.
  - A tab restored behind, or a never-shown tab still loading, is shown once painted, at most 1.5 s after its load started (a page that draws nothing by then is shown as far as it got, as Safari would). The outer limit is 2 s, was 2.5.
  - ⌘T, the palette and searches: the new tab comes to the front once painted. The 5 s limit stays for a page that never paints, and leaving, closing and the page in front navigating still call it off. A tab shown painted but still loading is introduced too (before, only a loaded one was).
  - A tab shown without loading it behind: the cover lifts once the page has drawn something of its own or loaded, at most 1.5 s (`content/core.js` `coverWhileLoading`). Never before that, except at the limit.
  - **No fixed wait after an introduction** (`background/tabs.js` `paintBarrierInPage`): a script in the tab waits for two rendering updates (a ResizeObserver on the root, then a second one started in a task of its own after the first's callback; nothing added to the page), at most 400 ms. One isn't enough: the answer can leave the page before the commit of the update it ran in. In the host, shown after one, a never-shown tab showed the empty view for 23–30 ms in 4 of 6 runs; after two, 1–2 ms in 18 of 18. For a restored, loading or never-shown tab, this barrier is also the last thing before the show, run again if something else finished after it, so the show goes out in a gap after the page drew.
  - For a tab that waited, its fresh wake and its arrival cover are asked for together (the cover answering proves the page is there), and its preparation gets 500 ms, not 900. One that misses it is shown unprepared: its page has said hello and draws its own tab.
  - A never-shown tab whose wake timed out is introduced too. Only "no page" means no page.
  - **A page Safari made from the tab's saved history, or the first of a tab opened in front, that started visible and hasn't come into view from hidden since** (`Margin.content.windowSeen()`) says so to the wake (`unseen`), and its tab is introduced first, once until it's shown. In Safari's log, all four such dark returns were tabs Safari restored with no process yet (`restoreFromSessionState`, then `goToBackForwardItem`, `PID=0`), so their page's navigation is `back_forward`. It needs no arrival cover, but it's prepared after the introduction, the early preparation called off (that instant on screen could take it). A copy of Margin that came into a page after its start (an install or update) doesn't know how the page started and counts it as seen, as a page with an earlier copy is.
  - The switch log says `painted` in `restored` and `loadWait` when a wait ended at the paint, what a new tab's wait ended on (`ready`), `unseen: true` when an introduction was for that, and each barrier's answer and time (`barrier`: `drawn`, or `timeout` at its limit).
- **Deviations from the plan, with the evidence:**
  - Two rendering updates in the barrier, not one (the host numbers above).
  - `windowSeen` is false only for a page whose Margin started with the document. Counting every visible page made pages open at install introduced on their first switch: about 70 ms more and 3–5 ms of late content, where the old build had 1 ms (host harness, 6 runs).
  - The early preparation is waited for up to 150 ms before it's called off for an unseen tab, so its cancel can't arrive before it (a unit test caught the order).
  - A new tab from openBehind counts as having waited, so it's introduced while still loading.
  - Chrome: `hiddenPaints` is false (no paint recorded for a hidden page), and a new tab there waits for its load as before. Its switches don't wake, restore, introduce or prepare.
- **Measured** (WebKit host, the switch-path harness, n = 6 each, medians, HEAD 0973822 / this change before the review's fixes, from the click; its pages draw their own text at once, so the review's content check doesn't change them):
  - Unloaded tab that paints at once and loads at 3 s: real show 2910 / 294 ms, cover gone 3054 / 422 ms. Its content there as it's shown 2 / 1 ms.
  - Unloaded slow page (parsed at 1.5 s): 1997 / 1838 ms.
  - Unloaded fast page (loads at about 145 ms): 252 / 292 ms. 40 ms slower: the barrier now waits for two rendering updates of a page still starting.
  - Never-shown tab: 284 / 68 ms, cover lifted 428 / 207 ms, content 2 / 1 ms.
  - Loaded tab: 10 / 10 ms. A busy page (main thread blocked 280 ms): 278 / 280 ms.
  - Host scenarios: `unloadedlate` shown in under 1 s from the click, content 1 ms (HEAD: shown after its load, about 3 s); `loadcover` cover lifted at first paint (HEAD: still covered 1.5 s after the show); `behindlate` in front still loading, under 1 s (HEAD: after its load); `unseen` content 1–2 ms on its first return (HEAD: 6, 11, 14 ms).
- **Not changed:** keep-ready (another slice), a tab Safari's own tab bar shows first, the 250 ms hold on a slow first commit.
- **Risks** (to check in Safari: TESTING.md):
  - Pages come up still loading, as in plain Safari: images arrive after the show. An app whose first contentful paint is a loading screen shows that screen.
  - The question "has it painted?" is asked every 50 ms until the page commits: a restore can send a few dozen failing scripts (each a line in Safari's log), and a question waiting in the page holds its process in front for up to 1.5 s (5 s for a new tab).
  - A tab whose page came back in front with Back, or a tab opened in front whose page had a process already, is introduced once on its return when it needn't be: one flip and the barrier, about 70 ms in the host. If Safari makes a page with no process some way other than from saved history or in a new tab, that tab's first return can still show the empty view.
  - A page whose content is all in an iframe, or only a CSS background image, isn't seen as drawn: it waits for its load or the limit.
  - A never-shown tab whose wake timed out because Safari was slow (not the page) is introduced while it may have no page; that loads it as it's flipped, the same as showing it.
  - The barrier's evidence is the host's. Safari's own timing between a page's reply and its commit isn't measured.
- **After review** (two reviewers, each finding checked in the WebKit host first):
  - Held, and the biggest: the first contentful paint was often Margin's own. Its sidebar (kept open, drawn in a page loading hidden) and the switch cover's spinner are in the page's document, and count for it. An app's page with an empty body that its script fills later (the host's new `/p/Y…`, 0.7 s later) had its first contentful paint at 54 ms, Margin's, and was shown then, empty; the loading cover and a new tab from the palette did the same. So readiness now also needs something of the page's own drawn in view (above). After: shown 43–46 ms after its own content came (`shelllate`), not before it and not at the limit; `shellcover` and `shellbehind` the same for the loading cover and a new tab.
  - Held: the barrier's second observer, started inside the first's callback, was skipped in that update, and the page got an error event for it: "ResizeObserver loop completed with undelivered notifications.", twice per restored tab in the host's page log (a site's error reporting, or a dev server's overlay, would take it for its own). The second observer now starts from a message the first posts to itself. After: no error (`unseen`, `unloadedlate` check the page's log).
  - Held: `windowSeen` was false for every page that loaded in front, so the first return after a link, the address bar or a reload was introduced for nothing (80–83 ms in the host against 9–12). WebKit's mark stays with a tab's page for good, so only a page made afresh needs it: `back_forward` (Safari's restores, from its log above) or the tab's first document.
