After: such a return 7–9 ms, not introduced (`navigated`). The host now restores a sleeping or relaunched tab from its saved history, as Safari does, so `unseen` still covers the real case (its page's navigation checked to be `back_forward`).
  - Held: an old page still loaded in a tab going somewhere else answered "loaded" for the new one. "Loaded" now counts only once the tab says so too.
  - Held: on Chrome, ⌘T tabs came forward 250 ms after DOMContentLoaded with no paint evidence. Chrome waits for the load again.
  - Held: the barrier's answers weren't in the switch log; they are now (`barrier`). The unseen test didn't cover the 150 ms wait or the late cancel; a test does now, and fails without either.
  - Held: `unseen`'s 10 ms limit let 6 ms through without the introduction. Now 4 ms (1–2 ms with it).
  - Not kept: asking for readiness answered at once, every 50 ms, instead of one question waiting in the page. A new tab's answer looked held back until its load, but a heartbeat in the background showed the background itself had stalled (below), on HEAD's build too.
- **Verified:**
  - Unit tests: 341 pass, each new one failing without its change (checked by undoing each change in a copy): Margin's paint not taken for the page's, the barrier's second observer outside the first's update, the 150 ms wait and the late cancel, the old page's "loaded", Chrome's new tab waiting for its load.
  - WebKit host, on the build before the review: `shelllate` shown at 63 ms, before its text at 726 ms; `shellcover` and `shellbehind` failed; `navigated` introduced, 80–83 ms; two ResizeObserver errors in the restored page. After: all pass.
  - WebKit host, all 57 scenarios (`run.sh all`): in the last full run 55 passed. `shelllate` failed once when the page in front was hidden for an instant during the restore's flip, which turns introductions off for the session (the same host race once failed `unloadedlate`'s "hidden once" check in the run before), and `behindfocus` hit the host's known crash; both passed when run again. In the run before, `shellbehind` crashed the host by checking the new tab before it existed; it now waits for its row (3 of 3 passed after).
  - The host's background stalled for 2–4 s from about 1 s after it started on most runs today (a 100 ms heartbeat in it stopped; load average 7–20), on HEAD's build as well: `tabids` failed on both. The new scenarios do their switch within about a second of install.
  - Not yet in Tirth's Safari: TESTING.md.

## Keep-ready costs less: six tabs, no retries of tabs with no page (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari, with no flicker. The look at his Safari's log for that found what keep-ready costs; this is that part.
- **Found** (his Safari's log, 00:32–00:52 on this build, read with `/usr/bin/log`; the code):
  - Hidden tabs held by keep-ready ran at foreground priority for 6039 process-seconds in 19 minutes, against 346 s of tabs on screen: about 5 hidden processes at any moment, 4–8 even through 6 minutes with no switching, one of them 348 s of CPU. A held process skips Safari's whole suspend path (its layers aren't made volatile, its memory isn't given back). The holds didn't prevent the dark frames in that log; those had other causes (the switching entry above).
  - 950 failed scripts ("Could not execute script on this tab") in 19 minutes, 16–46 a minute, in groups 40–60 s apart. Keep-ready chose tabs with no page (most, after a relaunch) and tried each again 40 s after it failed: the schedule treated the failure like a hold nearly over, though a minute was meant. Nothing else fails on a clock. The other scripts that fail on a tab with no page run on a switch or a start: the wake (two per switch, and two every 0.5 s while the pointer rests still on such a tab's row, for at most 10 s), the paint question while a tab is restored, the cut-off check on a switch to a suspect tab, the current-copy check (once per tab per background run), and at a relaunch or install the repair of open pages (two per unloaded tab in each of four passes over 4.5 s: the groups of 46–87 at starts).
  - Of 25 returns to a tab seen before, in 8 hours of his tab selections, 20 went to one of the six tabs used last. The other 5 went 8–10 back.
  - A background start ticked at once, beside the switch or click that may have started it, and with no holds known: up to 24 at once.
- **Changed** (`background/keep-ready.js`, `background.js`):
  - At most 6 tabs held (24 before): the six used last of the spaces the windows show, not the one in front. Still 75 s holds, renewed, for 30 minutes after Safari was last used (the setting).
  - A tab a hold found no page in (or no access to) is set aside. It isn't tried again until a tab event says its page changed (`tabs.onUpdated` with a status or an address, or it's shown), or 10 minutes have passed. It doesn't count toward the six: the next tab with a page is held in its place 1.5 s later. An event that comes while the failing hold was on its way keeps the tab from being set aside.
  - A background start's first tick comes 1.5 s after its first sync. A background that stopped and started again still holds the tab just left at once; a copy's first start (after a Safari restart or an update) holds it with the set, since its tab ids aren't known before the sync.
  - The tabs set aside are kept in `storage.session` too, so a background Safari stops and starts again doesn't try each of them again.
  - A hold that leaves the six is forgotten at the next tick; it ends by itself.
- **Expected:** at most 6 hidden processes kept held, against up to 24 (more for up to 75 s right after switching through many tabs: each tab left is held at once, and a hold isn't called off). In that log most tabs had no page after the relaunch, so the gain there is small; it grows as tabs load during a session. Failed holds: about one per unloaded tab of the spaces shown every 10 minutes, or after a page event, not one every 40 s. For the 17 a group in that log, under 2 a minute instead of 16–46.
- **Deviations from the plan:** none in behavior. `resetKeepReady` and a tick count in `keepReadyState` are new, for tests only.
- **Not changed:** the hover wake's renewals and the relaunch repair (not on a clock, and the switching code's), the setting's label (it still says "the tabs of the space you're in").
- **Risks:** a tab used further back than six shows dark for a moment when switched to after Safari suspended it (in plain Safari too). A tab whose page comes back with no tab event isn't held for up to 10 minutes.
- **After review** (one reviewer, five findings, each checked against the code):
  - Held up: a hold that left the six still set the next tick by its end. Once its renewal time had passed, ticks came a second apart until it ended, about 25 on every switch to a tab outside the six (a unit test with mocked timers counted them). Such holds are now forgotten.
  - Held up: the tabs set aside lived only in the background's memory. Whether Safari stops the background often in use isn't known (it stayed up 15+ minutes in his log, under the old code, which kept it much busier). If it does, each start would try every unloaded tab again, about 17 failed scripts in his log's case. They're now in `storage.session`, read once per start with the time Safari was last used. Its ids hold: it's cleared with them (a Safari restart, a new copy).
  - Held up: "at most 6 held" overstated it right after quick switching (above).
  - Held up in part: "the tab just left is still held at once" was wrong for a copy's first start. Not for the case the reviewer gave (a background Safari stopped when idle, then a switch): that background has its tabs matched as soon as it knows it owns the state (`store.js` `decideOnce`), so `holdNow` works at once. The suggested fix (hold the left tab once synced) was not taken: before a first start's sync, the id read for the tab left is the last session's. The comment and this entry now say so.
  - Not changed: `background.js`'s two new calls have no unit test. Nothing in the tests imports `background.js` (it adds about 30 listeners as it loads); the host runs it.
- **Not verified:** the host suspends no hidden tab in the time its background lives, so the six and the tabs set aside are checked by unit tests, not seen. Whether Safari stops the background often while one page is read, and whether it tells `tabs.onUpdated` for tabs it restores unloaded (that would bring the retries back): Safari's log, TESTING.md.
- **Verified:** unit tests (349; new: at most six, tabs with no page not counted and replaced at once, not tried again for ten minutes, tried again after a page event or once shown, an event while the hold was on its way, a background start holding nothing at once; after review, a hold that leaves the six forgotten with no ticks a second apart (25 ticks in 70 s without it, at most 3 with it), and the tabs set aside kept across a background's restart, a tab shown before the read not set aside by it; each fails with its change undone). WebKit host, `run.sh all`: 57 of 57 on the final build. The run before it (the same Safari build but for a check that only matters in Chrome): 56 of 57, `introduce` hit the host's crash (EXC_BREAKPOINT in the scenario runner, right after the new tab's eval). Run alone 3 times each on this build and on HEAD's: HEAD's crashed once and passed twice; this build crashed once, passed once, and once its switch log didn't answer (the background stalled; the load average had jumped to 17). Before review, 54–55 of 57 three times on this build, with the same scenarios failing the same way on HEAD's.

## Lighter page work: storage read without the icons, a hidden tab catching up before it's shown, no drawing where Chrome hides Margin (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari, with no flicker, and Margin light on memory and CPU in Safari and Chrome. This is the third part: work each page does that it doesn't need to, or does too late.
- **Found:**
  - Every page read all of `storage.local` at its load, at every preparation for a switch and at every show that wasn't prepared. That is about 305 KB with 120 sites' icons, all but 1–2 KB of it the icons, and took 6–18 ms in the page in the WebKit host (median 7, 15 reads). The icons rarely change.
  - A hidden tab puts some of its page's work off until it's shown: a new sheet's media queries and units rewritten for the box, a move of its box (its media lists, a read of `<body>`, the page's resize event), and a read of `<body>` its styles or attributes asked for. The preparation draws the sidebar and can move the box itself (a new width, Keep open turned on in another tab), so that move was put off too. All of it ran once the tab was in view: in the host, a frame (11–21 ms) after the show, 5 runs. A tab loaded behind the page always had a read owed, because Margin marks its `<html>` once the sidebar takes over from the placeholder: that read came 18–24 ms after its first show (6 runs). This was the "read on show" left open in the entry on reading `<body>` again.
  - Frames a hidden page asked for (`requestAnimationFrame`) don't run until it's shown, so their work also landed on the show.
  - Chrome, Margin only in full screen (the default): in a window that isn't full screen the sidebar isn't shown, but it was drawn all the same, at every load and every change, in every tab.
- **Changed:**
  - **Storage** (`shared/stored.js` `readForPage`, `content/core.js` `refresh`, the new-tab page): a page reads all of storage once. After that it reads the keys it uses (the open tabs, spaces, pins, folders, archive, settings, owner, the shown-tab note, page zoom, and any key it has seen in a read or a change event), not the icons, `lostTabs2` or the old names of the open tabs. With the open tabs only under their old names (just updated from a build before them) it reads all of it, as before.
  - The background writes a short mark beside the icons (`faviconsMark`: how many, and the sum of their times), in a `set()` of its own once the icons are stored (`background/store.js` `flush`). A page keeps its icons current from the change events, as it does the rest. It reads the icons again only when the stored mark isn't the one it has, so a tab Safari suspended (it misses events) finds out at its next read. Icons stored by a build before the mark are written again with it once, at the next background start of the copy in charge.
  - **A hidden tab catches up** (`content/page/box.js` `catchUp`, `sheets.js`): the preparation for a switch asks the page's world, after drawing the sidebar and placing the box, to do now what it put off: changes made in that same turn and not yet told to its observers, the sheet pass and `:has()` answers, the first children, a move rested, a resize's read, an owed read, the locks. A frame put off for any of these is cancelled and run now. Nothing is left for the show. Only while the tab is hidden, and only in a page with a box. A copy of the page's script from before this ignores the message. The page's own resize event goes in a task of its own right after (a message the page's world sends itself, which WebKit doesn't hold back in a hidden page as it may a timer), not inside the preparation the background waits for. With under 150 ms left before the background stops waiting (it now says when: `until`), none of it runs and it's left for the show, as before.
  - **Chrome:** in a window where Margin isn't active (not full screen, Margin only in full screen), the sidebar's rows aren't drawn. Everything else runs as before (the state, the layout, the trackpad, the palette's state). It's drawn when Margin comes in, in the same turn, before the layout shows it: the full-screen message, `hello`, and the setting turned off all draw. With Keep open, the tab in sight draws its rows once its page is idle (`requestIdleCallback`), so the sidebar it shows on going full screen has its icons loaded. Hidden tabs, and every tab without Keep open (the sidebar comes in closed), don't. Safari always draws, as before.
- **Measured** (WebKit host unless said; HEAD 2365a15's build / this change):
  - Storage read in a page, 120 icons (306 KB), 3 tabs, 4 prepared switches and a show by Safari's tab bar, 3 runs: HEAD 306,039 bytes, 6–18 ms (median 7, n = 15); now 1,419 bytes, 0–13 ms (median 1, n = 14). A page's first read at load is still all of it.
  - Hidden tab with a sheet added and its box moved by the preparation (`catchup`, 5 runs each): everything there in the tab's first moment in view, 0 frames / 1 frame, 11–21 ms after it on HEAD. The preparation took 5–6 ms / 5–6 ms.
  - Tab loaded behind, first shown, opened before Margin started (not introduced or covered; 3 runs each): its read of `<body>` before the show / 18–24 ms after it. Preparation 5–7 / 7–11 ms (machine load 4–12).
  - Tab opened behind after Margin started, introduced and arriving covered, as real tabs are (`catchup-loaded` after review, 3 runs each): its read of `<body>` in the preparation, after the introduction / 1 ms after the show, before its first frame. Preparation 4–7 / 7–14 ms.
  - Chrome, headless Chromium, 30 tabs, a window that isn't full screen, 10 reloads × 2 runs: rows drawn 0 / 30; main-thread task time per load, median 15.9 and 16.5 ms / 20.8 and 20.0 ms (after review, Keep open off: 14.5 and 16.8 / 20.6 and 20.2; Keep open on, the tab in sight draws at idle: 20.7 and 20.7, the same work as HEAD's, off the load). On the full-screen message: 30 rows, opens on hover, its width and its box (360 with Keep open) the same in both.
- **Deviations from the plan:**
  - The icons aren't split into a key per site. That would make a page's first read smaller too, but it's a change of format that every copy of Margin reads (an older one still running beside a newer one reads all of storage), so the mark was the smaller change. The first read at load stays about 305 KB with 120 sites.
  - The new-tab page reads the same way (it read all of storage twice each time it was shown).
  - Catching up also covers frames already asked for and changes not yet told to the observers, not only the work put off for being hidden: the preparation's own changes (the sidebar drawn, the box placed, a cover's style on `<body>`) are in that same turn.
  - No unit test for the last two parts. The page's world and `core.js` have no harness in `tests/` (they need a real page); the host's `catchup` and `catchup-loaded` check them, and both fail on HEAD's build. The Chrome part was checked with a headless Chromium script outside the repo (no Playwright suite runs now).
  - A page shown takes a change of the mark alone without drawing its sidebar again (the icons came in the change before it). The same on the new-tab page.
- **Not changed:** a page zoom changed while the tab was hidden still comes after the show (the page asks the background for it then). A hidden tab's first read at load. Safari drawing the sidebar in hidden tabs.
- **Risks** (TESTING.md):
  - A copy of Margin from before this, running beside this one and writing icons, writes no mark: pages of this copy see those icons only at their next load. Only the copy in charge writes (shared/owner.js), so this needs an old build to be in charge.
  - Margin's own catch-up work (the sheet pass, a read of `<body>`) runs during the preparation: on a page with many sheets added while hidden, the preparation takes longer. Before, the same work ran at the show, in the way of its first frame. It doesn't start with under 150 ms left; the page's own resize listeners never run inside it.
  - Chrome: a window that goes full screen draws its sidebar at that moment in hidden tabs, and in the tab in sight without Keep open, about the same work as a load's draw before.
- **Verified:** unit tests 356 (7 new, in `tests/page-reads.test.js`: the mark and its place in the write, the keys read, the icons read again only when the mark changes, the old names, an icon learned during a read, a long random run of writes with events taken, missed and arriving during reads against a full read, and the icons written again with their mark at a start). All fail on HEAD's code; with the mark check, the icons taken as the read finishes, the old names' full read, or the start's rewrite undone in turn, the test for it fails. WebKit host `run.sh all`: 58 of 59; `behindfocus` hit the host's known crash (Trace/BPT trap). Run alone 3 times on each build, it crashed twice on both and passed once on both. After review: unit tests 357 (the write order test is new; the key-order check went, as it showed nothing about Safari). WebKit host `run.sh all`: 59 of 61 (load average 8–12): `unloaded` failed 2 checks and `behindfocus` hit the host's crash. Alone, `unloaded` passed 3 of 3 on this build and on HEAD's; `behindfocus` crashed once in 3 on each.
- **After review** (four findings, each tried first):
  - **The mark could land before the icons.** Safari keeps a `set()`'s values in a hash map and writes one row per key in its order, stopping at the first that fails (WebKit's `WebExtensionContextAPIStorage.cpp` `storageSet`, `WebExtensionStorageSQLiteStore::setKeyedData`); it fires events only for the rows written. The key's place in the object didn't decide anything. A new mark over old icons, read by a page during the retry's wait, left that page with the old icons for good if it then missed the icons' event (a suspended tab). Fixed: the mark goes in a `set()` of its own once the icons are stored; if that fails, both are written again. New test: storage that writes the last key first and fails the icons' row once; on the old code the stored mark was one of icons never stored and the page never got the new icon.
  - **The catch-up ran the page's own resize listeners inside the preparation.** Confirmed in the host (`catchup-slow`, a 1.2 s listener): the preparation ran past its 0.9 s, was cancelled, and the switch took 4.1 s; on HEAD's build 0.33 s, then the page's 1.2 s at the show. Fixed: the page's resize event goes in a task of its own right after the preparation answers (passes 3 of 3, 0.35 s), and no catch-up starts with under 150 ms left (`catchup-late`; each half fails with its change undone).
  - **Chrome, Keep open: icons empty as Margin came in.** Confirmed in headless Chromium with icons 40 ms away: rows drawn at the full-screen message showed 30 empty icons for 3–4 frames (45–55 ms); HEAD's had them all in the first frame. Fixed with the idle draw above, for the tab in sight and only with Keep open (without it the sidebar comes in closed, so its icons load before it's opened): all 30 in the first frame.
  - **The read for a tab loaded behind was moved in front of the show for nothing:** not so for real tabs. The host scenario opened its tab before Margin started, so it was neither introduced nor covered. Opened after (the scenario now does), HEAD's build made the read 1 ms after the show, before its first frame; now it's in the preparation, and the preparation isn't longer (4–7 ms against 7–14). Not done: leaving Margin's own `data-margin-docked` out of what asks for a read. Turning the placeholder off changes `<html>`'s size, and a `<body>` sized in percent or by the page's styles can read differently after it; that read is HEAD's, not this change's.


## A page left doesn't stay in memory (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** Margin light on memory in Safari and Chrome. Earlier host measurements saw a tab keep 7–23 MB more with each same-site navigation with Margin than without, cause unknown.
- **Found** (WebKit host on macOS 27.2, WebKit 22625.2.8.11.9, the same build as Safari's web content processes on this Mac; the footprint of the tab's process from `proc_pid_rusage` after two forced collections, and `heap`/`vmmap` of that process, which count what's kept):
  - Most of the old numbers were the host's. A page made to navigate by a host `page` step (`callAsyncJavaScript`) stays in memory whenever any content-script world is in it: an empty Margin content script, or an empty `WKUserScript` in a world of the host's own with no extension at all, kept all 21 of 21 documents of a 20-navigation run (276–280 MB against 88 without, pages of 20,000 elements). Pages that navigate themselves (as a link does) kept 3 of 21 with the empty script, as without it.
  - Margin's own share is real. With pages that navigate themselves, Margin kept every page left: 21 documents and 21 content-script worlds (`WebKit::WebExtensionAPINamespace`) after 20 navigations. 353–370 MB against 88–92 without Margin (large pages, 4 runs), 115–125 against 27 (small pages, 3 runs). Wikipedia, 15 navigations: 641 MB against 208.
  - The cause is the two listeners on `browser.*` events in `content/core.js` (`storage.onChanged`, `runtime.onMessage`). WebKit keeps each listener with a hold on its world (`WebExtensionCallbackHandler`'s `JSRetainPtr` of the global context), and the world holds the document. The pages here went into the back/forward cache as they were left (`pagehide` with `persisted`) and were dropped from it later; their listeners were never let go. A build with the listeners not added kept 3 documents. Taking them off only on a `pagehide` that isn't `persisted` changed nothing; taking them off on every `pagehide` kept 3.
  - Not the cause, each tried: a page script's own memory (16 MB in the page's world isn't kept), a content-script world by itself, an isolated world holding a 16 MB array, the listeners left on with the `document` held, Margin's sidebar removed and `globalThis.Margin` deleted at `pagehide`.
- **Changed** (`content/core.js`): the two listeners come off at `pagehide` and go back on at `pageshow` when the page comes back from the back/forward cache. That `pageshow` already has `shown()` read storage again, so what changed while the page was away isn't missed. Nothing else changes.
- **Measured** (host, pages that navigate themselves, 20 navigations, after two forced collections; HEAD 5f7f4cc's build / this change / no Margin):
  - Large pages (20,000 elements): 357, 353, 370, 361 MB / 130, 120, 130 MB / 88, 92 MB. Documents kept 21 / 3 / 3.
  - Small pages: 115, 125, 125 MB / 76, 71, 76 MB / 27 MB.
  - Wikipedia, 15 navigations (network): 641 / 298 / 208 MB. Content-script worlds kept 18 / 3.
  - What's left above no Margin is Margin's cost in the pages still alive (the one in front and those in the back/forward cache): its sidebar's layers in the page shown (about 30 MB of graphics memory, `vmmap`), and its scripts' objects.
- **Per-tab cost:** no cheap cut found. `heap` shows Margin's malloc share of a page as mostly untyped (about 21 MB over three pages kept), then fonts, SVG icons and transitions in tens of KB each. Splitting it further needs allocation stacks, which WebKit's web content process doesn't take from the host's environment. The earlier findings stand: script size isn't the driver, and the theme's lazy mount was judged too risky.
- **Risks:** a page in the back/forward cache gets no `browser.*` events until it's back; it catches up from storage as it's shown. In Chrome the same code runs, with the same effect.
- **Not verified:** real Safari. Same engine build, and Safari keeps pages in its back/forward cache too, so the same is expected there (TESTING.md).
- **Verified:** unit tests (357). New host scenario `bfcache`: back from the cache, a change in storage reaches the page (Keep open turned on in storage puts it in its box); with the listeners not put back at `pageshow` it fails. WebKit host `run.sh all` twice (load average 4–18): 60 of 62, `press` and `slowunloaded` failing once each; then 61 of 62, `behindbox` hitting the host's crash (Trace/BPT trap; passed alone after). Run alone 7 times on this build and on HEAD's: `press` failed 2 / 1, `slowunloaded` 1 / 2.
- **Method** (for later): `heap <pid>` and `vmmap --summary <pid>` work on the host's web content processes (the pid from `_webProcessIdentifier`); a document kept shows as one more `WebCore::HTMLDocument` and, with Margin, one more `WebKit::WebExtensionAPINamespace`. Navigate with the page's own script, not a host `page` step (tools/webkit-host/README.md, Limits).

## The branch after its last review: questions that end, slow servers, unseen tabs across pages (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari, no dark flash, Margin light on memory and CPU, no new problems. A last review of the whole branch found these; each was reproduced first with a test that fails without its fix.
- **Fixed:**
  - **A question a page never answers.** The "has it painted?" question (`readyInPage`) and the paint barrier ended only on their own timer, in Margin's world. A page that went away before answering (a script redirect still loading an image or a tag, a JavaScript login or locale redirect) took that timer with it. The switch then waited out the whole wait on that one question and was shown at the load, as before this branch, and WebKit kept the tab's process awake for the script until a garbage collection (the 11 minutes in Safari's log that `keepAwake` already fixed). Both now end on `pagehide` and `pageswap`, the way `keepAwake` does. The question is also asked 400 ms at a time, so one that never comes back costs half a second, not the rest of the wait.
  - **A slow server shown as Safari's empty view.** The 1.5 s limit counted from the load's start whether or not the page had committed. A tab whose server took 1.5–2.5 s to answer was shown with no document and came up dark, where the build before waited and introduced it. The 1.5 s now starts only once the tab's own document has answered. Before that the switch waits for the whole wait, which is back to 2.5 s from the click (`RESTORE_MAX_MS`, 2 s on this branch before).
  - **Fewer failing scripts while a server is slow.** Before its document commits, the question was sent every 50 ms: 58 failing scripts in 3 s for a new tab from the palette, each a line in Safari's log. Now every 50 ms for the first half second (the usual commit, so no slower there), then less and less often, at most 200 ms apart: 23 in the same 3 s (unit test).
  - **Unseen tabs across pages.** WebKit's "was in a window" mark is the tab's, not the page's. A tab in front at a launch whose page followed a link, reloaded or redirected before it was first left started a new page that counted itself as seen, so its first return wasn't introduced and showed the empty view. Now the page says so as it starts (`hello`, `unseen`), and the background keeps such tabs in `storage.session` (`unseenTabs`) until it sees them shown (their activation) or introduces them.
  - **Keep-ready:** a tab taken back before the background had read the tabs set aside stayed in `storage.session`. The next background start set it aside again for up to 10 minutes, dropping a tab with a page from the six. The read now writes the cleaned list back.
- **Low findings:**
  - Fixed: Margin's own arrival cover (opacity 0 on `<body>`) made a second switch to a tab still under it see nothing drawn, and wait for the cover, the load or the limit. `readyInPage` now looks past that cover only when it is Margin's (`Margin.content.arrivalHides`), and then looks at each element's own opacity up to `<body>`, so an app fading itself in still counts as not drawn.
  - Fixed: a page could hold the icons' new mark with the old icons, if it took the mark's event after a read that landed between the background's two writes. It then never read the new icons. A read now compares the stored mark with the mark of the icons the page has, not the mark it was told.
  - Not changed: a paint barrier that runs to its 400 ms limit still lets the show go out. Waiting for another one would add up to 400 ms to every such switch if WebKit doesn't run a hidden page's rendering updates. No barrier timeout was seen in the host, and the switch log records each barrier's answer, so Safari's log can show first whether it happens.
- **Measured** (WebKit host, medians, n = 6 each, the build before this branch, 0973822 / this one, from the click; load average 5–14):
  - Unloaded tab that paints at once and loads at 3 s: shown 2899 / 310 ms, cover gone 3037 / 450 ms. Its content there as it's shown 2 / 1 ms.
  - Unloaded slow page (parsed at 1.5 s): 1998 / 1741 ms. Unloaded fast page: 258 / 303 ms (the barrier's two rendering updates, as before).
  - Never-shown tab: 184 / 71 ms, cover gone 320 / 206 ms. Loaded tab: 12 / 14 ms. Busy page (280 ms): 280 / 281 ms.
  - Storage read in a page (120 icons): 306,039 / 1,419 bytes, 7 / 1 ms.
  - Memory per hidden tab (two forced collections): 60.0 / 59.8 MB for a large page (44.1 with no Margin), 20.1 / 19.9 MB for a small one (10.3). No change there; the branch's memory gains are the pages left (the entry above) and fewer hidden tabs held.
- **Risks:**
  - A tab whose page is too busy to answer the question isn't known to have committed, so it waits up to 2.5 s from the click, as before this branch, not 1.5 s from its load.
  - A tab in `unseenTabs` that the user brings back with Safari's own tab bar, then navigates in front, is introduced once more than needed on its next Margin switch (one flip, about 70 ms in the host). The page start and the activation can also race if the background is slow to handle the activation, and then the tab counts as seen.
  - Looking past the arrival cover reads each candidate element's computed opacity, only while that cover is on.
- **Verified:** unit tests (365, 8 new; each fails without its fix). WebKit host: new scenario `unseennav` (restart, a link followed in front, switch away and back) passes on this build, and on HEAD's build the tab isn't introduced. `run.sh all`: 61 of 63. `unloadedspace` failed, and fails 3 of 3 on HEAD's build too. `slowunloaded` failed: its page loads 1.5 s after it commits, the same as the limit, so it races. Run alone with the load average at 10–22, it failed 5 of 8 on this build and 3 of 8 on HEAD's, mostly from the host's background stalling (no answer from the background).

## Keep-ready holds end when they're no longer wanted; switch log timings (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari, no dark frames, much less CPU and memory. Round 3 starts with what can be measured and what is safe now.
- **Why:** a keep-ready hold is a `keepAwake` script waiting in the tab. While it waits, WebKit keeps the tab's process at foreground priority (`WebPageProxy` holds a foreground activity for a pending script). In Tirth's Safari log (2026-11-02) held hidden processes used 68% of all web content CPU, 18% of a core each. Twelve of them kept 1.7 GB. They averaged 11.5 held, up to 23, although the cap was six: a hold couldn't be taken back, and every tab left got 75 s.
- **Changed:**
  - Each keep-ready hold has a random key of its own. `keepAwake` also ends when a release names that key (`releaseTab`, a script in the page's own world). A key is per hold, so a late release never ends a newer hold, in whatever order the scripts land. Random, so a page sees nothing that ties two sites or a copy of Margin together. The wakes' own short holds have no key and are never released.
  - The tick releases every hold whose tab is no longer kept (it only forgot them before). `holdNow` releases the tab used longest ago once a switch takes the held set past six. So no more than six are held at any time, also in a run of switches. Turned off, unused for 30 minutes, or no longer the copy in charge: every hold ends at once.
  - The tab just left counts among the six by when it was used, whatever space it's in. A change of space and back returns to that tab (`showSpace`), so the tick doesn't let it go for being in the space left. It keeps the 75 s hold `holdNow` gave it, as before, but isn't renewed. If it's among the six used last, it takes one of the six from the new space until its hold ends; a tick just after gives that place back. Its mark is in `storage.session` too, since Safari stops an idle background within a minute.
  - `holdNow` lets the oldest go only once the new hold has gone out and 250 ms have passed without it failing. A tab that can't be held (Margin's start page, a site without access, a tab ⌘W is closing when its activation comes first) used to cost a held tab its hold, and the tick held that tab again 1.5 s later, maybe on a process Safari had just suspended.
  - A hold still waiting on its permission check when it's released is called off (`holdTab`'s `sending`). Sent after the release, it would have run 75 s with nothing left to end it. After a background start the permission check is a round trip, so this can happen.
  - Not while a switch to the tab is under way (`isFocusing`): it ends as the tab is shown. The plan also skipped tabs with a wake under way; not needed, since a release can't end a wake's hold.
  - A hold's keys stay known until its script answers, also after the tab was shown for an instant (an introduction may not end it). A renewal's older hold still running is named too.
  - The holds placed are kept in `storage.session` (`keepReadyHolds`). A background that started again releases the ones the run before placed once they aren't chosen. They ran out after 75 s before. The chosen ones are held again, as before.
  - A tab a held key only passes through isn't held when it's left. Keep-ready also counts it by its use before the key passed (`passing`, from `navigation.js`), not as just used, until it's shown again not in passing. Its activation makes it the latest in the state, so before this the tick 1.5 s later held the tabs passed and let go of the tabs used before the run, and of the tab the run started from once more than five were passed.
  - `MAX_HELD` (6) and the hold length (75 s) are unchanged. Cutting them waits for the stuck-wake fix to be checked in Safari.
- **Switch log** (development diagnostics): each switch now records `heldAtAsk` (keep-ready held the tab as it was asked for: a hold sent to a process known to be running, the tab just left or one held without a break since. Not a hold still on its way, or a first hold of a tab left a while ago: that one may be waiting behind its process's resume, and the tab shows dark all the same. A hold a background before placed counts as it did there, unless the tab's page changed), `hiddenForMs` (since the tab was last left; `keepReadyLeftAt` in `storage.session`) and the preparation's `timings` from the page: `lagMs` (from the background's call to the page running it), `readMs`, `renderMs`, `catchUpMs`, `layoutMs`. That says where the 70–686 ms in the page go, and gives click times next to Safari's log.
- **Risks:**
  - A tab that leaves the six can now be suspended about 8 s after, not 75 s. A switch back to it then lands on a suspended tab, as it did after 75 s before. The stuck-wake fix is next.
  - The other tabs of a space left are released at the next tick. Switching back to the space lands on the tab left there (still held), but a tab after it there can be suspended.
  - Holding a key through more tabs than fit the six releases the oldest held tab at the first step, before it's known where the key stops. If it stops there, that tab was let go a moment before, and the switch wakes it as any tab let go.
  - One more `storage.session` write per switch (the holds and the time left), coalesced into one. A hold that ends isn't written then; the next write carries it.
  - A hold whose release script lands before the hold itself (scripts reordered) runs to its end, as before.
- **Verified:** unit tests (382; 17 new, each fails without its change: on HEAD's code, or by a mutation for the review's fixes; the per-hold guard tests also fail with one shared release event). New WebKit host scenario `keep-ready-cap`: ten tabs, nine switches in the host's tab bar, then the holds waiting in each page counted. This build: the six tabs left last hold one each, the first three none. HEAD's build: eight held at the count, and the first three still held 1.5 s later. The `switching` scenario checks the new switch log fields. `run.sh all` after the review's fixes: 63 of 64 passed, load average 5–7. The host crashed in `behindlate` (Trace/BPT trap), which passed when run alone. `keep-ready-cap` still holds D–I one each and A–C none at the count.

## A tab Safari suspended is kicked awake, and a stuck wake isn't waited on (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari, with no dark frames.
- **Why:** 18 of Margin's switches in Tirth's Safari log showed dark for 116–949 ms. Each went to a tab Safari had suspended. Margin's wake is a script, and a script reaches a suspended process only once WebKit resumes it. For an extension's script WebKit resumes it at background priority only (executeScript asks for the tab's frames first). Under swap that took a median 710 ms, p90 3.6 s. Margin waited out its 400 ms wake and its 900 ms preparation, then showed the tab dark anyway, about 0.9 s later than Safari would have. Only the tab's view coming into the window takes a foreground activity, and the resume then followed a median 32 ms later.
- **Changed:**
  - A switch to such a tab kicks it: the tab to the front and the page in front straight back, in one run loop turn (`kickTab`, the same two updates as an introduction). WebKit dispatches a view newly in a window at once, so the tab's process should resume at foreground priority while the page in front stays on screen. Then the tab is woken again (the instant on screen ended its wake's hold), prepared under a token of its own, and drawn (`paintBarrier`) before it's shown. All of that within 600 ms of the kick.
  - When it kicks: 40 ms after the wake went out for a tab that is likely suspended (`likelySuspended`: left 8 s ago or more, Safari's median suspend delay; not held by keep-ready, shown before, at rest, no wake answered there in the last 1.5 s). Any other tab is kicked once its wake hasn't answered 60 ms after it went out (a running process answers in 17–40 ms). Only a tab Margin knows has a page: one left in this copy's time, or whose wake answered, and not since found without one. The time counts from when the wake's scripts went out, not from the permission check before them, which is slow while Safari starts. An early wake (a press, the palette) that already ran out of time counts as stuck.
  - A preparation sent beside the wake is called off after the kick, now and again once it answers.
  - Never a kick for a tab keep-ready holds (its process runs; a slow wake there is a busy page), a tab never shown (it's introduced instead), a page loading, or a held key passing through (only the tab it stops on goes through `focusTab`). None with introductions off (Safari left a tab in front, or the page in front blinked: `pageBlinked` turns kicks off with them), with another introduction or restore under way in the window, or with less than 150 ms left before the page that asked stops waiting.
  - A switch never sits on a stuck wake. With no kick, a wake not answered 150 ms after it went out isn't waited for. After a kick, the same for the new wake 150 ms after Safari answered the kick (the kick didn't resume it). The preparation is called off and the tab shown at once. The show's own foreground activity resumes the process (a median 32–53 ms in Safari's log). That is as fast as Safari's own tab bar, with the same dark frame, instead of 0.9 s later with the same dark frame.
  - A tab whose late wake says it has no page after the kick has started loading from the kick. The kick's introduction ends there, so it's restored behind the page as before. A kicked tab found loading when its new wake doesn't answer is waited for behind the covered page, as a restore.
  - When a tab was left (`keepReadyLeftAt`) was a switch log field only. Now `likelySuspended` reads it too, whether keep-ready is on or off.
- **Switch log** (development diagnostics): `kicked` is `{ at, kickMs, wakeMs, outcome }`: ms from the ask to the kick, Safari's answer to its two updates, the new wake's answer in ms from that answer (null: none in time), and `resumed` or `stuck`. `stuck: true` means the switch stopped waiting for the wake and showed the tab at once.
- **Not known yet:** the kick comes from WebKit's source, not from Safari's log. If the foreground activity ends before the process is scheduled, the resume may still crawl. Then the fallback caps the cost at about 60 ms, plus the kick's own 110–260 ms, plus 150 ms, instead of 0.9 s. The kick also shows the tab for an instant: if Safari draws a frame in between, that's a blink of the other tab. `pageBlinked` turns kicks off for the session if the page in front notices. The kicked page's own visibility handlers run (a site may count a view), as with introductions. Keep-ready's diet (fewer held tabs) waits for this to be checked in Safari.
- **Verified:** unit tests (10 new: the kick at once and at 60 ms, the renewed wake, the fresh preparation and the barrier before the show, the early preparation called off, the timed-out early wake, the stuck fallback with introductions off and after a kick that doesn't resume, the deadline, a blink turning kicks off, held and loading tabs not kicked, passing keys not kicked, and `likelySuspended`'s timing). The fake browser now models a suspended process: scripts wait for its resume, at background priority or once the tab is selected. Nine of them fail on HEAD's code. The tenth (passing keys) guards what HEAD already did, and fails when the passing path goes through `focusTab`. Each guard fails its test when it's removed (held, loading, passing, introductions off, timing from the send, the deadline, a timed-out early wake). Two older tests used wakes slower than 60 ms where they only meant "slow"; they now use 50 ms. New WebKit host scenarios `kick` and `kick-stuck`: the host can't suspend a process, so the tab's page is busy from the moment the wake's hold reaches it. The page in front is hidden once, by the real switch; the tab's visibility changes twice for the kick before its real show; `kick` is resumed, prepared and drawn before the show, `kick-stuck` shown at once. Against HEAD's build, `kick` fails (no kick). `run.sh all`: 66 of 66 scenarios passed (load average 6–7).
- **After review** (six findings, each reproduced first; a test for each fails on the code before the fix):
  - **The new wake's 150 ms counted from before the kick.** Safari takes ~110 ms to answer the kick's two updates, and the new wake only goes out after. So a kick that worked often came out `stuck`: shown with no preparation and no barrier, and logged as a failed kick. Now the 150 ms, and the 500 ms cap (was 600 from the kick's start), count from Safari's answer. The switch log has the kick's own time (`kickMs`). Test: updates answering in 110 and 260 ms, the page answering in 40 ms; before, `stuck`.
  - **WebKit holds the window during a kick.** A view that was in a window before and comes back makes WebKit wait for the page's paint, up to 250 ms, inside the first update (`WebPageProxy::activityStateDidChange`, `waitForDidUpdateActivityState`). Introductions never hit this: their tabs had never been in a window. A kicked tab always has. Seen in the host: `kickMs` 96 for a page busy 150 ms, 258 for one busy 700 ms. If the page answers, its reply lands before the new wake's, so the real show waits for its paint as usual. If not, the show doesn't wait and Safari shows its empty view until the page draws: the same dark frame the switch had before, without its 250 ms freeze. A real cost: Safari's whole window can freeze up to 250 ms during a kick. TESTING.md asks Tirth to watch for it; if it shows, kicks go.
  - **A tab Safari unloaded whose "no page" answer was slow got kicked.** The kick selected it, which started its load with no cover, and it was then shown `stuck`, with no document: Safari's empty view. Now only a tab known to have a page is kicked or shown without its wake. Any other waits for its wake as before, so an unloaded one is restored behind the covered page. And a kicked tab found loading when its new wake doesn't answer (its page had gone) is waited for behind the covered page.
  - **Awake tabs were kicked.** 20 ms is below a running process's answer time (17–40 ms), and 4 s hidden is below Safari's median suspend delay (8 s). A tab awake after all got a flip, a second wake, a second preparation and a barrier, about 100–300 ms slower. Its preparation also no longer went out beside its wake. Now 40 ms and 8 s, and the preparation goes out beside the wake for every tab, called off if there's a kick.
  - **A kick Safari left in front didn't call off the preparation sent beside the wake.** It would land on the page on screen. Now called off.
  - **A wake that failed before its scripts went out left the switch waiting for good** (the "sent" promise never settled), and the tab stayed marked as being switched to. Now it settles on failure too, and the switch fails as before.
- **Verified after review:** unit tests 398 (6 new). All 6 fail on the code before the fix. Each fix fails its test when undone: the clock from before the kick, no check for a page, no look for a load after the kick, 20 ms, 4 s, no call-off when left in front, `sent` not settled on failure. The host's `kick` and `kick-stuck` scenarios now show B once first in the host's tab bar, so Margin knows it has a page. That is also when WebKit's hold shows.

## A loaded tab is prepared without reading storage (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari.
- **Why:** before Margin shows a loaded tab, it asks the tab's page to draw its sidebar for the switch (`prepareForShow`), and waits for the answer. In Tirth's Safari log that step took a median 70 ms in the page, p90 686 ms, on every switch to a held or awake tab. 11 of 45 such switches waited 529–1017 ms on it. Most of it was the page's `storage.local.get`, a round trip through Safari's busy UI process. Yet a hidden page already keeps its state current from storage's change events. In the WebKit host every part of the preparation is 0–2 ms (read, render, catch-up, layout), so what costs in Safari is the round trip itself, not Margin's work.
- **Changed:**
  - Every write of the state is stamped: `stateRev` `{ id, after }`, in the same `set()` as the state's keys (`store.js flush`), so one change event brings both. `id` is new for each write, `after` is the write before it. The background passes the id of its last write that went through when it prepares a page (`rev`, read as the script goes out).
  - A page keeps the id of the last write it knows: from its last read, then from each change event whose `after` is the id it knows. A gap (an event missed), a change sent to a read instead of taken, a read that missed a change, or the page leaving (its listeners come off) make it unknown.
  - `prepareForShow` with a `rev` the page knows, no read under way and no stale read: it draws from the state it has, at once, and doesn't read. Otherwise it reads, as before. Storage events and the preparation's script reach the page's process over one connection, in order, and the event goes out before the background hears its write went through. So a page that knows the background's last write has seen every write up to it.
  - A write that fails leaves no id known until the next one goes through: pages read meanwhile. The next write still follows the failed one, so a page that saw part of it finds the chain unbroken, and one that didn't reads.
  - A background starting again (Safari stops it within a minute when idle) knows the stored id from its first read, and its first write follows it. Pages don't have to read again after each restart.
  - A claim (a newer copy taking over, or a copy claiming back from an older one) starts a chain of its own, and its mark that the stored tabs are unmatched is stamped too. Copies don't hear each other's events, so pages read once more after a claim. A page of one copy knows another copy's write only if it read it, and its state is then what storage held at that write.
  - Before it draws, the preparation lets one task of the page's go by (a message to itself, which WebKit doesn't hold back in a hidden page). WebKit queues a `visibilitychange` as a task, so the hide of an introduction (the tab to the front and straight back) can still be waiting when the preparation's script runs. Drawn at once, the sidebar it carried open was then closed by that late hide (`layout.leave`): the host's `unloaded` scenario failed in about half its runs. The storage read used to give that event its turn. With the turn it passed 8 of 8.
  - Writes outside the state (`owner`, `showing`, `pageZoom`) aren't stamped. A page takes their events as before. It only misses one if Safari drops the event, and then `shown()` still reads 250 ms after the show if no change came.
  - Chrome: no stamp is written (`platform.prepareSidebar` is off; Chrome pages aren't prepared).
  - The preparation sent beside the wake, for a tab neither kicked nor introduced, gets 250 ms (`PREPARE_AWAKE_MS`) once its wake says the page is awake, instead of 900. Counted from the wake's answer, not from when the preparation went out: a process Safari resumed slowly answers both scripts together, the preparation just behind. A preparation later than that is called off before the show, and the page draws as it's shown, inside WebKit's hold, as with Safari's own tab bar. A wake that doesn't answer in time leaves the preparation its 900 ms. Kicked tabs keep the kick's 500 ms, tabs just loaded behind keep 500 ms, introduced tabs 900 ms.
  - The page's catch-up (`link.catchUp`) stays. It already does nothing when nothing waits for the tab to come into sight (each step checks first), and it measured 0–3 ms in the host. The full-document rescan of style attributes a hidden chat tab can queue (sheets.js) is left until the switch log from Tirth's Safari shows `catchUpMs` matters.
- **Switch log** (development diagnostics): a preparation's `timings` has `known`: 1 when the page drew from the state it had without reading (its `readMs` is 0 then), 0 when it read. `revSent`: 1 when the background passed a rev, 0 while a write was waiting or under way (after review). A preparation's `reason` `awake budget` means it missed the 250 ms.
- **Expected:** the switch waits for the script's trip to the page and a few ms of drawing, about 20–60 ms in Safari, instead of a median 70 ms and a tail to 1 s. A busy page (its main thread on its own work) now shows after about 250 ms, drawing its sidebar as it's shown, instead of up to 900 ms later fully prepared.
- **Risks:** a first frame drawn from a state that missed a write the rev doesn't cover (see above): `shown()`'s refresh corrects it 250 ms later at most. A busy page shown before its sidebar is redrawn: its highlight can lag a moment.
- **Verified:** unit tests 404 (6 new). The page's rules (`revAfter`, `knowsState`) and a page fed by a real `store.js`: no read while it heard every write, a read after a lost event, then no read again. The store: the stamp in the same set, following the one before, none known after a failed write, the next following the failed one, a restarted background following the stored one, a claim stamped and starting a chain of its own. The background: the rev passed, the 250 ms cap from the wake's answer, a slow wake keeping 900 ms, a preparation just behind a slow wake not cut off. Chrome writes no stamp. On HEAD's code all of them fail. Eleven mutations of the new code (no stamp, `after` ignored, reads under way ignored, no cap, cap from the send, a failed write keeping its id, no id at start, a stamp on Chrome, no rev passed, the claim unstamped, a claim keeping the chain) each fail a test. WebKit host: a new `prepared` scenario, three switches all drawn from known state (`known: 1`), the first shown frame with its own row highlighted and a setting changed in another tab while it was hidden, and a preparation with an unknown rev reading. It fails on HEAD's build. The full host suite passes (641 checks).
- **After review** (two reviews, five findings; each reproduced first; a test for each fixed one fails on the code before the fix):
  - **A cancel landing during the preparation's first turn was lost.** The turn came before the preparation took the page (`preparedShow`), so `cancelPreparedShow` found no token and did nothing. The preparation then finished anyway: it opened the carried sidebar, and left a token for a later show to take as prepared, for a switch the background had let go of (one past its 250 ms, or superseded). Before S3 the token was set at once, so a cancel never missed. Now the page keeps the last 8 tokens called off before they took it, and a preparation whose token is there stops after its turn. That also covers a cancel reaching the page before the preparation's script runs. Setting the token before the turn instead would let a show queued ahead of it take the new token as prepared. Host: a check in `prepared` sends the preparation and its cancel back to back. It fails on the S3 build in every run (7 of 7) and passes on this one.
  - **A switch asked while a write was still under way drew without it.** The background passed the id of its last write through, and the page knew that id, so it drew. But a read, as before S3, comes behind the write in Safari's storage and gets it. The write's event then lands while the page is hidden, and `shown()` doesn't draw it for a prepared show. So the first frame missed it until the next change or the 250 ms refresh. Now `stateRev()` is null while keys wait to be written (including the owner check before the `set()`), while the `set()` is under way, and until the icons' mark after it is through. The page then reads, as before. A unit test holds each step and fails on the S3 code. Each of the three conditions, undone alone, fails it. Cost: a switch asked during a write reads. In the host, a switch asked about 10 ms after the one before found the activation write still under way and read. The `prepared` scenario now spaces its switches 150 ms. Its check allows a read only where no rev was sent.
  - **A claim kept the last write it knew.** A claim started a new chain but kept `doneRev`. So until its first stamped write, the copy passed the id from before the claim. When it claimed back from an older copy (its tabs' write only queued), its pages knew that id and missed what the older copy wrote. When it claimed at its start with the stored tabs not matched, it passed the other copy's id. Now a claim forgets it too. `stateRev()` is also null whenever the last write sent isn't the last through. A unit test covers both claims and fails on the S3 code.
  - **Not changed: an older copy answering switches with its own rev after a newer copy claimed.** This needs two copies loaded at once (the host's `twins`). Updates and rebuilds normally unload the old copy first. It also only affects that copy's switches before its next ownership check, and every write and every tab event (at most once a second) rechecks. Before S3, the read didn't give a right first frame either: it handed the old copy's page the newer copy's tabs, under the newer copy's ids, unmatched, and the page stood down only after a round trip to the background, after the show. Guarding against it would put a storage read back on the switch path, which is what S3 removes. `shown()`'s refresh reads 250 ms later and stands the page down.
  - **Not changed: drawing what changed while hidden in a prepared show.** A hidden event after the preparation can still carry the open tabs from before the switch. Drawing it in `shown()` would undo the preparation's highlight. The write-under-way fix closes the case the review found.
  - **Not changed: calling off a preparation past its 250 ms again when it answers.** With the tokens kept, the one cancel holds whenever it lands, so a second script would only add work for a busy page.
  - **Tried and dropped: stopping a preparation whose page was shown during its turn.** Before S3, `shown()` took the token in that case. Now a preparation that turn overlaps with Safari's show goes on to draw on the page in sight, and the background's cancel cleans up after it. But at the end of the turn, an introduction's page is still in sight as well, its hide not yet in. Stopping there failed `unloaded` (the carried sidebar closed) in 3 of 5 host runs, against 4 of 4 without the stop. So it stays a risk: the background only shows after the preparation answers or is called off, so the show comes during that turn only if the cancel and the show both overtake the turn's message.
- **Verified after review:** unit tests 406 (2 new; the claim test also covers claiming back). WebKit host: `prepared` passes 3 of 3 and fails on the S3 build. `unloaded` passes 4 of 4. Full suite (`run.sh all`, 67 scenarios): 65 passed. `behindlate` and `behindfocus` hit the host's known crash (Trace/BPT right after a new tab's eval), and both passed 2 of 2 run alone.

## No wasted wakes (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** lower CPU and memory use in Safari, with switching as fast as before.
- **Why:** each wake is a script sent to a tab. For a process Safari suspended, that script resumes it at background priority, and under swap that pages the whole process back in. In Tirth's Safari log 65 of 114 such resumes were never followed by a show of that tab. The sidebar woke a row after the pointer rested on it 80 ms, so a pointer passing over rows woke some of them. It then sent a full wake (two scripts) every 500 ms while the pointer stayed, even when the first hadn't answered. A switch took over a wake only if it was under 600 ms old, although its hold ran 1.8 s. And keyboard runs woke the next tab ahead even when the last one woken ahead was stuck.
- **Changed:**
  - The sidebar wakes a row after the pointer rests on it 150 ms (was 80), and renews every 1.5 s (was 500 ms). A hover hold runs 2 s (`HOVER_KEEP_AWAKE_MS`), so each renewal lands while the one before still runs. A press still wakes the row at once.
  - A renewal (`holdOnly`) sends the hold alone, one `keepAwake` script, once the tab's page has answered a wake and that wake's hold still runs. It asks for no answer. While the page hasn't answered yet (a process resuming slowly), a renewal sends nothing: a second wake would only queue behind the first. A wake not answered is now kept until it is, past the old 1 s, so a renewal can see it. A renewal after a wake that found no page, or answered late, wakes afresh. A renewed hold that fails (the page went away) leaves nothing for a switch to take over.
  - A switch takes over a wake whose page answered in time for as long as its hold runs, less 400 ms (`HOLD_LEFT_MS`; the plan said about 300). The switch's preparation holds the process itself while it's under way, up to 250 ms for a page awake, and `tabs.update` takes about 110 ms. A wake that didn't answer in time still counts 600 ms only, so the kick's timing (`focusTabNow`) sees the same wakes as before. The hold's end counts from when the wake started, before its script reached the page, so it is never later than the real end.
  - A wake no longer counts once its tab is shown (`tabShown`: the hold ends then) or starts loading a page (`onUpdated` status `loading`: the hold ends at `pagehide`). With 600 ms this mattered little. With longer reuse it would have let a switch take a hold that had ended.
  - Keyboard runs wake the next tab ahead only once the one woken ahead before answered within the wake's 400 ms. One that didn't was likely a process resuming at background priority. A new run of presses (1.5 s after that wake, as `NAV_CHAIN_MS`) wakes ahead again.
  - Chrome: no change. Wakes do nothing there (`platform.wakeTabs` is off). The sidebar sends fewer `prewake` messages.
- **Expected:** hover scripts drop from 4 a second per hovered row to about 0.7, and none for a pointer passing over. Fewer background resumes queued ahead of a real switch, and less paging under memory pressure. In the Safari log, the share of `GetFrameInfo` resumes not followed by a show should fall from about 57%.
- **Risks:** a click on a row the pointer reached less than 150 ms ago no longer has a hover wake, but its press sends one, as before. A tab hovered and then left keeps its hold 0.5–2 s after the pointer leaves (was 1.3–1.8 s), and a rest of 150 ms gets a whole 2 s hold. A wake taken over up to 1.4 s after it started (1.6 s for a hover wake, more while the pointer rests on the row) carries its old `ms` in the switch log.
- **Verified:** unit tests 412 (7 new, one old test rewritten: a wake answered 650 ms ago is now taken over, and one 1.45 s old isn't). The new tests cover the takeover inside the hold, the 600 ms that still apply to a wake not answered in time, a tab shown or loading forgetting its wake, the 3 s hover hold with a one-script renewal, no renewal while the page hasn't answered (also past 1 s), a renewal after no page or after a failed hold, and the key run's wake ahead. On HEAD's code, 5 of them fail. The other 2 guard what HEAD already did. Three mutations of the new code each fail a test: no skip while unanswered, a failed renewal kept, `tabShown` not forgetting. WebKit host, `run.sh all` twice on this build: `unseennav` failed once and then passed 3 of 3 alone. `press` failed once (66 of 67 passed). Run alone it failed 2 of 3 on this build, and 3 of 3 on HEAD's build, with the Mac's load average at 10–20, so that's the host under load, not this change. The host can't hover, and it can't suspend a process, so the hover and wake-ahead effects need Tirth's Safari (TESTING.md).
- **Changed after review:**
  - **A switch takes over a wake only if its hold covers the rest of that switch.** The 400 ms left (`HOLD_LEFT_MS`) only covers a preparation bounded by the 250 ms awake budget. Other switches can prepare for up to 900 ms: a tab never shown with introductions off or its window busy, a page with an earlier copy of Margin (given the current one, then prepared again), a page that may be cut off (the wait for its repair). There a wake 1.35 s old was taken over and `tabs.update` went out 330 ms after its hold had ended, with nothing holding the process through the show. Now `wakeTab` takes a `need`: such switches ask for `SWITCH_HOLD_MS` (900 + 300 ms, what a wake 600 ms old always left before) and get a fresh hold otherwise. The earlier-copy path asks again before it adds the copy. Switches inside the awake budget (a click on a hovered row, a key run's last press, the palette) still take the wake over.
  - **A hover hold runs 2 s, not 3 s.** Nothing ends a hover hold when the pointer leaves the row, and a held hidden process runs at foreground priority (about 18% of a core in Tirth's Safari). With 3 s holds renewed every 1.5 s, a hovered row stayed held 1.5–3 s after the pointer left, more than the 1.3–1.8 s before S5. With 2 s it's 0.5–2 s, and renewals still land 500 ms before the hold ends. Not done: a keyed hover hold released as the pointer leaves. That's one more script per row left, and it must not end a hold a click has taken over.
  - **A key run's wake ahead that hasn't answered yet no longer blocks the next one.** The check ran as soon as the next press was done, often before a page that takes 180–400 ms had answered, so tabs were woken ahead only every other press. Now the next wake ahead waits for that answer and goes out then if it came in time (the latest press's tab). It's still skipped after a wake that timed out.
- **Verified after review:** unit tests 415 (3 new, 1 changed). The new tests: a never-shown tab with introductions off and a 1.35 s old wake is still held at its show; a page given the current copy is still held at its show; a wake ahead that answers in 250 ms, after the next press, still gets the tab after woken. The hover test now checks the 2 s hold. On the pre-review code all 4 fail (the show went out 327 ms and 23 ms after the hold ended). Removing the earlier-copy hold alone fails its test. WebKit host `run.sh all`: 65 of 67 passed. `behindfocus` and `introduce` hit the host's known crash (Trace/BPT). Run alone, `behindfocus` passed 2 of 2. `introduce` passed 2 of 4 here and 1 of 3 on HEAD's build (load average up to 13), so it's the host.

## Restores: never shown with no document, less waiting after the paint (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as Safari, with no dark frames.
- **Why:** a tab Safari unloaded is loaded behind the page, then shown. In Tirth's log one such tab was shown at the 2.5 s limit with nothing committed, and the window was Safari's empty view for 2014 ms. Restores in general held a page that had painted back a median 1.2–1.4 s: after the paint, the wake renewal and the arrival cover went out one round trip, then the introduction, then the preparation (up to 500 ms) and a paint barrier, often two.
- **Changed:**
  - A tab whose own document hasn't answered by `RESTORE_MAX_MS` (2.5 s) is waited for up to `RESTORE_HARD_MAX_MS` (5 s), still behind the covered page, within what the page that asked waits (its 7 s less the 1.5 s the switch keeps for itself). Once its document answers, the usual limits apply again. Escape, another click and a key press end the wait as before (within one look, under 100 ms).
  - The wake renewal and the arrival cover go out as soon as the tab's document first answers (`waitLoaded`), while its paint is still waited for. On the paint the tab is introduced at once. To know sooner, the first question to a page not yet answered asks it to answer within 150 ms (`READY_FIRST_ASK_MS`) instead of 400. The answer is still waited for as long as before, so a busy page doesn't get a second question queued sooner. Only restores and loading tabs do this; a new tab from the palette asks as before.
  - A page just loaded behind gets 300 ms for its preparation (`PREPARE_LOADED_MS`, was 500). One that misses it is shown unprepared and draws its own tab, as before.
  - No second paint barrier when only the wake answered after the first. The wake changes nothing in the page. Only a preparation answering after the barrier calls for another.
  - No second paint barrier for the cut-off check when the tab wasn't cut off. After a relaunch every tab not loaded yet is a cut-off suspect (it didn't answer when the open tabs were checked). Each restore then reset the barrier for a check that found nothing.
  - Readiness (`readyInPage`): a page still parsing counts as painted once its own content is drawn and its first contentful paint is 150 ms old. A page whose parser waits on a script at the end of its body has drawn the rest long before, and Safari shows it at its first layout. The own-content check stays, so an app's empty shell is still not painted. This also lifts the loading cover of a page Safari loads in front a little sooner.
  - Switch log (development): `committed`, ms from the click to the tab's document first answering, null if it never did.
  - Not done: the pushed `painted` message from the page (the plan's optional step). The page doesn't know it's being restored, so it would need its own rules and a fallback to the questions anyway. Questions per restore are still 15–35.
  - Chrome: no change. It doesn't restore behind the page (`platform.restoreTabs` is off) or ask about paints (`hiddenPaints`).
- **Expected:** no restore shown with nothing committed, short of a dead server at 5 s. In the WebKit host, from the click to the show, 3 runs each against HEAD's build: `unloadedlate` 246–266 ms (was 308–318), `shelllate` 523–534 ms (was 611–638). Both now have one paint barrier where they had two. In Safari the steps after the paint were slower (762 ms paint to introduction, 694 ms introduction to show), so the gain there should be larger.
- **Risks:** a server slow to answer keeps the spinner up to 5 s, not 2.5 s. A page Margin's scripts can't run in once it commits (a PDF, say) also waits until it has loaded or 5 s. The arrival cover now goes on while the page is still loading, so a switch called off then leaves that tab's page hidden under the cover for up to 3 s behind (`ARRIVAL_MAX_MS`), as it would have after the paint. A page shown while still parsing can be less complete than before, as in Safari.
- **Verified:** unit tests 422 (8 new): a tab with no document at 2.5 s stays covered until it commits; one that never commits is shown at the hard limit; Escape and a key press still end that wait; the wake and cover go out before the paint; one barrier when the wake answers last; one barrier for a cut-off suspect restored; the 300 ms budget; still-parsing readiness. The first five and the readiness test fail on HEAD's code; the two barrier tests fail with their change undone. WebKit host: `shelllate`, `shellcover`, `shellbehind` pass. One `unloadedlate` run failed with the host turning introductions off for a blink at the restore (load average 12); it passed 5 other runs. `run.sh all` on the final build: 62 of 67 passed. `unseennav`, `press` and `behindbox` (the host's Trace/BPT crash) passed alone. `slowunloaded` failed once with the background not answering, then passed 3 of 3 alone. `unloadedspace` failed 3 of 3 here and 2 of 2 on HEAD's build, each time with the background not answering (`setSpace no answer from the background`, load average 14–20): the host, not this change.
- **After review** (two reviews, ten findings; each reproduced or checked first; a test for each fixed one fails on the code before the fix):
  - **A busy page with its document isn't taken for one with none.** A readiness question that no page answered in time (`late`) never told the wait the document was there. So a heavy page, its main thread busy after a relaunch, waited the 5 s hard limit, got twice the questions, and the switch log said `committed: null`. Before its load commits, a script fails at once with an error, so a question still pending means a document is there. Now its late answer counts once it comes: `timeout` says the document is there, and `painted` ends the wait. A busy page that has painted is introduced at about 1.4 s in the unit test (HEAD 2.9 s, before this fix 5.4 s). One that paints nothing is shown at the usual limit, not 5 s.
  - **A document that arrives late gets time to paint.** Once the document answered, the wait's end dropped to 1.5 s from the load's start, already past for a page whose server answered at 3 s. It was shown about 100 ms after its commit with nothing drawn. Now a document gets `COMMITTED_PAINT_MS` (1 s) from its arrival to paint, still within the hard limit. A document there within 0.5 s of its load's start is waited for as before. A page whose document came at 1.9 s and never paints is now shown at about 3.3 s, not 2.5 s.
  - **A wake that ran out of time calls for a fresh paint barrier.** S6 stopped the wake's answer from counting as the last step, so a wake answering after the barrier doesn't cost a second one. That is right for a wake that answered. One that timed out shows the page was busy with a task of its own after the barrier. The show then went out 400 ms after the last proof that the page drew. Now only an answered wake is left out.
  - **The arrival cover stays through a preparation called off for time.** A preparation past its budget was cancelled just before the show, and the cancel lifted the page's arrival cover. The show's first paint was then the page's own, maybe heavy, not the cover's. Now that cancel keeps the cover (`keepCover`), and the show lifts it as for any page arriving covered. A cancel because the switch failed or ran out of time still lifts it (`coverKeptFor` in content/core.js). The deadline check now runs before that cancel, so a switch that expires lifts the cover too. With that, the 300 ms budget stays.
  - **The arrival cover's 3 s limit counts again from the preparation.** It goes on as the document first answers, so its limit (`ARRIVAL_MAX_MS`) started up to 1–1.5 s earlier than before. Now a cover asked for again (the preparation, asked covered, after the introduction) restarts its limit. No extra script.
  - **Still parsing, a scrap of the page's own isn't painted.** The relaxed readiness took one word of text as the page's content. WebKit itself, while a document is still parsing, waits for over 200 characters or over 32 × 32 px of drawn content before its first visually non-empty layout (LocalFrameView `visualCharacterThreshold`, `visualPixelThreshold`). `readyInPage` now asks the same while `readyState` is `loading`, so a site's header alone doesn't count. Once parsed, it's as before.
- **Not changed after review:**
  - **The early arrival cover stays.** A reviewer asked whether it only moves the gain behind the spinner, since the page's first paint then comes after the show. Measured in the WebKit host, click to the cover lifting (the page in view), 3 runs each: `unloadedlate` 421–424 ms on HEAD's build, 350–355 with this one, 350–361 with the cover asked at the introduction instead; `shelllate` 708–761, 580–590, 597–612. The show to the lift was about 110 ms in all three. So the gain is real in the host, and the early cover is worth 0–20 ms of it. The host's pages are light, so TESTING.md asks Tirth to look for a late lift on heavy pages. Also noted: WebKit counts no visually non-empty layout while `<body>` has opacity 0, so for such a page it comes after the show. Nothing seen depends on it.
  - **No slower asking past 2.5 s.** Asking every 500 ms after 2 s with no document would save about 9 of the 33 questions to a dead server's tab, but it found a late commit up to 300 ms later. Two older tests caught that (a server answering at 3 s). The questions run at one every 200 ms past the first 0.5 s, the rate already accepted for the first 2.5 s.
  - **Introductions turned off mid-restore still show the tab at once.** A reviewer suggested waiting for the document then. But a blink of the page in front ends that page's own switch cover (shared/switching.js: hidden ends the switch), so waiting would leave the page uncovered with no sign of the switch for up to 5 s. It happens at most once per Safari session (introductions stay off after it). The failed `unloadedlate` host run (above) was this.
- **Verified after review:** unit tests 427 (5 new, 2 changed). The new tests: a wake that times out after the barrier gets a second barrier just before the show; a document arriving at 3 s and painting at 3.5 s is shown painted; a busy page that has painted is introduced on its late answer, and one that paints nothing is shown at the usual limit, both with `committed` set; a preparation past its time is cancelled with its cover kept; a header alone, still parsing, isn't painted. The S6 still-parsing test now draws a paragraph, not one word. The slow-server test from bed58e8 now expects 1 s after the commit, not 2.5 s from the click. All five new tests fail on the S6 code before these fixes. WebKit host `run.sh all`: 65 of 67 passed. `press` failed the same check ("the release asked for no second switch") 3 of 3 alone on this build and 3 of 3 on HEAD's build, so it's not this change. `introduce` hit the host's Trace/BPT crash, 1 of 3 alone here and 2 of 3 on HEAD's build.

## A hidden tab's changes are written together (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** lower CPU use in Safari and Chrome, with nothing slower.
- **Why:** every write of Margin's state reaches every open page as a `storage.onChanged` event. In the round-3 measurements each one cost about 1 ms in each hidden page, about 4.6 ms in the page in front and about 4 ms in Safari's UI process. A page Safari suspended gets the events it missed when it resumes, which is on the way of a switch to it. Each tab event that changed the state was written on its own. A hidden tab loading a page sends its address, title, icon and load one after another, so 3–5 writes. A chat or timer whose title ticks every second wrote once a second for as long as it ran: about 40 ms/s across 30 tabs (estimated).
- **Changed:**
  - `store.js mutate` takes `within(state)`: how long the write may wait. The change goes into the state at once, in order with every other change. Only its write waits. It goes out with the first write after it of any kind, or when its time is up. So a write made later (a switch, a tab opened or closed, a pin) always carries every change made before it. Nothing is reordered, and a closed tab can't come back from an older update.
  - New `background/tab-updates.js` decides the wait for `tabs.onUpdated` (title, address, icon, a page loaded). A tab Margin has behind waits 300 ms (`GATHER_MS`), so a page loading behind is two writes: its new address at once (below), then its title, icon and load together. Its title alone is written at most once every 2 s (`TITLE_EVERY_MS`), per tab. The tab in front, and a tab Margin doesn't know yet, are written at once. So are a tab Safari says is active while Margin has it behind, or the other way around: either one saying it's in front is enough.
  - A hidden tab's new address is written at once too, whether it comes in the event or only in the tab with its load (a redirect). After a Safari restart or a Margin update every tab has a new id, and Margin matches tabs to their records by address (`model.js matchTabs`). A new address lost with the background stopping would leave the tab unmatched, in its window's active space instead of its own. A new fragment alone still waits: matching ignores it, and some sites change it often.
