  - The waits are counted with `performance.now()`, a clock that only runs forward. With `Date.now()`, the Mac's clock set back (by hand, or synced after a sleep) held a title back by as much, and one set forward kept a later change waiting for an earlier, longer wait.
  - Activation, new tabs, closed tabs and every other change write at once, as before. A change with nothing new no longer writes the waiting changes early.
  - `stateRev` (what the background passes when it prepares a page) still names the last write while only changes that can wait are waiting. Storage has the write before them, the same as the page, so a read would get the same thing. Once their write is on its way (its time is up, or another write takes them), no write is known until it's through, as before.
  - Chrome: the same. It has the same storage events and hidden tabs that load.
- **Expected:** a page loading behind costs two writes, not 3–5. A ticking title costs one write per 2 s, not one per change. Each write saved is about 1 ms in each hidden page plus about 9 ms in the page in front and in Safari, and less for a suspended page to catch up on when it's shown.
- **Risks:**
  - A hidden tab's row shows a new title up to about 2.3 s late, and a new icon or fragment up to 300 ms late.
  - A background that stops with a write waiting loses it: a title, an icon, a fragment or a load, never an address. At its next start it syncs with the open tabs (`resync`), which writes them again, and Safari sends titles and icons again as tabs load after a restart. Safari stops a background after much longer than 2 s without events.
  - Timers in the background can fire late. In the WebKit host the 2 s write came about 0.7 s late once. The wait is then longer, never shorter, and any other write still takes the changes along.
- **Verified:** unit tests 434 (7 new, in `tests/tab-updates.test.js`): changes of a hidden tab gathered into one write, the title alone written at most every 2 s, the tab in front and an unknown tab written at once, a switch carrying the waiting change in its own write with nothing written after, a tab closed with a change waiting staying closed, a change with nothing new not writing the waiting ones, and `stateRev` while changes wait, while a write is on its way and after a failed one. With HEAD's `store.js` (it ignores `within`), 6 of the 7 fail. These mutations each fail a test: `within` ignored, the old `stateRev`, an empty change writing the waiting ones, a waiting key not marked on its way by a write made at once, no title limit, the tab in front waiting. Two guards don't fail a test: a waiting change behind a failed write is already covered by the failed write's own unknown state, and clearing the timer in `flush` only saves an empty wake. New WebKit host scenario `titles`: a hidden tab's title set 8 times in half a second. This build: 2 writes, and the row ends on the last title. HEAD's build: 8 writes. The tab in front's title shows in about 110 ms in both. `run.sh all`: 66 of 68 passed (load average 3–8). The host crashed in `introduce` (Trace/BPT trap), which passed alone. `press` failed its late checks. Run alone at load average 5–10 it failed 5 of 5 times on this build and 3 of 4 on HEAD's, with the switch landing 0.4–3 s after the press on both (near the host's background stopping). A switch asked from the page at 1.5 s, 3 runs of each build alternated, took 13–17 ms on this build and 13–22 ms on HEAD's, each preparation `known: 1`. So the press failures follow the load and the host's timing, not this change.
- **Review:**
  - **Clock set back (fixed).** The title limit counted with `Date.now()`. With the clock set back 60 s, a hidden tab's title waited about 60 s. It now uses `performance.now()`, and so does `store.js` for the delayed write's own time. With `Date.now()` there, a clock set forward kept a 300 ms change waiting behind a 2 s one.
  - **Address lost on a quit or update (fixed).** A scratch check of `syncTabs` with a renumbered tab: a stored address that's current keeps the tab in its space, and a stale one puts it in the window's active space. Before this slice the address was written within milliseconds. A hidden tab's new address is now written at once (see Changed). A page loading behind costs two writes instead of one.
  - **Tests that time real timers (fixed).** With a shim that fires `store.js`'s timers 200 ms late, 4 of the 7 tests failed on a correct build. They now wait for an expected write for up to 1.5 s. Checks that nothing is written yet stay timed, because a late timer can only make them pass. The tests pass with timers 200 ms and 500 ms late.
- **Verified after review:** unit tests 436 (9 in `tests/tab-updates.test.js`, 2 new: the clock set back and forward, and a hidden tab's new address). Both new tests fail on the code before these fixes, and 7 of 9 fail with HEAD's `store.js`. These mutations each fail a test: `Date.now()` for the title limit, `Date.now()` for the delayed write's time, no address rule, comparing raw addresses (a fragment written at once), the address taken from the event only, no title limit, the tab in front waiting. New WebKit host scenario `addresses`: a hidden tab's `pushState` is stored at once and a new fragment about 300 ms later. It passed 3 of 3 on this build and failed 2 of 2 on a build without the address rule. That build stored the address at about 330 ms, which a first, looser version of the check let through. The host looks every 100 ms, so the check now allows about 200 ms. `run.sh all`: 67 of 69 passed. `unloadedlate` failed once and passed alone 2 of 2 on this build and on HEAD's. `press` failed the same checks as before ("in front with the button still down", "the release asked for no second switch") 2 of 2 alone on both builds.

## Cheap page fixes: style reads not repeated, lists that don't grow, the zoom kept (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** lower CPU and memory use in the tab in front, in Safari and Chrome.
- **Why:** the round-3 code audit found four small costs that repeat.
  - `layout.js syncPlaceholder` read the style of `<html>::after` on every render, to see if the first-paint placeholder was there. A style read makes the page work out its styles first if anything changed. With the placeholder there, the first read switches it off and the reads stop. With none, they never stop: in Chrome's full-screen-only mode (Chrome's default, where nothing is registered), and in Safari with no frame and no Keep open.
  - `in-view.js outsideBody` walks from an observed element up to `<body>`, reading each one's style. It runs every time a page's IntersectionObserver hears different answers from the window and from `<body>`, which is every threshold crossing of anything fixed. The audit counted 5,760 style reads in 240 frames on an app layout.
  - `handlers.js getZoom` read `pageZoom` from storage and asked for the tab's zoom settings on every ask. Pages ask as they start, on every resize and every time they're shown.
  - `media.js` lists, `in-view.js` stand-ins and `sheets.js` moved `@media` rules were only cleared of what the page dropped when the box rested or `<body>` changed. On a page that makes many and keeps the box still, they grew for as long as the tab lived. `sheets.js` holds its rules strongly, so the rules of replaced sheets stayed in memory too.
- **Changed:**
  - `syncPlaceholder` asks once per `<html>`, then at most every 5 s (`PLACEHOLDER_RECHECK_MS`). It also asks at the first render after the page is hidden and when it's shown. A render in sight within the 5 s asks once they're over. It still asks again because WebKit applies newly registered CSS to pages already open: in the host, a page opened before Margin was installed had the placeholder after the install. A Margin update can register it after its pages have started. When the settings the placeholder is registered from change (Keep open, the frame, the width, the theme, full screen only), the page sets `data-margin-docked="off"` without asking. The new placeholder would arrive after the change.
  - `outsideBody` keeps each element's answer for 1 s (`OUTSIDE_FOR_MS`), in a WeakMap. It forgets all of them when the box moves, comes on or goes off, or `<body>` changes. A target whose answer came from a kept walk is chosen again once that walk runs out, and the page hears it if that changed what it was told. So something that turns fixed, a sticky header say, gets the right answer within a second, even if nothing crosses a threshold again.
  - `getZoom` keeps the stored `pageZoom` and each tab's default zoom in the background for up to 60 s (`ZOOM_KEEP_MS`). An unchanged zoom costs only the browser's answer. It forgets the stored zoom when `pageZoom` changes (`storage.onChanged`, or its own write), and the default zooms when the browser reports a zoom change (`tabs.onZoomChange`, where the browser has it). A read that started before a change isn't kept. What's kept only tells that nothing changed. A write reads storage first and builds on that. The 60 s limit is for copies of Margin, which don't hear each other's writes. Both listeners run in every copy, not only the one in charge, so a copy that takes charge again starts from what's stored. The times use `performance.now()`, a clock that only runs forward. The zoom itself is still asked of the browser every time.
  - The three lists are also swept whenever they hold twice as many as after their last sweep, starting at 64. The sweep drops what the page let go (`WeakRef`s), and rules no longer in a sheet on the page. It's the same test as when the box rests.
  - Chrome: the same code. Its full-screen-only mode gains most from the placeholder change.
- **Expected:** fewer forced style updates in the tab in front: one placeholder read per 5 s at most instead of one per render where there's no placeholder, and one walk per fixed element per second instead of one per threshold crossing. No storage read for a zoom ask that changed nothing. Lists that stay small on long-lived app tabs.
- **Risks:**
  - A site that strips `data-margin-docked` from `<html>` shows the placeholder's frame until a render, and then for up to 5 s more if the page is in sight (before: until the next render).
  - A placeholder registered late reaches a page in sight up to 5 s after the render that would have caught it. A hidden page catches it when it's shown.
  - An element that turns fixed, or stops being fixed, may get the wrong root for up to about 1 s: it can be reported in view or out of view for that long.
  - A copy of Margin that writes `pageZoom` while another copy keeps it can skip a write for up to 60 s: the other copy's kept map says nothing changed. A later ask after that writes it. Pages then draw Margin at an old zoom until the browser's answer, a moment later, as they do on a first visit. No write loses another copy's sites.
- **Verified:** unit tests 442 (6 new: 5 in `tests/page-upkeep.test.js`, against a small fake of the DOM, and 1 in `tests/spaces-switch.test.js`). All 6 fail on HEAD's code, and the existing zoom test now names the zoom change it simulates. These mutations each fail a test: no forgetting on a box move, no age limit on the walk, no `off` on a settings change, no recheck after 5 s, the placeholder answer not tied to its `<html>`, no sweep in `sheets.js`, `zoomChanged` or `zoomStored` doing nothing. WebKit host, scratch scenarios, 2 runs of each build, alternated with HEAD's build:
  - A fixed bar of 40 observed items crossing the window's edge every frame for 3 s: 8,648 style reads on HEAD's build, 264 on this one, with the same 181 callbacks and 3,982 entries for the page. The page's styles were clean when the observer ran, so the reads themselves took 2–4 ms in all on HEAD's build (at the page clock's 1 ms resolution). The saving is larger on a page whose styles change between reads.
  - 200 sidebar opens with no placeholder registered (copies of both builds with the placeholder files emptied): 200 placeholder reads on HEAD's build, 0 on this one. Opening took about 20 ms for the 200 on both, on a small test page.
  - 40 `getZoom` asks: under 1 ms each on both builds. The host's storage is fast, so the saving doesn't show there.
  - `run.sh all` (load average 4–5): 66 of 69 passed. `unseennav` and `slowunloaded` failed once and passed alone on this build and on HEAD's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds, as in the slices before.
- **Review:**
  - **A kept walk could leave a page's answer wrong for good (fixed).** The kept answer is only looked at when a new entry comes. Something walked while in the flow, then floated with `position: fixed` within the second, would be told it's out of view, and nothing new would come while it stays in view. The reverse could also happen. A target whose answer came from a kept walk is now chosen again as that walk runs out (one timer per observer), and the page hears it if the answer changed. The review's unit scenario failed before the fix and passes now. In the host the scenario's first step didn't happen: `<body>` scrolls, so it clips an element in the flow for the window's observer too, and the two observers agreed there. The fix stays because it costs at most the walk the second saved. The host's fixed-bar measure still makes 264 style reads, the same as before the fix.
  - **A zoom write could drop another copy's sites (fixed).** The write was built from the kept `pageZoom`. Another copy's write within the 60 s, which this copy never hears of, was lost for good. A write now reads storage first and builds on that. The kept map only answers "nothing changed". It can still skip a write for up to 60 s (see Risks). A write costs one more storage read.
  - **A late placeholder was missed when the page was shown (fixed).** A page restored behind after a relaunch or an update can render before Safari brings its registrations back. Shown within 5 s, its show render skipped the check, so the placeholder's frame stayed on the page. It's now asked again at the first render after the page is hidden, when it's shown, and, for a page in sight, once the 5 s are over. In the host the registrations were there with the first render, after an install and after a relaunch, so this is covered by unit tests only.
  - **Verified after review:** unit tests 444 (2 new: the floated player in `tests/page-upkeep.test.js`, a placeholder registered late, shown, prepared hidden and in sight; the zoom test gains another copy's write). Each new check fails on the code before these fixes. These mutations each fail a test: no recheck of a kept walk, no fresh read before a zoom write, no reset when hidden or shown, no ask when shown, no ask once the 5 s are over, the late ask scheduled while hidden. `run.sh all` (load average 3–4): 66 of 69 passed. `behindlate` stopped with the host's Trace/BPT crash and passed alone 2 of 2. `slowunloaded` ("shown once it had painted") failed alone 4 of 4 on this build and 3 of 4 on HEAD's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds.

## Round 3's last fixes: a restarted background knows its tabs, a tab resumed behind is kicked, holds of the tab in front aren't lost (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari, no dark frames, less CPU and memory, with no new problems.
- **Why:** the reviews of round 3 found four real gaps, plus three small ones.
  - **A restarted background didn't kick.** Safari stops Margin's idle background within a minute. When a tab was left (`keepReadyLeftAt`) and which holds run (`keepReadyHolds`) are kept in storage.session for that reason. But only keep-ready's tick read them, 1.5 s after the first sync. The click that starts a stopped background is often to a tab left minutes ago, which Safari has likely suspended. That switch found nothing known about the tab, so it wasn't kicked and didn't use the stuck fallback. It waited 400 ms for the wake and up to 900 ms for the preparation, about 0.9 s, and then showed dark if the resume was slower. Two reviewers found this separately.
  - **A tab resumed behind was shown dark.** A hover wake, a press, the palette, a key run's tab woken ahead or keep-ready's first hold can resume a suspended tab at background priority before the switch. Its wake then answers, so the switch took the tab as awake: no kick, no paint barrier, and the preparation cut at 250 ms. In Tirth's Safari log such tabs still had their content hidden at the selection though they had resumed 0.9–2.6 s before. They showed Safari's empty view for 141–360 ms. Only a visible state clears that hide, which is what the kick gives.
  - **A hold that reached its page once the tab was in front was lost.** A first hold sent to a suspended tab waits behind its resume. A click shows the tab first, so the hold starts on a visible page and doesn't end (keepAwake only ends on the page coming into view, not on finding it there). The next tick, with the tab still in front, threw its key away. Once the tab was left, nothing could name that hold, and it ran its 75 s at foreground priority outside the six.
  - Small ones. A wake whose permission check took longer than 400 ms (Safari starting) turned the kick and the stuck fallback off for that switch. When tabs were left was kept for closed tabs too while keep-ready was off, and written whole at each switch. And the tab left is taken from the state, which can lag behind an activation queued behind a write: then the tab really left kept an older leave time, and looked suspended when it had been in front a second before.
- **Changed:**
  - keep-ready reads storage.session as the background starts (`sessionKnown`), and a switch waits for that read first, 200 ms at most. It's one read per background run, and nothing once it has answered. Chrome reads nothing (it keeps no tabs ready).
  - A wake answered more than 60 ms after its scripts went out marks its tab (`resumedBehind`): its process had been suspended. A switch to a marked tab kicks it even though its wake says it's awake, then gives it the paint barrier as any kicked tab. The mark goes when the tab is shown, kicked or introduced. A switch's own late wake was already kicked. The switch log's `kicked` has `behind: true` for these.
  - keep-ready keeps the sent, unexpired hold keys of the tab in front, in place of forgetting them. They're named once the tab is behind and not kept: at the next tick, or when keep-ready stops (turned off, or Safari unused for the window), as the next switch's tick stops again.
  - A wake still in its permission check at 400 ms counts as unanswered: the switch kicks the tab, or shows it 150 ms later.
  - Closed tabs' leave times are dropped at every tick, also with keep-ready off.
  - A tab shown counts as left no earlier than its show. A missed leave can only make a tab look hidden for less time than it was, so it waits the 60 ms before a kick instead of being kicked at once.
- **Not changed:** keepAwake still doesn't end when it starts on a visible page. The hold Margin places on the tab just left can reach the page before its visibility change does, and would then end at once, losing the most useful hold. The tab left is still taken from the state, so `holdNow` can hold the wrong tab in that race (as before round 3). A keep-ready first hold that resumed a tab behind, followed by a quick wake there, isn't marked: no answer comes back from a hold until it ends.
- **Risks:**
  - A busy page that answers a hover wake late, though its process was running, is kicked on the next switch: a flip, a second wake and a barrier, about 100–300 ms slower, the same as a switch whose own wake is late.
  - The first switch after a background start waits for the session read, a few ms normally and 200 ms at most.
  - A key kept for the tab in front stays until the tab is left and a tick runs. It isn't sent anywhere while the tab is in front.
- **Verified:** unit tests 451 (7 new, in `tests/wake.test.js` and `tests/keep-ready.test.js`). Each fails when its fix is undone: no wait for the session read, no kick for a tab resumed behind, the slow permission check taken as an answer, the active tab's keys dropped (2 tests), no prune with keep-ready off, and no lower bound at a show. The wake tests' fake storage.session now reads several keys at once, as Safari's does. `run.sh all` (load average about 4): 67 of 69 passed. `unseen` failed once and passed alone 2 of 2 on this build and on bed58e8's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds, as in the slices before. Round 3 against bed58e8's build in the host, interleaved, medians of 4–5 runs: 10 hidden pages still held 2 s after a burst of 10 switches before, 6 now. Restores came up at 243 ms against 308. Loaded and never-shown switches were at the host's floor on both (11 and 82 ms against 12 and 85). Scripts per switch: 4 for a single switch on both, 4.4 against 4.0 in a burst (the releases). A hidden tab ticking its title every 250 ms: 2 state writes in 3.3 s against 7. The host can't suspend processes, so the kick paths are covered by unit tests only.

## No more asking pages for their animations (2026-11-03)

- **Why:** Tirth's Safari crashed the tab in front three times on 2026-11-02 (10:39, 11:37, 22:18), each time with the same signature: EXC_BAD_ACCESS in WebKit's `compareStyleOriginatedAnimationOwningElementPositionsInDocumentTreeOrder`, under `compareAnimationsByCompositeOrder`, `Document::matchingAnimations` and `document.getAnimations()`. The calls came from a requestAnimationFrame callback twice and from a dispatched event once, the two ways Margin reads `<body>` (`content/page/box.js`). The screen went dark for about 1.8 s while Safari reloaded the page. The signature first shows after 0973822, which added `document.getAnimations()` to every read. `Element.getAnimations()`, used to see whether a length was on its way, goes through the same sort. The bug is WebKit's, but Margin's calls reached it.
- **Changed:**
  - Nothing in `extension/` calls `getAnimations` any more. A unit test fails if anything does.
  - **What's on its way is known from events** (`content/page/moves.js`). `transitionrun` and `animationstart` on `<html>`, `<body>` and the first children add to a list. What's on it counts if it moves something a read looks at. Their `transitionend`, `transitioncancel`, `animationend` and `animationcancel` take it off. A read with something on the list waits, and runs again once the list is empty, as before. Listeners are on the window, in the capture phase, and only trusted events count.
  - An event comes a frame after its transition started, so a read can miss one that has just started. Whatever starts is read again as it ends.
  - An animation counts only if it has an end, runs, and its `@keyframes` set something a read looks at. The keyframes are looked up in the sheets the page can read. If none has them (another site's sheet), the animation counts.
  - What left the page drops off the list. So does anything older than 10 s, in case its end never comes.
  - **A first child that has just come** is counted from its first start. Starts are kept for anything up to 8 levels under `<body>`, as deep as the first children go, and counted once a read looks at the element. A banner put first in `<body>` in a frame's callbacks starts moving in the next frame's events, before Margin's next look at the first children. Before this, its start was thrown away, the read caught its margin part way, and the page's top margin stayed lost (review, both engines).
  - What ends is read again if a read looks at it by then, so one found by a read itself on its way is read again too. An animation no longer named in `animation-name` doesn't count.
  - **A read's own transitions** in what inherits from `<body>` are now seen as events in the next frame. Each comes as a `transitionrun` and, at 0 s, a `transitioncancel` (Chromium) or a `transitionend` (WebKit), for the same element and property, in the frame after a read. A page's own transition never does that. Such a pair isn't the page moving, so nothing is read again for it. It sets `held` in both engines, so later reads start none. Before, `held` was set in the read itself. Without this, WebKit read again at each of those ends, each read started the same transitions, and a page with `margin: inherit` and a transition on a first child was read 5 times a second for as long as it was open (review).
  - **What no event tells of**: an animation a script starts with `element.animate()` sends none. A read the page's own changes asked for, which found something new the page says, is made once more 1 s later. If that one finds something new again, another follows, 3 at most in a row. A read for a move of the box or a resize starts none: the box's width is left out of what counts as new. Before, a script's slide of `<body>`'s margin or of a new first child's was read at its start and kept (review).
  - **The page's style is brought up to date before a read**, with `checkVisibility()` on `<html>`. `document.getAnimations()` did that as a side effect. Without it, WebKit restyled what was waiting under `<body>` with the read's elements in, and not again once they were gone. The host's flex column (`body-size` F and O) kept its children shrunk. `getComputedStyle` isn't enough: it only brings the element asked for and its ancestors up to date. On 20,000 rows `checkVisibility()` cost the same as `getAnimations()` (25 ms in WebKit and 11 in Chromium with style to redo, 0 without) and no layout (59 and 27 ms).
- **Checked:**
  - Headless WebKit and Chromium, the page bundle at HEAD and now, side by side: `<body>`'s margin in a transition lands at 300, a margin animation at 120, both builds and both engines. A child with `padding: inherit` and a transition: HEAD's Chromium build sends it 8 transition events on the first late read and none after. This build sends none in either engine.
  - Host, before the review's fixes: `late-body`, `read-limits`, `late-reads`, `read-shape` and `body-size` all pass. `body-size` failed F and O before the style update was added, and passed on HEAD's build.
  - The review's pages, headless, before and after its fixes, in both engines. A first child put in by a frame's callback, or in a task with its style forced, sliding its margin in by an animation or a transition: the gap above the page was lost (0 px) and is now kept (40 px, as at HEAD). `margin: inherit` and a transition on a first child, in WebKit: 25 reads in 5 idle seconds before, 1 now (the settling read), none after. A script's `animate()` of `<body>`'s margin, and of a new first child's: read part way before, read at its end now.
- **Risks:**
  - A transition started in the same frame as a read is read once at its start and again at its end. That's one more read than before for such a transition.
  - A transition of a length on `<html>`, `<body>` or a first child is followed by a read as it ends, even if no read ran during it. Rare on those elements.
  - On a page that inherits from `<body>` in Chromium, if a child's transition from an earlier change is still running when the first late read comes, that transition plays out instead of jumping to its end. Before, `held` was set within the read.
  - An animation whose play state is changed from paused to running later isn't seen, since no new `animationstart` comes.
  - A read the page asked for that found something new costs one more read a second later, sometimes up to 3. A script's animation of these lengths that runs longer than about 3 s is still read part way.
  - Starts on anything up to 8 levels under `<body>` are kept until they end. Endless animations among them are swept once more than 64 elements are on the list, or as a read looks.
  - A page that stops transition events at the window before Margin's listener (only possible when Margin is injected late) holds reads back up to 10 s.
- **Verified:** unit tests 461 (10 in `tests/page-moves.test.js`). The check that nothing calls `getAnimations` fails on HEAD's `box.js`, and the rest fail on HEAD, where `moves.js` doesn't exist. The review's three (a first child that has just come, a read's own transitions, the settling read) fail on the version reviewed. Host, alone: `read-limits`, `late-reads`, `read-shape` and `body-size` pass. `read-limits` P failed while a move of the box could start a settling read, so the box's width is left out of what counts as new. `late-body` fails M, O and T "in its box at 360" alone on this build, on the version reviewed and on HEAD's (the sidebar width change never comes), and passes within `run.sh all`. `run.sh all` (load average about 3): 67 of 69 passed. `update` A and `catchup-loaded` B failed. Run alone, both passed on this build and on the version reviewed, `catchup-loaded` 5 of 5 on this build.

## Keep-ready made light: the tab just left, for a minute (2026-11-03)

- **Asked (Tirth):** with keep-ready at 0 he sees big black flashes. He wants them gone without giving up battery and memory.
- **Why:** his Safari on 2026-11-02 (round 4's diagnosis). Keep-ready held about six heavy hidden tabs at foreground priority all the time: up to six tabs of the space in front, renewed every minute until 30 minutes after Safari was last used. Any click, key or scroll renewed the 30 minutes. Held hidden tabs used 60–70% of all page CPU, about 22% of a core each, and his battery went from 45% to 6% in an hour. With keep-ready off, every switch back lands on a tab Safari has suspended, and that shows dark. Earlier rounds showed tabs held awake were never dark (0 in about 60), and most returns are to a tab used a moment ago. So the hold is worth keeping, but only for the tab you're most likely to go back to, and only for a short time.
  - Two smaller problems: the keep-ready alarm started Margin's background once a minute in his other Safari profiles for nothing. And a restarted background didn't know the holds the one before had placed, so it held six more beside them.
- **Changed** (`background/keep-ready.js`, `background.js`, `background/handlers.js`, `shared/away.js`, the new-tab page's Settings):
  - Only the tab just left is held, once, for the setting's minutes (default 1, at most 10). Nothing is renewed. The space's other tabs aren't held, and a background start holds nothing.
  - At most 2 are held: the tab just left and the one left before it. A third lets go of the one left longest ago at once. Which one goes is decided by when each tab was left (the tab events), not by the state's last use. The state can lag behind a run of quick switches. A tab let go is released even if the state still shows it in front, as long as the tab events say it was left. In the host, a fast run of switches otherwise kept a tab two switches back running as if it were in front.
  - The tab left is taken from the activations as they come (`frontChanged`), not from the state. The state's `active` lags while an activation's write waits behind the one under way. Through A → B → C in quick succession it still had A in front at C's activation, so A was held a second time and B, the tab just left, never was. The state is used only for a window this background hasn't seen a switch in (it has just started). A tab closed or moved to another window is no longer in front of its own.
  - When Safari is left for another app, every hold ends 3 s later, and none is placed until Safari is used again. Coming back doesn't hold anything again. Two signals say Safari was left:
    - `windows.onFocusChanged` with `WINDOW_ID_NONE`. WebKit sends this when the browser tells it no window has focus (`WebExtensionContext::didFocusWindow(nullptr)`, checked in WebKit's source). Whether Safari does that when it's deactivated isn't known.
    - The page in front loses focus while it's still in sight (`shared/away.js`: `blur`, then `document.hasFocus()` false and the page visible). It sends `away` once, and `back` when it gets focus again. A page hidden as it loses focus doesn't count, since that's a switch to another tab.
    - With focus in a frame of the page (Google Docs, an embedded editor, a payment form), leaving Safari blurs only that frame's window, and Margin's scripts run in the top frame only. So while focus is in a frame, the page asks `document.hasFocus()` once a second, and says `away` when it turns false. After `away` it asks the same way for up to 5 s, so focus coming back inside a frame still sends `back`. Past that, the next switch says Safari is in use anyway.
    - A page's `away` comes through more hops than a window's focus or an activation, so it can arrive after them, and then nothing would cancel its 3 s wait. It carries when the page lost focus. It doesn't count if Safari was seen in use after that time (a tab switch), if it comes from a window other than the one focused last (a click into another Safari window), or from a tab no longer in front.
    - The 3 s wait covers a glance at another app and straight back, and a `WINDOW_ID_NONE` that may come between two Safari windows. A window focused, a tab switch or `back` cancels it.
  - The 'used' messages are gone (a page sent one at most once a minute on a click, key or scroll). Nothing needs to know when Safari was last used any more.
  - No keep-ready alarm. Nothing is renewed, so nothing needs to wake the background. If an alarm from the old build still fires, it's cleared, in any copy or profile.
  - A background that starts again reads the holds still running from `storage.session`. They count toward the 2 and are released with the rest.
  - A closed tab is forgotten at once (`tabs.onRemoved`): its holds, when it was left, and whether it had a page.
  - **The setting:** `keepTabsReadyMinutes` is now `keepLeftTabReadyMinutes`. It's labelled "Keep the tab you just left ready to show, with no flash, for this long (minutes, 0 = off; let go when you leave Safari)", from 0 to 10. A stored old value doesn't carry over, not even 0: the new one is 1 until it's saved. Tirth set the old one to 0 to stop what it cost, and asked for the flashes gone without that cost, which is what the new one is. Keeping his 0 would have kept every flash. Pages read the settings as stored, so they apply the same default (`shared/view.js` `keepReadyMinutes`), and Settings shows what the background does. A downgrade to an older build would see no old setting and turn keep-ready on at 30.
- **Expected:** at most 2 hidden tabs held, each for a minute after it was left, and none while Safari isn't in front or once switching stops. Before, about 6 were held all the time. Battery and CPU should be close to keep-ready off. Switching straight back to the tab just left, or to the one before it, within a minute should show no flash. A tab left longer ago than that is suspended, and switching to it is the kick and paint path, as with keep-ready off.
- **Not changed:** the kick, the stuck path and the space switcher (other slices). Hover wakes still hold the tab under the pointer for their own few seconds.
- **Risks:**
  - Clicking into Safari's own address bar or a Safari sheet also blurs the page. If focus doesn't come back to the page within 3 s, the holds end, and going back to the tab just left can flash.
  - A page's `away` is ignored when it comes from a window other than the one focused last. That relies on `windows.onFocusChanged` with window ids, which Safari may not send. Without it, a click into another Safari window can still end the holds of the first one 3 s later.
  - With focus in a frame, leaving Safari is noticed up to a second late, and a page that keeps focus in a frame runs a 1 s check while it's in front.
  - If Safari doesn't send `WINDOW_ID_NONE`, and the window is hidden (⌘H, minimized) without the page seeing a blur while it's visible, the holds run out their minute.
  - A second tab held costs one more hidden process at foreground priority for the minute after a switch. Set `MAX_HELD` to 1 if his log shows going back two tabs is rare.
  - A tab more than two switches back, or left more than a minute ago, flashes when switched to, as with keep-ready off.
- **Verified:** unit tests 452 (21 in `tests/keep-ready.test.js`, rewritten for the new behaviour; 1 new in `tests/model.test.js`, which also checks the pages' reading against the background's; 2 in the new `tests/away.test.js`). Against HEAD's code (with names added for the new calls), 13 of the keep-ready tests fail: only the tab left held, its length, never renewed, at most two, no alarm, when Safari is left (3), a restarted background, a closed tab, the switch log facts and the two stale-state cases. The other 8 are behaviour kept from before and pass on both. The model test and `away.test.js` fail on HEAD. Mutations each fail a test: no `heldUntil` from storage.session, no 3 s wait, holding while away, three held, a closed tab keeping its leave time, away releasing nothing, the state's `active` trusted, and the state's last use for the order. WebKit host: `keep-ready-cap` changed to two held of nine switches, and a new `keep-ready-away`. Both pass, but under a load average of 13–24 they sometimes failed: a release landed 1.5–3 s late, near the end of the host's ~5 s of background life. HEAD's `keep-ready-cap` never released anything in that run (the switches before the first sync aren't held, so it held 4 of 6), so it can't be compared. The release checks now wait 3 s. `run.sh all` (load average 4–8): 68 of 70 passed. `unseennav` failed once and passed alone 2 of 2 on this build and on HEAD's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds, as in the slices before. After the last changes, `keep-ready-cap` and `keep-ready-away` passed alone. The host can't suspend processes or take focus away, so CPU, battery and the real focus signals are for Tirth's Safari (TESTING.md).
- **Review fixes, verified:** unit tests 456. New: the tab left from the activations with the state behind, a page's `away` that doesn't count (three cases), the frame watch (2 in `tests/away.test.js`), and the old setting's 0 not carried over. Each fails on the version reviewed. With the state's `active`, the quick-switch test held tab 1 twice and tab 2 never. Each of the three `away` checks is the only one that rejects its case in the test. `keep-ready-away` now also sends `away` from a tab behind, 1.2 s before the real one: it passes on this build and fails "still held 2 s after Safari was left" on the reviewed version's (MARGIN_HOST_DIST). `run.sh all` (load average 3–9): 69 of 70 passed, `keep-ready-cap` and `keep-ready-away` among them. `press` failed "in front with the button still down" and "the release asked for no second switch" alone on this build and on HEAD's (git archive), as before.

## Wake on intent, and show a sleeping tab only once it's awake and drawn (2026-11-03)

- **Asked (Tirth):** with keep-ready at 0 they see big black flashes on switches to tabs Safari has put to sleep. They want the flashes gone without giving up CPU and memory.
- **Why:** their Safari on 2026-11-02 (round 4's diagnosis).
  - The kick (round 3: the tab brought to the front and the page in front straight back, for a foreground resume) never landed in one run loop turn. The other tab was on screen for 11–148 ms each time, and its content was still hidden in 112 of 131 flips. It didn't get heavy swapped-out tabs drawn either.
  - With no answer 150 ms after the kick, the `stuck` path showed the tab anyway. Heavy tabs (0.75–1.3 GB, mostly swapped out) then showed dark for about 1.2 s.
  - Earlier rounds: tabs woken by Margin's script before the show and shown after a paint barrier came up clean in 25 of 29 cases. Tabs held awake were never dark. Safari's own tab bar onto a sleeping tab was dark for 300–550 ms. Margin's script resumes a process at background priority, a median of about 710 ms under heavy swap.
  - So waiting for the wake, with the page before on screen, is what works. Waiting longer costs time, not CPU. Waking earlier makes the wait shorter.
- **Changed** (`background/tabs.js`, `background/handlers.js`, `background/navigation.js`, `background/diagnostics.js`, `themes/arcadia/sidebar.js`, `themes/arcadia/content.js`, `content/layout.js`, `themes/arcadia/palette.js`):
  - A switch to a tab that may be asleep waits for its wake's own answer, however late. That's any tab in Safari that was shown before, isn't loading, and isn't held by keep-ready (`mayWait`). The page before stays in front meanwhile.
    - A row clicked in the sidebar or the palette covers its page at once, as before. A next/previous key covers it after 150 ms, as before.
    - Other switches (a space's icon, ⌃⌘ and an arrow to a space, a tab closed) have the background cover the page in front once the wait passes 150 ms (`coverFront`). A restore after that uses the same cover.
  - A wake answered more than 60 ms after it went out was a process just resumed. Its preparation, sent beside the wake, gets 250 ms from that answer. Then the page draws (paint barrier), then the show. All by 2 s from the ask.
  - No answer by 1.6 s: the tab is shown as it is (its show resumes it), its preparation called off, no barrier.
  - Another switch, or a key press after it, ends the wait at once. The page checks every 50 ms.
  - A wake that answers late that there's no page (the process went away while asleep) has the tab restored behind the page, as for a tab Safari unloaded.
  - The kick is gone, and so is the `stuck` path. The code is removed rather than kept behind a flag. It had its own wake, preparation, loading and "left in front" branches through the switch, and a flag would have kept all of them. Introductions and restores still use the same flip, since a tab never shown needs its view in the window once.
  - A wake keeps its page's own answer, however late (`wakeAnswer`). The 400 ms `timeout` stays for callers that only want a quick answer. A late "awake" now counts for its hold, like one in time. A wake still waiting for its answer is taken on instead of sending a second one, which would only queue behind the first.
  - Wakes start earlier, each once, never renewed:
    - The pointer resting 150 ms on a row (the sidebar's 1.5 s renewal loop is gone; an earlier copy's renewals are ignored).
    - A press on a row, as before, and now on a space's icon.
    - The pointer resting 150 ms on a space's icon, or a swipe toward a space (once per direction per swipe): that space's most recent tab in the window.
    - The sidebar brought out over the page, by hover at the edge or the toggle: the tab used before the current one in the space shown. Not its peek after a space switch, and not a page shown with it already out.
    - A key run's next tab, as before.
    - None for a tab keep-ready holds.
  - **Switch log (DEVELOPMENT DIAGNOSTICS):** `wakeStartedBy` (`hover`, `press`, `reveal`, `space`, `keys` or `switch`), `wakeMs` (the page's answer, from the wake's start), `waitedOnOldPage` (ms from the ask to the end of the wait), and `wakeOutcome` (`awake`, `resumed`, `capped`, `no page`, `left`, `hurried`). The wake's own entry has `by`. `kicked` and `stuck` are gone.
- **Expected:** no black flash on a switch to a sleeping tab that wakes within 1.6 s. The page before stays up for the wake, about 0.7 s on a heavy tab under swap, often less when the wake started on hover or press. No other tab flashes. CPU stays close to keep-ready off: a wake is two scripts and a 2 s hold. A pointer resting on a row used to renew its hold every 1.5 s while it stayed (up to 10 s still, longer while it moved).
- **Risks:**
  - Switches to sleeping tabs show later than before. A wake past 1.6 s (p90 was 3.6 s under swap) still shows dark, as before.
  - A click more than 2 s after the pointer came to rest: the hold has ended, and Safari may have put the tab to sleep again. It's then waited for.
  - The sidebar coming out wakes the tab used before even if another is picked. A swipe wakes even if it's called off. Each is one wake.
  - A page that can't be covered (a PDF, Safari's own pages) stays as it is during the wait, with no sign that anything is happening.
  - The barrier after a resume relies on a hidden page running rendering updates. If it doesn't, the barrier runs out (400 ms at most) and the show goes ahead.
  - A busy page that isn't asleep but answers in 60–100 ms gets a barrier too: a frame or two later.
  - Tabs not known to have a page are waited for too. Before, only tabs left in this copy's time were kicked.
- **Verified:** unit tests 450. The kick tests in `tests/wake.test.js` are replaced by `wait:` tests. A sleeping tab is waited for, then prepared and drawn. No answer: shown at 1.6 s. Too little time left. The cover for switches that don't cover themselves. Another switch or a key press ends the wait. A hover wake answered late. A late "no page" is restored. A tab held, a tab loading, a running tab. A background restart. A slow permission check. There are also tests for a late answer that counts, and for one landing right at 1 s. The `prewake` handler gets one test. The hover tests are rewritten without the renewal. 15 of these fail on HEAD's code (with the new tests copied over). The 3 that pass there test behaviour kept from before: the held key, the permission check that throws, and the key run with a late wake ahead. The restore test that looked for the wake's renewal "after" Safari's answer missed it when both came in the same millisecond, so it now looks from that millisecond on. WebKit host: `kick` and `kick-stuck` are replaced by `wake-wait` and `wake-cap`. New: `wake-reveal` and `wake-space`. All four pass. `wake-wait` run against HEAD's build fails "B: shown once, nothing before", because the kick shows B first. `run.sh all` (load average 3–10): 71 of 72 passed. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch". Run alone it fails the same way on this build and on HEAD's build (git archive), as in the slices before. Under that load the host's background sometimes stalled for 1–2 s in the middle of a switch, on HEAD's build too. So the host can't say how long a switch takes in Safari. The host can't put a process to sleep, so how long Safari takes to wake one is for Tirth's Safari (TESTING.md).
- **Review fixes** (`background/tabs.js`, `background/handlers.js`, `background/navigation.js`):
  - A late answer's hold counts from that answer, less 60 ms, not from the wake's start. The hold's own timer starts as the script reaches the page, at the resume. Counted from the start, a switch that waited for an answer past 1.4 s (a hover wake's past 1.6 s) sent a second wake and waited up to 400 ms more for it. In a test with the second answer taking 450 ms, that took 401 ms of the 2 s the page had to draw.
  - A tab is marked as resumed behind only while its wake still counts. A switch that stopped waiting at 1.6 s showed the tab, and the late answer then marked it again. The next switch to it, running by then, was drawn first and logged `resumed`.
  - An answer already there counts even when there's no time left to wait. Before, a switch near its deadline was taken as `capped` with its preparation called off, though its tab had answered 300 ms before.
  - The switch no longer waits for the covered page's own answer to the cover once the tab has answered. It tells that page how the switch went once the page has answered. A restore still waits for it, since it needs to know the page is covered. In a test with the page slow to answer, a tab that answered at 170 ms was shown at 426 ms. It's now about 250 ms.
  - A tab never shown (a ⌘-clicked link read later) is waited for too, then introduced. Before, its introduction went ahead once its wake ran out of time at 400 ms, its process perhaps still asleep. One with no answer by 1.6 s is introduced as before. One the caller has just waited for to load (a new tab from the palette) isn't waited for.
  - A held key passes over a tab whose wake hasn't answered within 70 ms, as it already did for a tab with no page. The highlight moves on and the page stays. Shown, it was Safari's empty view until the next press. The tab the key stops on waits for its wake.
  - Bringing the sidebar out wakes the same tab at most once every 10 s. Brought out every few seconds, it kept that tab running most of the time, at about 20% of a core for a heavy tab.
- **Rejected from the review:**
  - Showing a tab with no answer sooner than 1.6 s. Under heavy swap it doesn't come up sooner once shown. In the 22:43:06 case (round 4) the tab was shown at .248, the process took the resume at 06.425, and it drew at 07.424. Showing it earlier only turns time under the cover into time on Safari's dark view. These switches are logged as `capped` for Tirth's count.
  - Drawing every tab hidden longer than Safari's suspend delay before its show, whatever its answer time. No case has been seen of a tab that answered in under 60 ms and showed dark. A paint barrier on a hidden page can take up to 400 ms. The switch log already has `hiddenForMs` and `wakeMs` beside each switch to check this in Tirth's Safari.
  - Introductions and restores still flip the tab to the front and back. That is older than this change, and a tab never in a window needs it.
- **Review fixes, verified:** unit tests 456. Six new tests in `tests/wake.test.js`, and two changed: the held key now expects only the tab it stops on to be shown, and the late-answer wake test expects the hold to run from the answer. Each fails on the version reviewed. WebKit host `run.sh all` (load average about 4): 69 of 72 passed. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on this build, HEAD's and the reviewed version's, as before. `behindlate` and `introduce` stopped with the host itself crashing (Trace/BPT trap) right after the step that sets up the show probe. Run again alone, `introduce` passed and `behindlate` passed 1 of 3 times on this build. The same crash came on the reviewed version's build (`behindlate`) and on HEAD's (`introduce`).

## A fast space switcher: on press, without waiting for the write (2026-11-03)

- **Asked (Tirth):** with keep-ready at 0 they see big black flashes, and want them gone without giving up CPU and memory. Round 4 also found the sidebar's space switcher slow.
- **Why:** round 4's diagnosis, read in the code.
  - A space's icon acted on `click`, when the button came back up. Nothing showed until then: no highlight, no cover, no redraw. Tab rows and swipes already act at once.
  - The background then waited for the space to be written before it even looked for the tab (`showSpace` awaited `mutate(setActiveSpace)`). That write waits behind any queued writes, asks storage who owns it, writes all of `openTabs`, and every page hears it.
  - The page asking drew the new space, and its rows of its own space were thrown away (`forgetUnused`). Back on it, every row was built again.
- **Changed** (`themes/arcadia/sidebar.js`, `background/navigation.js`):
  - A space's icon switches as the main mouse button goes down, without ⌃, like a row. The sidebar shows the space at once, from its top, the space's last tab is highlighted, and the page is covered (`switchSpace`, sharing `askSpace` with the swipe). The click that follows does nothing more. A click with no press (the keyboard, a script) does the same. The icon of the space the page is in, with no switch under way, only sets the window's space, as before.
  - In Safari, `showSpace` no longer waits for the space to be written before it starts the switch. The write waits for the next one, normally the activation's, at most 2 s; the activation sets the same space anyway (`followTab`). A write still under way as the tab is prepared would make the page read storage before drawing (`stateRev`). The page draws its own tab's space anyway (`stateForShow`). Chrome writes it first, as before. So does Safari for a space with no tab to bring, the page already in the space, `bringTab: false`, or no such space.
  - Rows are kept for the last three spaces a sidebar drew, not only the one on screen. A row taken back out of the cache loses any press, drop or landing class it had.
  - The space a sidebar shows ahead of the state is dropped once the switch that brings it is over (the page left, or the switch failed), not only when the state catches up or after 3 s. Without this, a page left with a press and come back to within 3 s showed the space it had asked for, not its own, for the rest of the 3 s. Swipes could already do this, but less often. A swipe still settling keeps it until it ends, so it ends on the space its picture slid in. It's dropped at the next draw, which is the page's preparation when it's shown again.
  - Waking the space's last tab on a pointer resting on its icon was done in the slice before (`wake-space`).
- **Expected:** the sidebar changes as the button goes down, and the space's tab comes up about one click's length sooner (the button is down about 100 ms), plus the time of the space's own write, which isn't measured in Tirth's Safari. A write already under way, with changes queued behind it, is still waited for (review fix below). Coming back to a space's page reuses its rows. No change in CPU or memory: a few dozen detached rows at most per page.
- **Risks:**
  - A press on a space's icon meant only to start a double-click (the editor) switches already. That was so on the first click before.
  - If the background stops within 2 s of a switch that never activated its tab, the space isn't saved. The activation sets it whenever the tab does come to the front.
  - Rows of spaces not on screen stay in memory, three spaces' worth at most per page.
- **Verified:** unit tests 459, with 3 new in `tests/spaces-switch.test.js`. Two of them fail on HEAD's `navigation.js`. In one, storage takes no write until released, and HEAD's switch never brings the tab. In the other, HEAD writes the space on its own before the switch. The third covers the cases still written first, and passes on both. WebKit host: new `space-press` and `space-click`. On HEAD's build (git archive), `space-press` fails, since nothing happens on the press. `space-click` fails "the rows it drew before", since the rows were all built again. Before the fix for the space asked for, the page come back to showed Work's one row for about 2.8 s, which fails the check's 500 ms. Times from Date.now in the pages, 3 runs each. HEAD's build, with the button down 100 ms: the sidebar showed the space 106–111 ms after the press, and the tab 116–120 ms after. This build: the sidebar at 2–3 ms, the tab at 12–18 ms, whether pressed or clicked. The host's storage takes a write in about 2 ms, so the time saved by not waiting for it doesn't show there. The page's draw in its preparation took 0–2 ms with five rows on either build, too little to compare. First `run.sh all`: 71 of 74 passed. `spaces` failed "a flick shows the next space at once": the space asked for was dropped as the swipe's switch landed, before the swipe settled. Fixed as above, and `spaces` then passed alone 2 of 2. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch", as on HEAD's build. `behindbox` crashed the host, and alone failed "then comes to the front" on this build and on HEAD's, with a load average of 16. Final `run.sh all` (load average 4–7): 72 of 74 passed. `press` failed as before. `behind` crashed the host (Trace/BPT trap). Alone it failed once and then passed on this build, and passed on HEAD's. Real clicks in Tirth's Safari, where the write waited behind others, are for TESTING.md.
- **Review fix:** the first version picked the tab from the cache straight away (`load()`), without waiting for changes queued behind a write under way. Those aren't in the cache yet. With storage slow, a press on a space right after using one of its tabs brought the tab used before it, and a tab just dropped on the space's icon was switched away from instead of staying. The goBack of a cancelled switch could also take the space before an earlier switch, and leave the window on the wrong space. Now the tab, the check that the page is already in the space, and the space to go back to are all read in the change itself, behind every change queued before it, as before the slice. Only the space's own write isn't waited for (`mutate`'s `within`). The cost: a write under way still holds the switch up. Not waiting for it too would mean making changes without waiting for earlier writes everywhere (`store.js`), which is a bigger change than this one. Verified: two new tests in `tests/spaces-switch.test.js` hold storage, start one write, queue a change behind it, and show Work. Both fail on the first version (tab 4 brought, not 3; tab 4 brought, not tab 1 staying) and pass on HEAD's and this one. The slice's two tests still fail on HEAD's `navigation.js`. Unit tests 461. WebKit host `run.sh all`, with a load average of 3 rising to 16 from other apps: 70 of 74 passed, `space-press`, `space-click` and `spaces` among them, and again alone. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch", and alone also "in front with the button still down". HEAD's build failed the same two checks alone. `introduce`, `behindlate` and `behindfocus` stopped with the host crashing (Trace/BPT trap). Alone, `introduce` and `behindlate` crashed on HEAD's build too, and `behindfocus` crashed once in 3 runs on HEAD's and passed once in 3 on this one.

## Keep-ready at background priority: a message, not a script (2026-11-03)

- **Asked (Tirth):** with keep-ready at 0 they see big black flashes, and with it on Safari ate their battery. They want the flashes gone without giving up CPU and memory.
- **Why:** research on WebKit's source and Safari 27.2, read passively (no Safari automation).
  - The dark view is the web view's own background (`NSColor.controlBackgroundColor`: #1E1E1E in Dark Mode, white in Light Mode). Safari copies a page's color into it only when its new design (Calistoga) is off, and here it's on. No page or extension setting changes it. So the only way out is a tab that isn't suspended when it's shown.
  - A process with any activity running isn't suspended, and keeps its drawn layers. Suspension is what drops them (`sendPrepareToSuspend` → `hideContentUntilPendingUpdate`).
  - Keep-ready's hold was a script left running in the page (`keepAwake` through `executeScript`). WebKit runs that with a foreground activity: RunningBoard's UserInteractiveFocal role, the fast cores. That matches round 4's `cpu_resource` reports, where held hidden tabs ran at User Interactive QoS, about 22% of a core each.
  - A message to a content script whose answer is still pending (`tabs.sendMessage`, the listener returning `true`) holds a background activity instead: RunningBoard's Background role, efficiency cores, background QoS, throttled I/O. It also prevents suspension. It costs one round trip, where `executeScript` first asks for each frame's info.
- **Changed** (`background/tabs.js` `holdTab`/`releaseTab`, new `content/hold.js`, `content/core.js`, `background/keep-ready.js`):
  - Keep-ready's hold is a message to Margin's page in the tab, answered when the hold is over: at its time, when the page is shown, when it goes away (`pagehide`, `pageswap`), or when a release names its key. WebKit puts no limit on the wait, so the page's own timer always ends it, capped at 11 minutes.
  - A release is a message too. It also fires the old `margin-release:<key>` event, which ends a hold placed the old way.
  - A page with no listener of this copy's (Margin's script from before an update) answers at once with nothing. It's then held the old way, by `keepAwake`, for what's left of the time. A message that fails after 2 s (its page gone with its process) isn't followed by anything.
  - The listener comes off at `pagehide` and back at a `pageshow` from the back/forward cache, like the page's other listener (a listener left on held the whole document in memory). Safari only: Chrome keeps no tabs ready.
  - The wakes before a switch still use `keepAwake`. Their hold is short, and the switch is about to show the tab anyway.
  - A page's word that Safari was left (`shared/away.js`) now lets go after 15 s instead of 3 s. Safari's own find bar (⌘F) and address bar (⌘L) take focus with the page still in front, and that sends the same word. `WINDOW_ID_NONE` still lets go after 3 s. A hold costs little now, and a dark switch back costs more.
- **Expected:** switching back to the tab just left (or the one before) within a minute shows no flash, as before. A held tab now runs at background priority: its site's work goes on, slowly, on the efficiency cores, instead of at full speed. Battery and CPU should be close to keep-ready off. Default stays 1 minute, 2 tabs.
- **Not changed:** what is held and for how long, the 2-tab cap, and the switch's wait for a sleeping tab.
- **Not done from the research, for later:** waking on intent with a message instead of two scripts (a wake's answer and its short hold, which the switch's wait relies on), holding the last tab of the space left too, dropping script calls that only ask whether a page is there, and fewer storage writes heard by every page (WebKit wakes a suspended process for 1 s once more than 128 messages wait for it: 52 times in 3 hours in Tirth's log, most likely those writes). Each changes more than this round should. No way to change the dark view's color exists, so none was tried.
- **Risks:**
  - The priority comes from reading WebKit's source and Safari's RunningBoard policy, not from a measurement. TESTING.md has the checks: `ps` priority 4 (darwinbg) for held tabs instead of 47, and the log's wake-up reasons.
  - A process at background priority may page out more under memory pressure, so a held tab may take a little longer to draw when shown. WebKit still keeps the page before on screen for up to 250 ms while it draws, and a switch through Margin now draws a held tab first if its wake doesn't answer in 400 ms (review fixes below).
  - If Safari answers a message with no listener by failing slowly rather than at once, those tabs are held only once the 2 s are up, or not at all.
  - A page that blocks both `pagehide` and `pageswap` before navigating away leaves its hold to its 1-minute timer, as before.
- **Verified:** unit tests (see the review fixes entry for the totals). New `tests/hold.test.js` (3: the page's side), and 3 in `tests/wake.test.js`: the hold is a message and no script runs; a page with no listener is held by the script for the rest of the time; a message that fails late isn't followed; the release is a message with the script only as a fallback; the message names match the page's. Each fails on HEAD's code. WebKit host: `keep-ready-cap` now counts holds in Margin's page and checks that no page-world hold runs; `keep-ready-away` checks the holds are still there 3.5 s after the page's word (the 15 s release is past the host's ~5 s of background life, so it's covered by unit tests).

## Review fixes for the round: restarts, stale holds, the old setting, wasted messages (2026-11-03)

- **Asked:** a review of the branch after round 5's slices. Each finding was reproduced in a test first.
- **Fixed:**
  - **A background started by a switch didn't hold the tab just left** (high). The activation that starts a background Safari stopped comes before the state is read. `frontChanged` then found no state and said "none". Now it says "not known yet" (`undefined`), and `activated` takes the tab left from the state once it's read (`leftIn`). Every activation now waits for the state, so they still run in the order they came. This is the commonest "there and straight back" after reading a page for a while.
  - **A hold whose page changed, or that had ended, stayed `sure` in `storage.session`.** A background started again read it back as running, so a switch to that tab didn't wait, had no paint barrier, and could show Safari's empty view. Now `pageChanged` and a hold's end write the change.
  - **A held tab whose wake doesn't answer in 400 ms is drawn before it's shown.** A switch to a tab keep-ready holds doesn't wait (it's running). If its wake times out all the same, it was likely suspended, and it now gets the paint barrier, as a tab waited for does. A slow answer under 400 ms is a busy page, and isn't drawn first, as before.
  - **The old setting is kept as saved.** `keepTabsReadyMinutes` was dropped from the settings at the next write. A build without this branch (main, or a branch Tirth is checking) then found nothing and turned the old keep-ready on at 30 minutes. It's now left in storage, unread here.
  - **A ⌘T tab that stays** (no palette in time, used, or the page before couldn't come back) now has its activation told once it's kept. Before, its activation was left out for good, so the next switch held the tab before it, likely suspended by then, and not this one.
  - **A cover the background put up during a wait is always lifted.** If the wake failed after the cover went up (past 150 ms), the switch threw before it knew how to uncover, and the page stayed covered for 8 s.
  - **No wake requests in Chrome.** The sidebar coming out, a pointer resting on a row or a space's icon, a swipe and the palette sent `prewake`, which started Chrome's service worker for nothing (Chrome wakes no tabs). Pages now don't send it there (`platform.js` `asksBackground`), and the handler ignores it.
  - **`away` and `back` only when they can matter.** A page doesn't say Safari was left once it was shown longer ago than the setting's minutes plus 5 s (no hold of the switch to it can still run). It doesn't say `back` more than 20 s after its `away`. Each message could start a stopped background for nothing.
  - **`@keyframes` looked up once per name every 2 s.** A read during an animation walked every rule of every sheet each time (3–11 ms in WebKit on a page with 30,000 rules).
  - **Showing a space checks this copy still owns the state first** (Safari, at most once a second). Its write waits, so it no longer reached the check a write makes, and a copy a newer one replaced went on to switch tabs from its stale state. The check is in `showSpace`, not in every write that waits: those are mostly a hidden tab's title and load, which don't act on anything.
- **Rejected or partly done:**
  - Holding the released tabs again when Safari comes back (the away finding). It would resume suspended processes in the background for tabs that may not be wanted, and they wouldn't be sure to have drawn. Margin's own switches wait and draw them anyway. The page's word now waits 15 s instead (above), which covers the find bar and short trips away.
- **Verified:** unit tests 476, all passing. New or changed, and failing on HEAD's code: 3 in `tests/keep-ready.test.js` (stale holds after a restart, the activation before the state, the page's word at 15 s), 4 in `tests/wake.test.js` (the late uncover, the held tab that times out, the ⌘T replay, prewake in Safari), and 1 each in `tests/model.test.js`, `tests/spaces-switch.test.js` (a stale copy showing a space), `tests/page-moves.test.js`, `tests/away.test.js` and `tests/open-behind-chrome.test.js`. Not covered by a unit test: `background.js` itself (the activation waiting for the state); the keep-ready test covers what it relies on.
- **WebKit host:** `run.sh all` at a load average of 4–6: 66 of 74 passed. `keep-ready-cap` failed only because of a bug in its new check (`a ?? -1 === 0` reads as `a ?? false`); fixed, it passes alone on this build, and against HEAD's build (git archive) it fails, as it should, since HEAD has no message holds. `keep-ready-away` passed. `catchup-loaded` and `unloadedlate` failed once and passed alone. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch", as in every slice before. `behindfocus` crashed the host (Trace/BPT trap), as before. `owner` failed its last four checks here and alone, 6 of 6 times; HEAD's build fails its last two alone. Its switch comes about 5 s after the install, at the edge of the host's background life (README: the background stops about 5 s after it starts), and at a load average of 11–12 the background stalled with the switch's wake sent: timers held up for 10 s. A version without one of the fixes (the ownership check made in every waiting write) passed it 3 of 3 times, but the final build doesn't have that check either and still fails, so that was the load. It needs a run on a quiet Mac, or splitting. After the last change (the check moved to `showSpace`): `space-press`, `space-click`, `spaces`, `wake-space`, `keep-ready-cap` and `keep-ready-away` pass alone.

## Every switch answered before its page stops waiting; the "isn't responding" notice only for a copy that's gone (2026-11-03)

- **Asked:** Tirth, about 09:27, on this round's build (installed 09:21): "Margin isn't responding on this page. Reload the page to fix it" on nearly every tab switch, with occasional blanks.
- **Found** (Safari's log through `/usr/bin/log`, the code, a fake Safari and the WebKit host):
  - **Not a crash, not a background restart, not a copy left from the update.** Margin's background (one WebContent process) ran without a break from 09:23:49 to 09:29:19. The `terminateWorker` lines were sites' workers. The only page loaded during the first copy's 2 s life was navigated away before the second copy started.
  - **The switches were slow, and their answers came after the page had stopped waiting.** The Mac had just booted (load average 34–53) and Safari had just relaunched, so every tab was unloaded and every switch went through restore-behind: 1.7–7.3 s each. That path was budgeted to use almost all of the page's 7 s (`RESTORE_HARD_MAX_MS` 5000, `RESTORE_RESERVE_MS` 1500). After it came Safari calls with no limit (`tabs.get`, `tabs.query`, `tabs.update`, `windows.*`). On the switch to tab 921 its introduction went out 7.33 s after the switch began, 14 ms before the tab's hung process answered again: a Safari call had waited on it. Past 7 s the switch was dropped as "Too late" (covered page or blank, then nothing). Past 8 s the page put up the notice.
  - **The notice went up on time alone.** `noAnswer` showed it 8 s after any request with no answer. Messages nobody waits on got no grace at all. The background was alive the whole time, so a reload wouldn't have helped.
  - **Round 5 made it a little worse, but didn't cause it.** In the WebKit host its new waits add 0.35–0.75 s on slow targets. Holds, prewakes and queues piling up were ruled out.
- **Changed:**
  - **`focusTab` holds the whole switch to the page's deadline** (`background/tabs.js`). If a switch hasn't shown its tab by `FORCE_SHOW_MS` (1.5 s) before the deadline, the tab is shown as it is. Its own steps stand down, as they do for another switch: `focusGen` is bumped, so no flip or show of theirs goes out after. Its introduction is ended and its preparation called off, keeping the arrival cover. `focusTab` answers by `ANSWER_BY_MS` (0.8 s) before the deadline at the latest. A show that went out but hasn't been answered is taken as made (Safari carries it out). A switch asked with less than `REFUSE_MS` (1 s) left doesn't start. Its steps now plan to have shown the tab `SHOW_BEFORE_MS` (1.8 s) before the deadline, instead of 0.4 s. The switch log says where a switch was stuck (`stuckAt`), and whether it was `forced` or `unconfirmed`.
  - **The restore budget shrank:** `RESTORE_RESERVE_MS` 1500 → 2800, `RESTORE_HARD_MAX_MS` 5000 → 4000. With a 7 s page, a restore waits until 2.5 s for a page that's there (as before), and until 4 s for one whose document hasn't answered (was 5 s). There's then about 1 s for the introduction and the paint barrier. An introduction needs at least `INTRODUCE_MIN_MS` (0.5 s) left before the planned show, or the tab is shown as it is.
  - **`background.js` answers every timed request** (`activateTab`, `openPin`, `setSpace`, `newTab`, `search`) by `REPLY_GUARD_MS` (0.5 s) before its deadline. Whatever is still under way then (the background starting, a space's write queued behind slow ones, a Safari call), the answer says the switch didn't happen, and it won't now (`focusTab` refuses past the deadline). The first-sync wait for such a request now leaves 2.5 s for the switch itself (`untilSynced(deadline)`). `setSpace` waits at most 250 ms to carry the sidebar open before it answers.
  - **The notice only for a copy that's really gone** (`shared/no-answer.js`, `content/core.js`). Before offering the reload, the page asks its own storage. In a live copy that answers, however busy the background, and the page logs "its background is slow" instead. A copy that's gone never answers (the same test as `cut-off.js` uses). The notice never shows for messages nobody waits on: `prewake`, `away`, `back`, `pageBlinked`, `pageIcon`, `fetchStyle`, `getZoom`, `switchLog`, the sidebar's scroll, `windowState`.
- **Tried and dropped:** skipping a restored tab's introduction when neither its renewed wake nor its arrival cover answers (a hung process). It also dropped the introduction for busy pages that are alive, which it's there for (a unit test caught it). With the deadline held, a hung introduction no longer breaks the answer.
- **Not done:**
  - Not restoring behind into an occluded window (tab 1325, blank for 53–70 s while Safari had its window covered). An extension can't see occlusion, and `focusWindow` focuses the window before a restore anyway.
  - Fewer probes of unloaded tabs at startup (about 54 failing `executeScript`s per repair pass after a relaunch). They're noise, not the cause.
- **Verified:** unit tests 483, all passing (`npm test` exit 0). New: `tests/answer-in-time.test.js`, which drives `background.js`'s own listener against a fake Safari whose calls can hang. Three of its tests fail on HEAD's code: a tab whose update never answers, an introduction stalled behind Safari, and a space's switch behind slow writes. HEAD gives no answer by the deadline in all three. A fourth checks a switch asked with little time left: with 1.2 s left it's shown at once, and with 0.9 s left it isn't switched at all. Its notice tests check `shared/no-answer.js`. Six tests in `tests/wake.test.js` were rescaled to the new margins: their deadlines had been set against the old 0.4 s reserve. One of them now checks that a switch out of time during its introduction is shown as it is and leaves the window free for the next.
- **WebKit host:** new `stall` op (`host.swift`: a tab's next `tabs.update` is carried out and answered `ms` later, as Safari's update of a hung tab was) and a `stalled` scenario (4 s stall, 3 s deadline). This build answers in 2.6–2.75 s, "shown", stuck at `show`, `unconfirmed`, 3 of 3 runs. HEAD's build gives no answer by 3 s (FAIL). In the host the background's timers fire about 100 ms late at a load average of 3, so the margins are wider than the bare transit. `run.sh all` (75 scenarios, load average 3–6), first against this build and then against HEAD's (git archive):
  - **First run of this build:** `loadcover` and `shellcover` failed, and failed again when run alone. Their 1.9 s deadline now left no time to wait for the wake, so an unloaded tab (it answers "no page" within milliseconds) was taken as capped and shown without its `showing` note and loading cover. Fixed: the wake wait now runs until `focusTab`'s forced show (less the preparation's 250 ms), not until the planned one. Both now pass alone.
  - **Final build, all scenarios:** everything passed except `press` ("the release asked for no second switch", which fails on HEAD and in every slice before) and `unloadedlate` (it also failed in HEAD's run, and passes alone 2 of 2).
  - **Host crashes (Trace/BPT trap):** `introduce` crashes alone on HEAD's build too. `behindfocus` and `behindlate` crash now and then, as before; `behindfocus` passes alone.
  - **HEAD's build** also failed `prepared` and `stalled` (expected: no answer by the deadline).
- **Review, then fixed** (each with a test that fails on the build before it; `tests/answer-in-time.test.js` unless said):
  - **A late show no longer takes the front from a newer switch.** If Safari held the update of a hung tab, the forced show landed seconds later, after the user had gone to another tab, and pulled the window back. In a fake Safari that holds a hung tab's calls: a space's switch to tab 4 (hung 6 s), then tab 5 at 1.7 s; at 7 s tab 4 was in front. Now `focusTab` watches a show it answered unconfirmed (`answeredLate`). Once Safari answers it, if the window has activated another tab since (`sawActivation`, from background.js) and this one is in front, the last of those comes back. Until then its activation isn't taken as the user's (`userSwitched`), for 10 s at most.
  - **A tab Safari is slow to look up is still switched to.** `tabs.get` ran twice before `focusTab` even started (`withTab`, then `activateTab`), with no limit. With it stalled 5 s the page got "Too late" and the tab never came. `resolveTab` now gives up 2 s before the deadline and goes on with the tab as Margin's state has it, so `focusTab` starts with time to show it.
  - **A space's switch with the sidebar carried open is answered as done.** Its carry wait (up to 250 ms) could run after `focusTab`'s latest answer (0.8 s before the deadline), leaving about 50 ms before the reply guard. With the background busy 150 ms there, the page was told the switch didn't happen while the tab came up. The wait now ends 0.8 s before the deadline (`CARRY_ENDS_MS`).
  - **Nothing opens after its page was told it didn't happen.** A pin with no open tab, behind slow writes, was answered "Too late" at 2.5 s, then its tab opened at 6 s. `openTab` and `openBehind` now refuse to open a tab within `REFUSE_MS` of the deadline (`tooLate`), and every timed caller passes it (`openPin`, `newTab`, `search`, `showSpace`'s start page).
  - **A new tab from the palette keeps its introduction.** `openBehind` left `focusTab` 2.5 s (`FOCUS_MS`), but `focusTab` now plans its show 1.8 s before its deadline, so a busy page (scripts taking 260 ms) had no time left to be introduced and was shown with WebKit's dark first frame (`tests/wake.test.js`). `FOCUS_MS` is now 2.1 s plus `SHOW_BEFORE_MS`, the 2.1 s it had before.
  - **An introduction's late activations aren't switches.** A forced show ended the introduction while Safari still held its two updates; when they came, background.js took them as real activations (target, page in front, target). The introduction ended that way now lingers until shortly after Safari answers, and its events are left out as they come (`lingering`); it tells nothing more once it's answered (`taken`).
  - Found on the way, not changed: in the fake, two activations fired in the same task can reach `activated()` out of order across an ownership recheck (`owned`). Safari's events come in separate tasks, so this shouldn't happen there; `answeredLate` waits a task before it looks.
- **Review, not changed:**
  - **Long waits by design.** A page can stay covered up to about 5.5 s and be answered by about 6.2 s. That's the 7 s budget from before, now shorter (the restore's hard limit 5 s → 4 s), not something this change adds.
  - **A show answered as done before Safari answers it** (`unconfirmed`). On HEAD the same switch was answered after the page gave up, then came the notice. Safari's log suggests tab selection doesn't wait for the page's process: tab 921's view became visible at 09:24:21.179, 14 ms before its process (pid 2307, unresponsive since 09:24:17.926) answered again. So such a show should rarely be late. If it is, and the user switched elsewhere meanwhile, it's now undone (above).
