  - Limits: a key's switch waits until 3 s after the press, then up to 1 s more for a look already out, then shows the tab as it is. A click waits until focusTab shows it at its deadline, as before.
  - Not used for a tab shown as it is at the wake's cap, one with no page, a restore cut short, or with Safari already found slow.
  - Changed after review: a tab capped at its wake whose page answered a wake less than 5 s before is gated too (`gate.stalled`). Its process runs and its main thread is stalled, so its show resumes nothing, and shown at the cap it would be the empty view. Safari suspends a hidden tab a median 8 s after it's left (10% within a second), so a tab suspended in that gap now waits for its page (the look resumes it at background priority) instead of being shown dark at the cap.
- **S2: after a flip, the page says whether it drew.** The page notes when it first drew after becoming visible (`drewVisibleAt`: the first ResizeObserver or animation frame callback after `visibilitychange`). After a flip, one quick script asks for it, beside the paint barrier. A time after the flip went out means the hide is over, so the switch goes on to S1's look without waiting for two more rendering updates of a page hidden again. A page that says nothing, or a time from before the flip, gets the barrier as before, FLIP_HIDE_MS limit included.
  - **Open question 1, answered in the WebKit host.** In 8 flips of tabs that had been in a window (a light page, and one whose visibility handler takes 120 ms), the first ResizeObserver and animation frame callbacks after the page became visible ran in the very rendering update whose commit unhid the content, 1–20 ms before WebKit logged `hideContentUntilPendingUpdate completed`. An observer armed as the page became visible also ran in an update after the page was hidden again; an animation frame didn't. When a hidden page with a heavy handler was shown, the update that unhid it ran before the page's `visibilitychange` handler, so the first callback came later than the commit. That errs on the safe side.
  - **Adapted from that run:** a show sent within a display refresh of the flip's commit wasn't held. WebKit still waited for the flip's own update (`m_waitingForDidUpdateActivityState`, cleared at the next `didRefreshDisplay`, which came 7–13 ms after the commit in the host and 27 ms in Safari's log for Instagram). In the host that was 118–246 ms of the empty view. So a show the page's word lets through waits 50 ms after that word (`FLIP_SETTLE_MS`).
  - The host can't settle the case of a tab without the mark: its pages behind start out visible, so the flip's visible change never reaches them.
  - Changed after review: the page's word counts as a drawing after Safari's answer to the flip, as the flip's hide does, not after the flip went out. Safari answers the flip's two updates about 110 ms after they go out, so after the word the hide still counted as pending for up to 1 s, and a held key refused the tab, where a barrier that drew cleared it.
  - Kept after review: the 50 ms settle. The page's first update once visible proves a commit was built and sent, not that the UI has it. A second rendering update would prove it, but needs the page's layout to change again (an element of Margin's resized each time), so the settle stays.
- **S3: the proof is kept with the document.** When the background tells a page its tab is proven with that page in it (the answer to `inView`, and now to `hello`), the page writes `Margin.inWindowAt` into its content-script world, not into its copy's `Margin.content`. That world outlives a copy of Margin (an update, Margin.app opened) and goes with the document. The next copy's wake (`pageThere`) reads it, so there's no extra Safari call, and counts it as proof (`how: 'page'`, `markReason: 'proven:page'`). A new document has a new world with nothing in it.
  - The build that ships this starts cold: the copy before it never wrote a proof.
  - Changed after review: the hello's answer no longer waits up to 200 ms for the tab's record on a background just started. It says `proven` if the record is read by then; else the background writes the word into that document's world by a script once it is (`provenInPage`, matched by the document's id).
  - Margin's own reload in place keeps the tab's record. The cut-off probe passes the old world's proof on before the load, and the new page's hello within 15 s isn't taken as a new web view's, even if it says `navigate` with one history entry. A GET of the same address can say that, and WebKit never clears the mark on the web view. That path never called `lose()`.
- **S4: on Safari the page listener takes the commands' keys too.** `installNavigation` matches the Settings shortcuts first, then the manifest commands' keys, read from `runtime.getManifest().commands` (⌃⌘↓/↑, ⌃⌘→; Safari's manifest has no key for the previous space). It keeps `preventDefault` and `stopImmediatePropagation`, so Safari's command doesn't fire for that press; the 150 ms dedupe is the backstop.
  - A custom binding still wins, and null settings still pause it.
  - It steps aside in a page cut off from Margin. The cut-off probe sets `Margin.cutOff` in that world, and the old copy's listener (from this release on) lets the keys pass to the page and to Safari's commands.
  - Every wake now says whether the page's world is another copy's, and if so the background probes it, so pages are told as they're woken.
  - Fallback: a press whose `navigate` gets no answer in 400 ms makes the listener ask whether its world still reaches Margin at all: a `storage.local.get` with 200 ms to answer, as the cut-off probe asks. Only a world whose storage doesn't answer either makes it step aside. It asks again at the next press (which then passes) and at any late answer, and takes the keys again once the world answers. `navigate` answers as soon as the press is queued, not when its switch is over.
  - Changed after review: the first version stepped aside on the 400 ms alone, until an answer of `true`. A Safari background starting again after it went idle has to start, decide its owner and sync before it answers, so presses during a cold start went to the page and were lost (⌥⌘↓ has no command on Safari), and an answer that wasn't `true` left the listener aside for the life of the page.
  - In a world that steps aside, the commands' keys (⌃⌘ arrows) go on with `stopImmediatePropagation` and no `preventDefault`: the page's own listeners never see them, and WebKit gives a key the page didn't turn down to the menu, where Safari's command runs in the copy running now. The Settings keys go to the page, since nothing else can take them there. The cut-off probe also puts one small listener into a cut-off world that does the same for the commands' keys, since a world from before this release doesn't know `Margin.cutOff`. So a quieted cut-off tab in front can still be left by ⌃⌘↓ on a site that eats arrows. Not tested in real Safari yet: the WebKit host has no Safari menu.
  - A ⌃⌘ arrow that Settings give to the sidebar (`sidebarShortcut`) is left to it, not taken for the command.
  - The manifest commands stay for Safari's own pages, the address bar, frames without Margin, and pages before the script loads.
- **S5: a tab in front is never reloaded.** A cut-off tab found in front is quieted at once (its dead sidebar out of sight) and loaded again once it's left: 300 ms after the leave, with no switch under way, asked again then, and only if nothing in it would be lost. A tab in use is never loaded again, as before.
  - Changed after review: the same goes for a tab a switch is under way to. The first version still loaded a suspect again from inside the switch to it, 150 ms before its show, so the load committed in front. Since S4 makes every woken tab of another copy a suspect, that was the common case right after an update. Now the switch's one probe also puts the dead sidebar out of sight (while the tab is still behind, as a rule), and the tab is loaded again once it's left. The open-tab repair and any other repair skip the load the same way while a switch to the tab is under way, also when it comes while the load waits its turn.
  - A suspect, or a tab already quieted, isn't probed again by each wake of it.
  - The wake test that expected a busy suspect to be loaded again first once its draft was gone (`a switch to it later loads it again first`) now expects it shown as it is, put out of sight before the show.
- **S7: the switch log** now has `flip` (Safari's answer to each of the flip's two updates), `drewVisible` (when the page drew after the flip, by its own word), `gate` (how the look ended, each ping's time and `since`, the page in front's ping), `showMs` (Safari's answer to the show), `hurried` (a show given up for a press), `cutOff` (the switch's cut-off probe), `layersBack: 'drew'` and barrier entries `how: 'drew'`.
- **Not done:** S6, the lighter wake-ahead, waits for the next run's data. S7's logging of each page-world hold and of the wake-ahead's foreground hold isn't done either. Nor is finding out why a cut-off page draws a sidebar with only "Personal" and "New Tab".
- **Costs and risks:**
  - Every Margin show costs one more script round trip (about 20–40 ms in Safari). A busy page's switch keeps the old page and cover on screen until it answers: up to 4 s for a key, until the deadline for a click.
  - A stall that starts after the look's answer and before the show still goes dark. So does a page whose own visible handler runs past 250 ms before its first commit.
  - A wrong proof in a page's world would show an unmarked tab plainly, dark until its first commit. Only a copy's own "proven" answer writes it.
  - A world whose storage takes over 200 ms to answer, 400 ms into an unanswered press, is taken as cut off for one press.
- **Verified:**
  - `npm test`: 620 tests, all passing (exit 0). New: 13 in `tests/in-window.test.js` (S1 ×8, S2 ×2, S3 ×2), 4 in `tests/cut-off.test.js`, 1 in `tests/copies.test.js`, 6 in the new `tests/shortcuts-safari.test.js`, 1 in `tests/shortcuts.test.js`.
  - Run against 9f1611f's code, those new tests that test new behavior fail (S1 ×8, S2 ×1, S3 ×2, copies, cut-off ×3, keys ×5). The guards pass on both: a silent page gets the barrier, a page in use isn't reloaded, a custom binding wins, null settings pause.
  - Changed on purpose: three tests in `tests/wake.test.js` count one more script, or allow one more round trip. The busy restore test now expects its show at the page's deadline (its page never answers), not at 2.5 s. The cut-off probe's answer has `proven`.
  - WebKit host: new `heavyflip` (a page that stalls 2 s as it's hidden again). At 9f1611f, 40 turns showed the empty view; now its content is there 2 ms after the show. New `commandkeys`: ⌃⌘↓ on a page that takes ArrowDown at every level, from a text field too. It fails at 9f1611f (the page ate it) and passes now.
  - `reloaded` and `reloaded-newtab` now expect the tab in front to be quieted, not loaded again in front, and loaded again behind once it's left.
  - Full host run (load average 3–6): 79 scenarios, 3 failed. `reloaded` and `reloaded-newtab` failed on the S5 change and pass after the update above. `press` fails now and then on this build (2 of 4 alone) and on 9f1611f's (1 of 3 alone), on the same check.
- **After review (the same day).** Two reviews of the build above found five problems that were real; each is fixed and noted under its S above, with a unit test that fails on the code before the fix:
  - S4's listener stepped aside on latency alone (presses lost on every cold start of the background, possibly for the page's life).
  - S5 still loaded a cut-off tab again from inside the switch to it.
  - S1's press after a give-up went back to the same busy tab once 1.5 s had passed, and that switch was then capped and shown dark. A tab that is capped at its wake but stalled was never gated.
  - S1 took a busy page in front for a slow Safari, and still held a click until the target answered when Safari really was slow. It also had no limit on the number of looks.
  - S2's word left the flip's hide pending when Safari answered the flip late.
  - Smaller fixes: the hello no longer waits for its proof; a suspect isn't probed on every wake; a ⌃⌘ arrow given to the sidebar is left to it; a cut-off world sends the ⌃⌘ arrows on to Safari's commands.
- **Not changed after review, and why:**
  - S2's 50 ms settle stays. Its replacement, a second rendering update, would mean resizing an element of Margin's in every page at each visibility change.
  - S3's 15 s window for Margin's own reload stays. The entry goes at the reload's first hello, and now also when the load fails, so only a Safari unload inside the second or so before that hello could slip through. Matching the reload by navigation events would be a bigger change.
  - The navigation keys in iframes: the content script stays top-frame only (`all_frames: false`). With focus in a frame such as Google Docs' text frame, a key goes to the page first and then to the command, as before.
  - A ping is still sent when the switch's last script answered moments ago. The look is the proof and costs one round trip.
  - A tab with no recent answer that is stalled can't be told from a suspended one. It is still shown at the wake's cap.
- **Verified after review:** `npm test` 632 tests, all passing, run twice (exit 0). In the WebKit host, `heavyflip`, `commandkeys`, `reloaded`, `reloaded-newtab`, `introduce`, `switching`, `keys` and `behindkeys` pass. So does the reviewer's scenario of a woken cut-off suspect: its switch now says `cutOff: 'quiet'`, the tab isn't loaded again around its show, and its content is there 1 ms after the show. `press` failed its release check on 9f1611f, on the build above and on this one alike (2 of 3 runs each, A/B in turn), so that failure isn't from these changes.

## Sleeping tabs: a tab not used for 4 hours gives its memory back (2026-11-04)

- **Why:** Safari keeps every tab's page process alive however long it's been hidden. Tirth's one window of 35 tabs had a 16.5 GB footprint, mostly compressed or swapped. Arc puts tabs it hasn't shown for a while to sleep. Tirth chose the same: after 4 hours unused (a setting, 0 = never), with a list of sites that never sleep.
- **No `tabs.discard` in Safari.** WebKit's extension engine has none (`typeof browser.tabs.discard` is `'undefined'` in the WebKit host, and `WKWebExtensionTab.h` has no discard). Chrome has it, so Chrome uses it (`platform.sleepTabs: 'discard'`).
- **So in Safari a sleeping tab shows Margin's sleep page** (`extension/sleep/`, logic in `shared/sleep.js`). Its address carries the page's address, its title and a key: `sleep/sleep.html?u=…&t=…&k=…`. The page is Margin's grey loading card (the switch cover's color and spinner), with the page's title and the site's icon from Margin's own favicon store, read once. It has no sidebar code and no storage listener.
- **Putting a tab to sleep is `tabs.update` to the sleep page.** In the WebKit host, a YouTube tab's page process (334 MB) went away as soon as the tab moved to an extension page, which cost about 16 MB. Three sleeping tabs shared one process for their sleep pages. The plan was `location.replace` from Margin's content script, so the site's page would leave no history entry behind. WebKit refuses that: a web page's own navigation to an extension page fails with NSURLErrorDomain −1008, even with the page in `web_accessible_resources` for every site (a `fetch` of it works). The other way is refused too: an extension page can't navigate itself to a web page (the navigation never commits). Margin's new-tab page already goes to sites with `tabs.update` for the same reason. So `tabs.update` it is, and nothing is exposed to web pages (`web_accessible_resources` stays empty).
- **Waking is `tabs.update` back to the page's address.** The sleep page does it for its own tab as soon as it's visible: at load, on `visibilitychange`, or back from the back/forward cache. It never wakes itself while hidden. Only `http:` and `https:` addresses are gone to, as the URL parser reads them. Anything else (missing, broken, `javascript:`, `data:`, another extension's page, a sleep page inside a sleep page) is shown as text and never opened.
- **History.** The WebKit host gives the tab a new web view with a history of its own at each change between a web page and an extension page, so the woken tab has one history entry and Back has nothing to go to. What Safari does with a tab's history across that change isn't known. If Safari keeps it, the sleep page stays in it, behind the page it woke to. Reached again with Back, it would wake again and send the tab forward, a Back trap. So the sleep page never wakes from an entry that was woken before. It knows by the entry itself: before it sends its tab back it marks its entry (`history.replaceState` with its key, and a sessionStorage flag as a backup), and Margin's background, when it wakes a tab itself (a switch, Wake all), notes the sleep's key in `storage.session` (`sleepWoke:<key>`). A history longer than when the page first loaded also counts. On such an entry, if the Navigation API says how it was reached (only when the page before was one of Margin's own), Back goes back once more and Forward goes forward. Otherwise it shows "Open the page" and does nothing by itself. It never goes back and forth, so there's no loop. The history's length alone wasn't enough: WebKit keeps about 100 entries, so a long history (Gmail used all day) stops growing, and after Back had passed the sleep page, Forward reached it and it went back, so Forward never got to the woken page. A relaunch that restores a sleep page never woken still wakes.
- **Which tabs** (`state/model.js sleepyTabs`, then each tab as Safari has it now, `background/sleep.js`): a web page not used (or woken) for `sleepAfterHours`. Not the tab in front of any window. Not one asleep already, playing sound, loading, or on a site on `neverSleepSites`. Not one Safari unloaded itself (nothing to free), one a switch is going to, or one keep-ready holds. The page itself is asked last (`sleepInPage`, 3 s to answer, or it waits for the next pass: a tab hidden for hours has a process Safari suspended, and the script resumes it at background priority). It must be hidden, still at the same address, with nothing playing (media, a call, picture in picture, full screen) and nothing typed and not sent. Margin's content script watches for that (`content/edits.js`): trusted `input` and `change` events, and the field still holds text that differs from what it held when the user first came to it (focus, or the first `beforeinput`; any text when neither was seen), or the editor typed into still has text. Not the field's `defaultValue`: React keeps a controlled field's value attribute in step with its value, so a typed draft looked like the page's own. A watch that started after the page had loaded (Margin installed or updated, its scripts added to open pages) missed what was typed before, so it also counts any text in any field or editor of the page, as a page without the watch (an earlier copy's) is judged, generously, as `content/takeover.js` judges a page. The check runs in every frame (`allFrames`), so text or media in another site's frame (a comment box, a form, a payment's) keeps the tab awake too; the address, hidden and playing checks for the page are its top frame's, and a frame whose answer can't be read keeps the tab awake. A tab used while a pass goes on (shown a moment with Safari's tab bar or ⌘1–9, which Margin's switches don't see) isn't put to sleep: whether it's still unused is looked at again before its page is asked and before it's sent. Pinned tabs sleep too: Tirth didn't exclude them. The time Safari was closed doesn't count, because a relaunch moves every last use forward (`syncTabs`), as the archive relies on.
- **When.** With the auto-archive, every 15 minutes (`browser.alarms`; on tab events at most every 5 minutes without it). Five tabs a pass at most, one at a time, 1 s apart. A pass stops while a switch is asked for or under way, while Safari is slow (`slow.js`), and while the warm-up after a relaunch is loading tabs (never past the warm-up's own time limit, so a warm-up that failed partway doesn't stop sleep for good). A page that said no (a draft, playing, shown, no answer) isn't asked again for an hour, or until its tab is used again, because asking resumes a process Safari suspended and can take up to 3 s. That is kept in `storage.session`, since a pass often runs in a background started for it. A pass asked for by hand (`margin.sleepNow`) asks every page. A tab woken isn't put to sleep again until it has been left that long once more (`wokeAt`).
- **What Margin keeps.** A sleeping tab's record is its page's: `state/model.js seenTab` reads the page's address and title out of any copy's sleep page, and adds `sleeping: { at, how: 'page' }`. A tab the browser says is `discarded` (Chrome's tabs.discard) is `how: 'discarded'`, and isn't due either. So its row, its icon, search and the palette, its pin, its space, the archive (`staleTabs` still archives it after 12 hours, by the page's address), and the matching after a relaunch or an update all go by the page. `tab-updates.js` doesn't take the sleep page's address for a new address. There's no asleep cue in the row: no style for one exists, and the design said not to invent one.
- **Margin's own switch to a sleeping tab** is a restore behind the page (`background/tabs.js restoreTab`). The page in front is covered, the tab is sent to its page's address while hidden (`wakeAsleep`), and its load is waited for as for a tab Safari unloaded. Then it's introduced (its page is new to the window), prepared under its arrival cover, and shown. No wake or preparation script goes to the sleep page first: no script of Margin's runs there. The switch log has `asleep: 'address'`. If the update fails, the tab is flipped for an instant (`asleep: 'flip'`) and its sleep page sends it back as it's shown. If restores are off for the session, it's shown as it is (`asleep: 'as it is'`) and the sleep page wakes it in front. The loads of sleeping tabs don't count toward turning restores off: those misses are Safari's, not Margin's.
- **Copies.** An extension's address changes with each copy of Margin (every update, every Safari relaunch). A sleeping tab at an earlier copy's sleep page is moved to this copy's, with its address after it kept, still asleep (`cut-off.js oldOwnPageAddress`, by path, in the same repair that moves an old new-tab page). The old address no longer opens, so Safari's tab bar would otherwise show an error page there. The startup repair, the cut-off checks and the no-page logic leave sleeping tabs alone: they aren't web pages. The warm-up doesn't pick them either.
- **Settings** (new-tab page): "Put tabs to sleep after not using them for" (hours; decimals work, for checks), "Never put these sites to sleep" (one per line; each with its subdomains; a leading `www.` goes, so `www.example.com` means `example.com`), and "Wake all sleeping tabs". Turning sleep to 0 wakes nothing by itself: each tab wakes as it's shown.
- **Diagnostics.** The switch log has `kind: 'sleep'` entries: `run` (how many were due, how many slept, why the others were skipped, counted per reason: among them `used` for a tab used during the pass, `asked lately` for one that said no within the hour, `frame unknown` for a frame whose answer couldn't be read), `slept` (the tab, how, how long it had been idle, whose watch judged the page: `margin`, `margin-late` for a watch that started after the page loaded, or `page`), and `woke` (by `switch`, `shown` or `all`). `margin.sleepNow(ms)` in the background's console runs a pass now, for tabs unused for `ms`.
- **If Margin is removed or turned off,** sleeping tabs are left on an extension page that no longer exists, and Safari shows an error page for them. Their address still has the page's address in it, after `u=`, readable. Mitigation: "Wake all sleeping tabs" before turning Margin off, and the same after any update that changes Margin's address (the sleep pages are moved automatically while Margin runs). Nothing can run at uninstall in Safari.
- **Risks, all needing Safari to check:** what Safari does with the tab's history across the change (above), and whether `tabs.update` gives the tab a new web view (as the host does, freeing the process at once) or keeps the site's page in the back/forward cache for a while. A site that registers `beforeunload` could ask "Leave page?" as its tab is put to sleep. Margin's draft check covers the usual reason for one, but not all. Web apps that notify while hidden (mail, chat) stop doing so while asleep, so they belong on the never list. A tab's scroll position comes back only as the site restores it on load.
- **Verified:** unit tests in `tests/sleep.test.js` (the address and its checks, the sleep page, the never list, which tabs, the closed time, what Margin keeps, archive, copies, a pass and every reason to skip, Wake all), `tests/sleep-chrome.test.js` (tabs.discard), and five in `tests/wake.test.js` (Margin's switch, its fallbacks, the address lookup, the move from an earlier copy). WebKit host: `sleep-show` (put to sleep, its page's process exits, Safari's show wakes it with one history entry and its content back), `sleep-switch` (Margin's switch wakes it behind the covered page, introduced, shown, the page before hidden once), `sleep-kept` (a typed draft and the never list keep tabs awake) and `sleep-copy` (an update moves it to the new copy's sleep page, still asleep). The host needed one change: it keeps an extension page's web view when the tab leaves it, because closing one stopped Margin's background there for good.

## Sleeping tabs: the review's fixes (2026-11-04)

An independent review of the sleeping-tabs build (7bb2896) found seven problems. Each was checked against the code; all were real. Each is fixed, with a unit test that fails on the code before the fix.

- **React drafts were lost.** The draft check compared a field with its `defaultValue`, which React keeps equal to the typed value, so a draft in Gmail, Slack or any React form counted as the page's own. Now a field counts against what it held when the user first came to it (focus, or the first `beforeinput`); typed into with neither seen, any text counts. Choices count the same way, falling back to the page's defaults. Editors are unchanged.
- **A watch that started late saw nothing.** After an update, a relaunch's injection or an install, Margin's watch starts in pages already loaded, with nothing seen yet, and still vouched for the page, so the generic look at its fields was skipped. Now a watch knows it started after loading (`document.readyState` wasn't `loading`), and then also counts any text in any field or editor of the page. That keeps such a page awake until it next loads, if any field holds text: the safe side.
- **A tab used during a pass could sleep seconds later.** Which tabs were due was worked out once per pass, and a pass can take tens of seconds. A tab shown in between with Safari's own tab bar or ⌘1–9 isn't a switch of Margin's, so nothing held it back. Now each tab is looked at again, as the state is then, before its page is asked and again before it's sent to sleep (`used`). Whether it's in front comes from a fresh `tabs.get` both times, as before.
- **Drafts in other sites' frames were lost.** The page check ran in the top frame only and skipped frames it couldn't look into, so text in a Disqus box, a Typeform or a payment form went. Now it runs in every frame (`executeScript` with `allFrames: true`; the host permission is `<all_urls>`). The page's answer is its top frame's (the address, hidden, playing, its own fields and those of its own site's frames). Any other frame with text or media keeps the tab awake. Each frame counts the frames inside it that it can't look into, and each of those must answer for itself, or the tab stays awake (`frame unknown`). WebKit runs the check in other sites' frames, and gives an error instead of an answer for frames it has no access to: empty, `srcdoc` and `data:` frames (WebKit host, 2026-11-04). Empty and `srcdoc` frames are the page's own site, so the top frame looks into them; a `data:` frame has no site, so a page with one stays awake. Treating every error as unknown would have kept Gmail and most pages with an empty frame awake for good. The WebKit host's new `sleep-frames` scenario passes, and fails on the build before (the frame's draft put to sleep). For a browser that refuses `allFrames`, the check runs in the top frame alone, and a page with other sites' frames stays awake.
- **The sleep page's Back trap at WebKit's history cap, and Forward never reaching the woken page.** See History above: the entry itself is marked now, Margin's own wakes are noted in `storage.session`, and on an old entry the page goes on the way it was reached when the Navigation API says, and otherwise only offers "Open the page".
- **Refusals were asked again every 15 minutes.** A tab with a draft, playing, or not answering had its page asked at each pass, all day, resuming its suspended process and costing up to 3.2 s each. Now it isn't asked again for an hour, or until it's used.
- **Smaller ones.** A warm-up whose loop failed partway stayed `running` for good, which stopped sleep for good; it no longer counts once its time is up. A tab woken back to its page's address no longer teaches Margin a search engine (`learnSearchEngine`): a search results page asleep for hours may be from an engine since changed. The background notes such a wake for a minute, by the page's exact address.
- **Left as it is:** after a change of copy (an update, a relaunch), an old sleep page's entry in a tab's history is at an address that no longer opens, so Back can land on an error page once. Nothing can move entries in a tab's history. It's in TESTING.md's Back check.
- **Verified:** `npm test` passes (exit 0), with new tests in `tests/edits.test.js` (React fields, focus-time values, a late watch), `tests/sleep.test.js` (the marked entry, the cap, Back and Forward, Margin's own wakes, every frame asked and the top-frame-only fallback, a tab used during a pass, refusals not asked again, no search engine learned from a wake) and `tests/warm-up.test.js` (a failed run). In the WebKit host, `sleep-show`, `sleep-switch`, `sleep-kept`, `sleep-copy`, `sleep-frames`, `update` and `switching` pass.

## Sleeping tabs: the first check in Tirth's Safari (2026-11-04)

The build was installed in Tirth's Safari (one window, 22 tabs, 33 page processes, 12.4 GB) and left to Margin's own 15-minute pass, at the default 4 hours.

- **The app had no sleep page.** The first pass sent the YouTube tab to `sleep/sleep.html`, which wasn't in the installed app: Safari showed "Failed to open page" (−1100, file not found). `npm run safari` makes the Xcode project again only when the app's own code changes, and the project lists each file of `dist/safari/` as it was when the project was made, so a new folder never reached the app. Worse, Safari then refused every load in that tab (its policy `Ignore`, the URL "not valid"), from AppleScript and from Reload alike; the tab had to be replaced. Fixed twice:
  - `scripts/safari.sh` makes the project again when `dist/safari/` has a file more or less (the list of its files is part of what the project is made from).
  - Each pass with tabs due first reads Margin's own sleep page (`fetch` of its address). Not there: no tab is sent anywhere (`stopped: 'no sleep page'`). A tab that, once sent, ends on something that's neither the sleep page nor a web page (Safari's error page) is sent back to its address, and the pass stops there (`'sleep page failed'`): the next tab would land there too. Whether Safari takes that send-back in such a tab isn't known (it refused AppleScript's); the check before is what keeps a tab out of it.
- **Every Google results tab stayed awake.** After an update, Margin's watch starts in pages already loaded, and counted any text in any field as a draft. A results page's query sits in its search box, so 10 of Tirth's 22 tabs could never sleep until reloaded. Now a late watch doesn't count text the page put there itself (a field's value equal to its HTML default) unless the cursor is in that field. What that gives up: a React app's draft typed before Margin came (its value attribute kept in step with its value), in a field that has since lost the focus. A half-written message keeps the focus, even with its tab hidden; a draft left by clicking elsewhere in the page, across an update, for 4 hours, is the case left out. (Not checked in Safari yet that Google's box has its query as its HTML default.)
- **Mail and chat tabs: left to the user's own list.** A tab on a mail or chat site sleeps like any other and stops notifying while it's asleep. Users put such sites on "Never put these sites to sleep" themselves (Tirth, 2026-11-04). A built-in list of mail, chat and calendar sites, a check for sites allowed to notify, and waking such tabs were built (b7583b5) and taken out again.

## Margin 2: the app draws the UI, themes are HTML and CSS (2026-11-04)

Tirth's decision, after a day of experiments (window tracking, panels and shortcuts without permissions, a plain-switch build in his Safari, reverse engineering how macOS moves windows, and window-moving tests in Chrome and Safari). The full plan is `PLAN.md`; `SPEC.md` now points to it.

- **Margin.app draws all of Margin's UI** in its own windows around each browser window's page area, in Safari, Chrome and Edge, with no Accessibility permission and no private macOS calls. The extension keeps the state, tabs, windows, the page box, sleeping tabs and the few scripts a page must run.
- **A switch is a plain activation.** Safari's own blink in the page area is accepted, so Margin no longer hides it. In the plain-switch test, 11 of 12 switches blinked for 1–2 frames (30–70 ms), one for about 250 ms; with the UI in the app's windows, the sidebar stays drawn through it.
- **Themes are HTML and CSS packages** with no JavaScript, built-in or user-made. Margin's runtime renders them through declarative bindings and a fixed action list, and a theme decides the layout, including where the page sits (its `<m-page>` slot gives the page box its insets). A sanitizer, CSS rules and a Content Security Policy keep a theme from running code or sending anything anywhere. User themes are imported from a file; a gallery comes later. Built-in themes: Arcadia, Neo-brutalism and Pixel (from Tirth's "Browser Theme Explorations" canvas).
- **On the Mac the app is required.** Users without it are asked to install it. Only Chrome and Edge on Windows and Linux get the UI inside pages.
- **Normal windows:** no browser can hide its toolbar there, so Margin covers it and moves the window through the extension (`windows.update`); Margin's windows move to wherever the browser reports it landed, and the frame's edges reach past the window's edges on fast drags. Tirth tried it by hand: it works, a bit laggy, accepted for now.
- **Distribution:** Developer ID, notarized, with Margin's own updates. No App Store version.
- **Future features:** Ask, a mini player and favorites (from the canvas designs).

**Superseded by this entry** (kept for the record; their code goes in `PLAN.md` Phase 3):
- The anti-blackout switching sections, from "Switching without black: wait for the wake…" (2026-10-26) through "After the proof runs: a show only into a free page…" (2026-11-04), including keep-ready, introductions, kicks, restore behind, warm-up after a relaunch, and the show gates.
- "Themes: Margin's look as the Arcadia theme" (2026-10-25): theme JavaScript bundles, `registerTheme`, `injectTheme`.
- "Experiment: sidebar as its own window" and its dock experiment (2026-10-25): their conclusion that a persistent sidebar needs a native companion app is now the plan.
- In part: "Switches that land or say why" (2026-10-28) and "Every switch answered before its page stops waiting" (2026-11-03), whose deadlines go; "One sidebar across pages" (2026-10-26), now Windows and Linux only; "The frame stays when the sidebar is hidden" (2026-10-31) and "One layout: the page's box" (2026-11-01), whose geometry now comes from the theme; "A set-up window in Margin's Mac app" (2026-10-28), which becomes the full app; "Sleeping tabs" (2026-11-04), kept but no longer tied to keep-ready and warm-up.

## Spike 2: a theme is HTML and CSS, rendered and kept live by Margin's JS (2026-11-04)

`PLAN.md` Phase 0, spike 2, on `spike/theme-renderer`. Code where Phase 1 grows it: `extension/ui/sanitize.js`, `render.js`, `actions.js`, and Neo-brutalism as a package in `extension/themes/neo-brutalism/`. Nothing in the extension loads them yet. Checked headless only, in Playwright's WebKit and Chromium.

- **Margin parses templates itself.** A small, strict tokenizer makes a plain tree, the allowlist cleans it, and the renderer builds DOM from it with `createElement` and `setAttribute`. The browser's HTML parser never sees a theme string, so nothing is parsed, written out and parsed again (where mutation XSS hides), and what the sanitizer checked is exactly what the DOM gets. It's not HTML5's algorithm: `/>` closes any element, there are no implied end tags, and raw-text elements (`script`, `style`, `noscript`, `textarea`, …) go with everything up to their end tag.
- **CSS is tokenized as CSS Syntax 3 says** (so `u\72l(` is a url token, as in browsers), then parsed into rules and declarations and written out from the tokens. Escapes are allowed only inside strings. A comment between two tokens becomes `/**/`. Functions come from an allowlist, so a CSS feature Margin doesn't know never gets through. `var()` and `env()` are refused inside functions that load (`image-set`), and a string that looks like an address is refused anywhere. Limits: 256 KB, 4,000 rules, 20,000 declarations, nesting depth 8.
- **Fonts come from bytes, not URLs.** `theme.json` lists them, and the runtime makes `FontFace` objects from the package's bytes. That way there's no request, not even to Margin's own files, and it works in shadow roots, where Chromium ignores `@font-face`. Archivo Black and Space Grotesk (latin and latin-ext woff2, SIL OFL) were fetched once from Google Fonts (`fonts/SOURCES.txt`).
- **The view model's values are checked when they arrive.** A theme can't make a URL, but the view model holds tab titles and addresses. `m-style:--x` values may hold no `url()`, string, escape, `!important` or function beyond colors and math. `m-icon` takes only a base64 `data:` image. Theme setting defaults pass the same check.
- **Rows are keyed and skipped when unchanged.** A row is keyed by its item's `id`. When a list's rows read nothing but their own item, a row whose item is the same object isn't looked at, so the view model must be immutable (a state mirror applying deltas is). Reorders move the fewest rows (longest increasing subsequence). Rows that leave are kept, keyed, for when they come back (switching spaces back and forth).
- **Added to PLAN §3's language:** `m-icon`, `!` in `m-if test`, `key` on `m-for`, `<m-else>` inside its `<m-if>`, and the action `palette.run`. The view model also needs `space.abbr`, the tab's `letter` and `color` (for letter icons), `window.sidebarOpen`, and `palette.rows[].id/tag/text/color/selected`.
- **Results:** with 200 tabs, a first render (compiling included) takes 3.4 ms in WebKit and 2.8 ms in Chromium, or 7.7 and 5.3 ms with style and layout. Every update's JS is under 1 ms. With layout, everything is under 4 ms except switching back to the 200-tab space: 5.0 ms in WebKit and 2.6 ms in Chromium. In both engines, 82 HTML and 50 CSS payloads (plus action abuse) ran live with no tripwire set and no request. The numbers and payloads are in the spike's report.
- **Not built in the spike:** the behaviors (`m-peek`, `m-drag`, `m-resize`, `m-rename`, `m-scroll`; they're parsed and listed, not wired), focusing the palette input, `<template m-part>` parts, and the Arcadia port.

## Margin 2, Phase 1 step 1: the UI runtime core (2026-11-04)

On `feat/ui-runtime` (from `spike/theme-renderer`, with `docs/margin-app-plan` merged in). `extension/ui/`: `view-model.js`, `theme-schema.js`, `theme-loader.js`, `runtime.js`, `host.js`, and `actions.js` grown. Nothing in the extension mounts them yet (step 4); today's extension works as before. What PLAN.md doesn't already say:

- **The view model follows Margin's model, not the canvas's.** Folders hold pins, not tabs. `space.pinned` is the pinned area in the order shown (folders and loose pins together, `isFolder` tells them apart), `space.folders[]` has `pins` and `shown` (a closed folder shows the pin of the tab in front, as today), `space.pins` the pins in no folder, `space.today` the other tabs newest first, `space.newTab.active` the "+ New Tab" highlight. `shared/view.js sidebar` decides all of it; the view model only reshapes it. A pin's row is `pin.open`; its tab, when open, is `tabId`.
- **Structural sharing, one way:** each build makes a fresh model, then shares it with the last one: lists match items by `kind:id`, objects field by field, and whatever is equal comes back as the last model's frozen object (the whole model, if nothing changed). An object listed twice (a pin in `pinned` and `pins`) stays one object. With 200 tabs a build takes about 2.3 ms in Node, 1.6 ms of it `shared/view.js sidebar` (its pins × tabs address matching), which today's sidebar pays on every draw too.
- **Icons:** only base64 `data:` images reach a theme (`m-icon` takes nothing else). Safari's stored icons are; Chrome's `favIconUrl` addresses aren't, so Chrome rows show letters until Chrome's icons are stored as data too. The letter and its color are Arcadia's (`letterAvatar`, moved into `shared/view.js`, same output).
- **Not stored today:** `audible` and `loading` are read from a tab's record if there (the background doesn't record them yet); `asleep` is `sleeping`. A space's `tabCount` is its tabs open in this window. `abbr` is the first letters of the first two words, or the first two of one word.
- **Actions:** `tab.unpin` takes the pin's id (a pinned tab is its pin's row, and the background's `unpin` takes a pin). `tab.new` with no space opens the palette for a new tab (Arcadia's "+ New Tab"). `sidebar.toggle` flips `sidebarPinnedOpen` (Arcadia's toggle); the palette's "Toggle sidebar" row shows or hides it locally (peek). While the sidebar is open over the page, switches carry `show: true`. `today.clear` has no background handler yet and is refused; `nav.*` and `window.*` go to the host (`perform`): the in-page host uses the page's own history, the app will use its window. `space.new` opens the new space in the editor once the reply comes. The palette's rows are `shared/search.js rankPalette`'s; `palette.run` sends the row's own request, which stays in Margin (no `action` in the view model). Palette rows carry `title`, `subtitle`, `badge`, `emoji`, `icon`, `letter`, `color`, `selected`, `goes` (the spike's `tag`/`text` became `badge`/`title`).
- **theme.json** has a schema as data (`THEME_SCHEMA`, JSON Schema's words) walked by `checkManifest`, now in `theme-schema.js`. A bad identity (id, name, version, format, modes, layout) refuses the theme; a bad font, image or setting, and unknown fields, are removed and reported. New: `images` (a template or stylesheet may name only the images the manifest declares, which also tells Margin which files to read for a built-in theme), the setting type `toggle`, and `min`/`max`/`step`. Settings become `--m-<kebab-name>` variables on `:host,:root` in a sheet of their own, after the same value check as `m-style` (a toggle is 1 or 0); a saved value that fails falls back to the default.
- **Package caps:** 3 MB in all, 64 files, fonts (woff2 only) 512 KB each, images 256 KB each, notes (txt, md) 64 KB each, nothing else; a stored package 4.5 MB with its base64. A font that fails to load is reported, not fatal.
- **A user's theme in storage.local:** `userTheme:<id>` holds `{ format: 2, id, version, savedAt, manifest, html, css, files: { path: base64 } }`, and `userThemes` an index (`id, name, version, author, bytes, savedAt`) so Settings can list them without reading packages; one `set()` writes both. A built-in theme's id can't be taken. Checked again at every load.
- **Layout reports** come when the page's insets or radius change, or the container's size does (a resize), each with the window id and mode. The in-page host keeps the last one and calls `onLayout`; which message carries it to the background is step 4's.
- **Moved:** `checkManifest` and `sanitizeTheme` out of `sanitize.js` (into `theme-schema.js` and `theme-loader.js`), `loadTheme` out of `render.js`. The theme tests' mock is now Margin's stored state (200 rows in Personal) built into a view model by `buildViewModel`, so the renderer's minimal-update checks run on the real pipeline; activating a tab now also updates the address (the window's active tab), one text and one attribute more than the spike counted.

## Margin 2, Phase 1 step 2: the UI runtime's behaviors (2026-11-04)

On `feat/ui-runtime`. `extension/ui/behaviors/`: one module per behavior (`palette.js`, `peek.js`, `resize.js`, `menus.js`, `rename.js`, `editor.js`, `drag.js`, `scroll.js`, `swipe.js`, `press.js`), installed by `ui/runtime.js`, and the pure rules they use (`drop-rules.js`, `menu-items.js`), unit-tested in Node. Themes opt in with attributes; no theme JS. Nothing in the extension mounts the runtime yet (step 4), and today's extension works as before: its only changes are new background handlers and `moveTab`'s index maths moved into `drop-rules.js moveIndex` (same results). What PLAN.md doesn't already say:

- **Behavior attributes stay on the element** (`m-drag="tab"` is in the DOM, so a theme's CSS can select it), and the view keeps each one's scope. A row's subject is the item of the innermost `<m-for>` around it (a tab row, a pin, a folder, a chip); a theme's own copy of the attribute does nothing, since only elements the renderer made are known.
- **Parts and slots.** `<template m-part>` is compiled once and shown in its slot while the view model says so: `<m-menu>` shows `menu` while `menu` is set, the new `<m-editor>` shows `space-editor` while `editor` is set, `<m-palette>` shows `palette` while `palette.open`, `<m-notice>` shows `notice`. What a theme puts inside a slot is used when it has no such part. Margin places `<m-menu>` (`position: fixed` at `--m-menu-x`, `--m-menu-y`, kept inside the window); the theme styles everything inside it. The sanitizer allows the parts `menu`, `space-editor`, `palette`, the element `m-editor`, `m-drop="pins"`, `m-swipe="spaces"` (new: the part that swipes between spaces), and `m-input="space-name|space-emoji"`.
- **New actions.** `tab.move`, `tab.pinAt`, `pin.move`, `folder.move`, `folder.setName`, `space.setName`, `space.update` take objects with exactly their fields, each checked (`name`: at most 40 characters, no control characters; `emoji`: 8; `hex`: `#rgb`/`#rrggbb` or null). These, `tab.moveToSpace` and `space.delete` are `BEHAVIOR_ONLY`: a theme's click is refused, only behaviors and menus run them. Theme-triggerable: `folder.newWithTab`, `folder.newWithPin`, `folder.rename`, `folder.delete` (its pins stay), `space.rename`, `menu.open` ('add'), `menu.run` (an item of the open menu), `menu.close`, `editor.save`, `editor.cancel`, `editor.delete`, `editor.color` (a swatch id). Menu items keep their action in Margin; the view model shows labels only, as palette rows do.
- **Menus** are today's for tabs, pins and folders (same items, labels and order), the "+" menu (New Folder, New Space), and new, a space's: Edit Space…, Rename Space, New Space, Delete Space. Deleting asks first (the item says "Really delete?", as the editor's button does); the last space can't be deleted.
- **Renaming** makes the theme's own element editable (`contenteditable="plaintext-only"`, class `m-renaming`) instead of drawing an input, so the name keeps the theme's look; works inside a `<button>` in WebKit and Chromium. Today's rules: double-click (the folder toggle its first click made is undone), Enter or leaving saves (trimmed, 40 characters, not empty or unchanged), Escape cancels, a new folder's name is edited once it's drawn. `m-rename="space"` is new (today only the editor renames a space).
- **What's shown before the state says so,** for up to 3 s: a folder's or space's new name, Keep open on or off (`sidebar.toggle`), a space switched to (chip pressed, swipe), the sidebar width being dragged. Each goes once the state agrees.
- **Resize** writes the theme's setting `sidebarWidth` (theme.json `settings`, a `number` of px or a `length` in px; the theme reads `var(--m-sidebar-width)`), bounded by `layout.sidebarMin`…`sidebarMax` and the setting's own min and max, snapped to its `step`. `updateThemeSettings` now also takes a package theme (`themes/packages.js PACKAGE_THEMES`, or one in the `userThemes` index), merging plain values (32 at most) under setting-name keys; the runtime checks each against the theme's spec again. Neo-brutalism's sidebar is now 200–400 px (256 by default, step 2).
- **Peek:** the `m-peek` element is the hover zone; the runtime gives it `--m-reveal-zone` (today's 10 or 24 px). The pointer in it brings the sidebar out; over the page (or the theme's bare root background) it hides it after 300 ms, waiting while something is under way. A host whose page gets the pointer's events hands them in (`runtime.pointer`, `pointerLeft`).
- **Press to switch** (today's): a mouse press on a tab, an open pin or a chip switches at once and its click does nothing more; the row has `m-pressed` while the button is down. `switchingTo` and `justShown` work as today's sidebar (shared/switching.js, `JUST_SHOWN_MS`); a chip or swipe shows its space at once, from the top of its list, with its most recent tab highlighted.
- **Fixed from step 1:** the palette's field is set from `palette.query` as it opens and emptied as it closes; "Toggle sidebar" in the palette runs `sidebar.toggle`; `today.clear` is the background's new `clearToday` (the window's space's tabs that no pin shows close, archived; the tab in front gives way to the space's most recent tab left, else its start page opens first); `nav.*` go to the host first, then to the new `goBack`, `goForward`, `reload` handlers (the app's way).
- **Neo-brutalism** uses them all: `m-input` on the palette, `m-peek`, `m-resize` (a handle in the gutter), `m-rename` on folder and space names, `m-drag` on tabs, pins and folder rows, `m-drop` on the pinned area, folders, the tabs and chips, `m-scroll`, `m-swipe`, and `menu` and `space-editor` parts; its "+" opens the add menu.
- **Tests:** unit 903 (20 new: drop rules, the action a drop runs, tab move index, menu items per kind, menus and the editor in the view model, two-value action checks, clearToday, goBack/goForward/reload, package theme settings). `npm run test:theme` 38 (20 new in `tests/theme/behaviors.test.js`, both engines): press, palette typing and keys, peek in and out with its delay, resize with live layouts and bounds, menus by pointer and keys, rename (Enter, Escape, blur, space), drag to a place, a chip and a folder, scroll memory, swipe; each checks the host's messages; no request made. The swipe test dispatches trackpad-like wheel events: Playwright's wheel is a mouse's (notches of 120), which never swipes. E2E not run (as for the spike).
- **Deviations from today's sidebar:** the palette's "Toggle sidebar" turns Keep open on or off (as the theme's toggle) instead of bringing the sidebar out over the page; right-clicking a chip opens the space's menu, not the editor (a double-click still opens it); a swipe moves the theme's part by `--m-swipe-offset` but draws no pictures of the neighbouring spaces; a drop's copy glides into place but the rows around it don't slide; hover and press prewakes are gone (PLAN §8); Neo's peek hides when the pointer is over the page or the gutter, not past a fixed width.
- **Open for steps 3 and 4:** the page's zoom (Arcadia's `hostScale`) isn't applied to pointer positions, menu placement or the ghost; `m-drag="space"` (no handler to reorder spaces), `m-rename="tab"` and `m-scroll="palette|archive"` are accepted and inert; the in-page host must feed page pointer positions for peek and keep ⌥S's show/hide toggle, the new-tab page's Escape (`cancelNewTab`), carry-open and the switch cover; menus aren't in the top layer (popover) yet.

## Margin 2, Phase 1 step 3: Arcadia as a theme package, and Margin's own words (2026-11-05)

On `feat/ui-runtime`. `extension/themes/arcadia-2/` (`theme.json`, `theme.html`, `theme.css`, icons as inline svg), named so while the old `arcadia` (theme JS) still runs today's pages; step 4 renames it. Nothing mounts it yet; today's extension works as before (its only change: `themes/packages.js` lists the package, which the background reads for theme settings). The theme author guide is `extension/themes/README.md`. What PLAN.md doesn't already say:

- **The port follows today's DOM and CSS** (sidebar.css, palette.css, newtab.css, chrome.js) class for class where it can, so measurements carry over. The page card is `<m-page>` and its 100vmax spread shadow is the gray frame (as today's `.frame .hole`), so the card stays clear for the page; a `.card-edge` above the sidebar draws the card's soft shadow and holds the page-load pill (`tab.loading`). Rows are `<a>` elements (an action needs a button or a link) with the close button inside. The root binds `data-sidebar` (`open` docked, `hidden`, `peek`), `framed` (`window.frameWhenHidden`, new in the view model), `loading` and `newtab` (the launcher is shown). Docked: the card at the sidebar's width, 8 px frame, radius 6.25; hidden: 8 px all round, or the whole window with the frame off; peek: the floating card, the page where it was. The width is the theme's `sidebarWidth` setting, a number, 160–500, step 10, 260 by default (`var(--m-sidebar-width)`).
- **Light and dark:** as today, the sidebar, frame, menus and notices are one look in both, the palette always dark, the launcher follows the system (`prefers-color-scheme`).
- **New parts and slots:** `launcher` in `<m-launcher>` (shown while `launcher` is set: the view model has it when the host is Margin's new-tab page: the space's pins as tiles, the archive's latest 12 with `ago`, "Show all"), and `prompt` in `<m-prompt>` (Margin's prompts and confirmations: `title`, `text`, `actions`). `notice` gets `actions` (buttons). New actions: `notice.run`, `prompt.run` (a button of the one shown, by id; the host's `perform('notice' | 'prompt', { id, action })` runs it, then it closes), `notice.close`, `prompt.close`, `launcher.more`. `m-input="launcher"`: Enter runs what the palette's "go" row would for the text (an address goes there in this tab, words are searched), and the field has the focus as the launcher shows. The menu has a `label`, the editor `labels` (title, name, emoji, colors, save, cancel).
- **Margin's own words (the security rule).** Inside the `menu`, `menu-item`, `notice`, `prompt` and `space-editor` parts, and the `<m-menu>`, `<m-notice>`, `<m-editor>`, `<m-prompt>` slots (their content and their own attributes), every visible text must be bound: a text node the theme wrote there is reduced to its `{{bindings}}` (dropped if none is left) and reported, and so are static values of `title`, `alt`, `placeholder`, `aria-label`, `aria-description`, `aria-roledescription`, `aria-valuetext`, `aria-placeholder` and the braille labels. Svg shapes, images and decoration stay; svg `<text>` and `<title>` are text. The sanitizer does it (`GUARDED_PARTS`, `guardText`) and the renderer again as it compiles, whatever tree it's given. Neo-brutalism now binds its editor's words and its menu's label, and passes with nothing removed.
- **A stylesheet writes no text**, because CSS can't tell which elements its rules reach: `content` and `quotes` take only `""`, no `var()`/`env()` in them, counters and list markers only as numbers or symbols (`upper-alpha` spells words from counter values), no strings in `list-style`, `text-overflow`, `hyphenate-character`, `text-emphasis`. Today's "Drop to pin" (a CSS string) became an element.
- **What still can't be stopped:** text outside Margin's parts drawn over them, and pictures of words (a bundled image, a font's glyphs). Margin places `<m-menu>` at z-index 2147483600; a theme element can still go above it.
- **Parity check** (`npm run parity:arcadia [out-dir]`, `tests/theme/parity.js`): today's Arcadia from its own modules (mounted as content.js and newtab.js mount them) and arcadia-2 through the runtime with a mock host, the same fixed state (3 spaces, an open and a closed folder, loose pins, 10 tabs with playing and sleeping ones, icons and letters, an archive, width 280), 1280×800, headless WebKit and Chromium, every request refused, compared pixel by pixel in the page (a channel off by more than 24). Docked, docked in dark mode, loading, hidden with and without the frame, peek, palette, menu, space editor, notice, launcher light and dark: 0 px for both hidden cases, else 0.05–0.075 % (514–772 px) of the window, all of it the new playing and asleep marks (below), plus in WebKit's palette the text caret's blink (62 px) and the launcher card's corner antialiasing (about 20 px).
- **Different from today's on purpose:** a speaker on playing tabs and a faded gray icon on sleeping ones (today's Arcadia shows neither; the title is a little shorter on a playing row); an address button in windowed mode only; the notice has no timer of its own (the host clears it); the prompt part is new. **Kept as today's though odd:** the notice is as narrow as its words allow (`width: min-content`), as today's is in a box of no size; worth fixing on purpose later.
- **Not ported, by design or for step 4:** pull-to-refresh and swipe-navigation badges, the switch cover and the startup cover (Margin 2 accepts the switch blink; the app draws its own), the warm-up note (superseded), the frame's four-strip "rims" (a memory saving per tab: step 4 should measure the in-page overlay's cost), `hover-stale`, the neighbouring spaces' pictures in a swipe, the motion of Keep open turning on (the card's edge slides; the frame and the sidebar jump), today's top-layer popovers for the menu and palette, the palette's short-window sizes from JS under page zoom (media queries here), and the new-tab page's Settings form: the launcher shows its "Settings" heading (`settings.open`); the form needs Margin-drawn controls or new bound inputs.
- **Tests:** unit 915 (12 new: the guard on menus, notices, prompts, the editor and slots, `guardText`, the CSS text rule, both built-in themes passing it, the view model's labels, notices, prompts, launcher and frame, the new actions, arcadia-2's card radius and shadow against today's). `npm run test:theme` 54 (16 new, `tests/theme/arcadia.test.js`, both engines): loads as a built-in theme, the sidebar and its rows, layouts docked, at a set width, out of range, hidden with and without the frame, peek; every part (palette with typing and Escape, menu, editor, notice with a real click reaching the host, prompt); the launcher (focus, tiles, archive, Enter navigates); the renderer guarding a template that skipped the sanitizer; no requests, no errors. E2E not run.

## Margin 2, Phase 1 step 4: the switch-over to the UI runtime (2026-11-05)

On `feat/ui-runtime`. The UI runtime (`extension/ui/`) with Arcadia's package is now the only in-page UI: web pages (`content/core.js`) and Margin's new-tab page (`newtab/newtab.js`) both draw through it. On the Mac this is a development host until Margin.app exists (PLAN.md §1). What PLAN.md doesn't already say:

- **Arcadia's package is `themes/arcadia/`** (it was `arcadia-2`), the default theme. Its folder still holds `theme.js` (settings and the first-paint placeholders) and `docked/` with their templates: first paint is Phase 3's. `state/model.js normalizeThemes` folds `themes['arcadia-2']` and an old top-level `sidebarWidth` into `themes.arcadia.sidebarWidth` (Arcadia's own first, then the package's, then the top-level one; snapped to 10, 160–500) and drops the old keys.
- **The theme reaches pages from the background, never by URL**: no `web_accessible_resources`. The background reads the active theme's package from its own files (`background/theme-package.js`) and keeps it in `storage.local` (`uiTheme`: `{ id, version, pkg }`, rewritten when the theme or the files change, compared by content so a rebuild with the same version counts). A page takes it from its first storage read (and later change events; it's not read again with the state: `shared/stored.js NOT_FOR_PAGES`), and asks with the `themePackage` message only when it isn't there. Without the stored copy every page load waited for the background to wake (Safari unloads it when idle), and in the WebKit host no page loaded after the background's ~5 s got a UI at all. Either way the page checks the package as any (`ui/theme-loader.js loadTheme`). Margin's new-tab page reads the files itself (an extension page).
- **The in-page host** (`ui/host.js createInPageHost`) takes core's state (`source`), core's `send` (deadlines, hints, retries) and core's `perform` (notices with their buttons' callbacks; on a web page the palette's address opens a new tab). The shadow host now fills the window (`shared/dom.js createHost({ fill })`, past the page's box as `.window-probe` was), takes no pointer itself, and `contain: layout` makes it the box `position: fixed` in the theme is placed against (menus, the drag copy).
- **The page's box is the theme's page slot**: the runtime's layout report (`<m-page>`'s insets, already in the page's pixels) drives `page-link.js` (`content/layout.js`, rewritten); measured again at once after every render so the box moves in the same frame as the theme; no box when the slot is the whole window, or with Margin out of the way. While the sidebar's edge is dragged the box follows live (`m-resizing` on the theme's root).
- **Pointer pass-through:** the theme's root covers the frame, so over the page's slot it gets `m-passthrough` (`pointer-events: none` on the root, its children keep theirs: what the theme draws over the page still takes the pointer). It's on by default and off only while the pointer is on the frame itself, so the page's own hit tests (`elementFromPoint`, `caretPositionFromPoint`, a press with no move before it, a pinch) find the page. `m-away` hides all but notices and prompts while Margin is out of the way (Chrome outside full screen, a copy that stood back).
- **What the page still does around the runtime** (`content/layout.js`): the pointer over the page handed to the runtime's reveal edge (`runtime.pointer`, `pointerLeft`); ⌥S (`runtime.sidebar.toggle`: Keep open off, or out until ⌥S, Escape or a click on the page); a moment out after ⌘⌥←/→ (`flash`); carry-open (`show('hover' | 'toggle')`); the first-paint placeholder's hand-over. Page zoom: `rt.scale()` (Margin's pixels per page pixel, as `hostScale` was) converts pointer positions for the reveal zone, menu placement, the drag copy and resizing.
- **Kept as they were:** the page box's world script, gestures, pinch, the draft check, the palette key (`content/palette.js` now opens the runtime's palette), cut-off, copies and ownership, hello, the anti-blackout machinery (Phase 3 deletes it). Core's own switching (next/previous keys from the background) highlights in the runtime (`setUi({ switchingTo })`); the runtime's own clicks report theirs (`onSwitching`) and their slowness and failure (`onSwitchSlow`: the loading pill; `onSwitchFailed`: "That tab is no longer open"). The page's load sets `ui.pageLoading` (the view model's `tab.loading`).
- **The new-tab page:** the runtime in the document, `launcher: true` (the view model shows the launcher, and the sidebar beside it whatever Keep open says); `#launcher` and `#settings` (Settings open, from a web page's Settings button: `openLauncher { settings: true }`) never hand off. ⌘T's hand-off, the fresh tab's Escape (`cancelNewTab`, through the runtime's new `onPalette('close')`), and leaving a fresh tab after a choice that shows another tab are as before; a pin with no tab opens in this tab on a new-tab page; an archived tab restored takes its place. Chrome outside full screen: Margin's `prompt` offers full screen. Zoom keeps Margin's size (the theme's root unzoomed).
- **Settings** (`ui/settings-form.js`, `ui/behaviors/settings.js`): fields in the view model (`settingsForm`, while the launcher's Settings are open), drawn by the theme's guarded `settings` part in `<m-settings>` with Margin's own inputs: `m-input="setting-number" | "setting-toggle"` (Margin sets the type, a toggle is a `switch`), `"setting-choice"` on a `<select>` (Margin draws its options), `"setting-list"` on a `<textarea>` (its text Margin's; the tokenizer keeps that one textarea, empty), `"setting-shortcut"` (read-only, records the next combination; Margin's shortcuts wait meanwhile), and `settings.run` for buttons (Wake all, Reset shortcuts). Every value is saved by the field's own rule (bounds, rounding, `siteList`, shortcut parsing, a taken shortcut refused with a note). Coverage: theme (Arcadia, Neo-brutalism), the search engine note, archive hours, sleep hours, never-sleep sites, Wake all, the three gesture switches, Chrome full screen and full screen only (Chrome), frame when hidden, the ⌘T hint (Safari), the five shortcuts and Reset. **Dropped:** keep-ready minutes and warm-up tabs (PLAN.md §8), and the switch log's buttons (development diagnostics; only the buttons: `background/diagnostics.js` and the `switchLog` handler stay until Phase 3).
- **Neo-brutalism** gets the parts it lacked (notice, prompt, launcher, settings) and no longer paints under the page: its color is the page card's spread shadow and its dots a layer with the card cut out (a mask), so the page shows through the slot.
- **Deleted:** `themes/register.js`, Arcadia's `sidebar.js`, `chrome.js`, `content.js`, `palette.js`, `newtab.js`, `newtab.html`, `assets.js` and `styles/*.css`; the theme bundles and their registration (`syncTheme`; `margin-theme` is now in `registered.js OLD_IDS`, and `themes/arcadia/content.bundle.js` an empty legacy stub, since Safari's stored registration names it), `injectTheme`, `requestTheme`; `build-styles.js`'s CSS strings; the old palette helpers and the pixel parity script (it compared against the old theme). Kept: the docked first-paint CSS, `at-*.js`, the LEGACY stubs.
- **Different from before:** no switch cover, startup cover or arrival cover (Margin 2 accepts the blink; the background's restore-behind and introductions therefore don't run, as they wait for a covered page); no pull-to-refresh or swipe-navigation badges (the gestures work); the Keep open slide and the frame's thin "rims" are gone (the frame is the card's shadow, the root takes the pointer on it); a pin tile on the new-tab page focuses the pin's tab if it's open in this window (`openPin`) rather than any tab with its address; the launcher doesn't zoom with ⌘+ (Margin keeps its size); the palette and menus aren't in the top layer; a theme's fonts (Neo-brutalism's) are added to the page's own `document.fonts`.
- **Tests:** unit 920 (new: `tests/settings-form.test.js`, `tests/theme-package.test.js`, the in-page host with core's state, the layout's box from the slot, the arcadia-2 settings migration; gone with their code: the palette helpers', the assets and old-markup checks). `npm run test:theme` 58 in WebKit and Chromium (new: the launcher's Settings driven by real clicks and keys, the host's show/hide key, Escape and flash; the pixel parity script went with the old theme). E2E not run (Tirth: no E2E for now); its files read the new DOM's selectors where the change was mechanical (`.launcher-query`, `.page`, `.margin-sidebar`, Settings by label), and flows that need Settings open or the old badges and top-layer popovers still need a pass.
- **WebKit host, all 84 scenarios**, against the same run on 8241c13: 61 pass (80 before). Failing on both: `press` (a different check each time; it passed on its own in a rerun), `slowunloaded`, `unloadedlate`, `space-click`. Newly failing, all by design: the cover, arrival and loading covers are gone, so the background neither introduces a never-shown tab nor restores one behind a covered page (`unseen`, `unseennav`, `unloaded`, `unloadedheavy`, `unloadedtwo`, `unloadedspace`, `loadcover`, `restartrepair`, `behind`, `behindlate`, `behindbox`, `behindfocus`, `behindkeys`, `arrival`, `shelllate`, `shellcover`, and `sleep-switch`'s "loaded behind" check: in `unseen`, B is shown before its page has Margin, which the old flow's restore-behind guaranteed), and hover prewakes are gone (`wake-reveal`, `wake-space`). Those are PLAN.md §8's scenarios for Phase 3 to delete. Updated for the new DOM: `closed-frame` (the theme's layout, no slide or strips), `zoom`, `pinch-box`, `dragtab`, `newtab`, `spaces`, `recorder`, `reloaded-newtab` (a new-tab page's own tab is the lit "+ New Tab", not a row), `box`, the theme check. Arcadia's rows carry their ids as `data-*` for them.

## Margin 2: Margin.app owns the lasting data on the Mac (2026-11-04)

Tirth's decision. On the Mac, spaces, pins, folders, the archive, settings, theme settings, user themes and site icons live in Margin.app's files (`~/Library/Application Support/Margin/`, one data set per browser and profile), not in the extension's storage. Safari can wipe an extension's storage (Margin's came back empty after Safari blocked and re-enabled it on 2026-11-04), the extension's quota no longer limits themes and icons, Time Machine backs the files up, and one place decides. The extension keeps the live tab state (tab ids exist only in the running browser) and a cache of the app's data for the moments before the app links; a wiped cache is refilled on link, and changes made while unlinked are queued and merged by the app (last write wins per item, deletions kept as tombstones). Windows and Linux keep the extension's storage as the main store. Details in `PLAN.md` section 6, built in Phase 2.

## Margin 2: Margin's sidebar is the real list of tabs (2026-11-04)

Tirth's decision. Tabs live in Margin's data (in Margin.app on the Mac); a browser tab is the open form of a Margin tab. Tabs Safari has and Margin doesn't are added to the window's space; tabs Margin has and Safari doesn't stay in their space as not-open tabs and open, in the window they're clicked in, when clicked. A tab closed on its own (×, ⌘W, Safari's tab bar) goes to the archive; tabs lost when a whole window closes or Safari quits, crashes or doesn't restore them are kept. Sleeping tabs follow the same model: Margin closes the browser tab and keeps it as a not-open tab, freeing all its memory and leaving nothing broken if Margin is removed (Chrome and Edge keep `tabs.discard`). The sleep page from "Sleeping tabs" (2026-11-04) goes in Phase 3. A reopened tab starts without its Back history or scroll position. Details in `PLAN.md` section 6.

## Margin 2 ships for macOS only (2026-11-04)

Tirth's decision. Windows and Linux are not planned for now. Margin 2 is Margin.app plus its extensions for Safari, Chrome and Edge on the Mac. The UI runtime still runs inside pages during Phase 1, to build and test it before the app exists, and in automated tests, but no product build ships an in-page UI; without the app, the extension asks the user to install it. Where `PLAN.md` mentioned Windows and Linux (the in-page host as a product, the extension's storage as the main store there), it no longer applies.

## Margin 2: full screen is macOS's own for now; spaces of other profiles switch by window (2026-11-04)

Tirth's decision, after testing Arc-style switches between spaces of different browser profiles (`PLAN.md` section 15). A profile's spaces live in that profile's window; switching to one brings that window forward through its own copy of Margin (`windows.update({focused: true})`). In macOS full screen each window is its own Space, so the switch is macOS's slide (about 366 ms) and Margin's overlay drops out for about 210 ms; screen-filling windows stacked one per profile switched in about 51 ms with nothing in between. Margin builds on macOS full screen now; its own screen-filling mode, with instant profile switches, comes later with windowed mode, since both need Margin to cover the browser's toolbar.

## Margin 2: instant switches between full-screen Spaces, with one private call and a fallback (2026-11-05)

Tirth's decision, after watching a demo. Switching to a space whose window is in another full-screen Space (another profile's window) normally plays macOS's slide (about 366 ms) and drops Margin's overlay for about 210 ms. SkyLight's private `SLSManagedDisplaySetCurrentSpace`, called from outside both apps, switched in under 1 ms with a clean cut, no permission and system protection left on. Margin uses it as the one exception to the no-private-calls rule: looked up at run time, checked after every call (the target on screen and the active Space changed within about 100 ms), and on any failure it falls back to the public switch (`windows.update({focused: true})` from the profile's copy, with the slide) and stays off until the next launch. Keyboard focus still goes through the profile's copy. Risks accepted: Apple may change or remove the call in an update (the fallback covers it), and the Dock and Mission Control may not register the switch (to check in Phase 2). Margin ships with Developer ID, so no store review stands in the way. Details in `PLAN.md` section 15.

## Margin 2: switching to another profile's space opens its window, with macOS's slide (2026-11-05)

Tirth's decision. Only one browser window exists at a time: switching to a space of another profile creates that profile's full-screen window (`windows.create({state: 'fullscreen'})` from its copy) and closes the old one, so Mission Control and the three-finger swipe never show an extra window, even with many profiles. Each such switch plays macOS's Space slide (about 0.4–0.65 s, Margin's overlay gone for about 180 ms) and the profile's pages reload from Margin's tab records. Keeping a window per profile with the instant switch was faster (13–47 ms) but shows one Space per profile in Mission Control; with ten profiles that's ten Spaces. Ways to remove the slide while keeping one window were not pursued further. The private instant switch from the 2026-11-05 entry isn't used for now, since there are never two full-screen windows to jump between. Details in `PLAN.md` section 15.

## Margin 2, Phase 2 step 2: Margin.app's shell (2026-11-05)

On `feat/margin-app` (from 07ab573). The app's end of the link, window tracking, the overlays and their page, the keys, with a fake extension to test against; no extension changes but the new `extension/ui/host-app.js`. Step 3 builds the extension's end against `app/PROTOCOL.md`. What PLAN.md doesn't already say:

- **Where the code is.** `app/shell/` is Margin.app's shell (13 Swift files), compiled into the app target by `scripts/xcode-add-sources.py` (from `feat/native-panel`, now adding `app/shell/` and `dist/app/` as `Resources/app`). `app/safari/` keeps the set-up window, gains `AppDelegate.swift` (an accessory app with no Dock icon while the set-up window is closed, as on `feat/native-panel`), and its extension handler passes a copy's `wantLink` on (only its nonce). `scripts/make-xcode.sh` turns the app's App Sandbox off (Developer ID, PLAN.md §6: it listens on 127.0.0.1, and later writes Chrome's host files); the extension keeps its sandbox. Checked by generating and building a scratch copy of the project with another bundle id (`xcodebuild`, never launched); Tirth's `xcode/` wasn't touched and needs `npm run safari` (or make-xcode.sh) again to pick this up.
- **The link, version 2** (`app/PROTOCOL.md`). Reused from `feat/native-panel`: the WebSocket by hand, the token and key, the mirror, ticks. New: `hello` names the copy's `browser` and `profile`, and a link is known by both, so Safari's copies (one per profile) are linked at once; a newer link for the same profile replaces the older; a copy that isn't `owner` is closed, as before. `setLayout` per window (numbered, only on a change of half a point or more, and only when measured at the window's current size: a report from before the overlay was sized to its window, or mid-resize, is never sent). Themes come as `theme` pieces of 512 KB with the whole's SHA-256 (any order; another transfer starts over; a whole that doesn't add up is refused and the theme in use kept). Nothing the app sends is over 1 MB: `AppMessage.encode` refuses it. The panel's `cover` lease is gone.
- **Window tracking:** `CGWindowListCopyWindowInfo` with `.optionAll` (every Space), read 4 times a second while a linked browser is in front, once a second otherwise, 30 a second for 1 s after an app activates, the Space or the displays change, a press, or a window list or snapshot arrives. Nothing is read until a linked browser has come to the front after the first link. Matching (`WindowMatcher.swift`): each edge within 8 pt, then by cost: focus agreeing with the browser's frontmost on-screen window (only while the browser is the active app), then the match before, then being on screen, then distance; one window each. Normal-layer, visible windows of at least 120 pt only.
- **Overlays:** level `.normal`, ordered `.above` the browser window, re-ordered when the window server's list shows anything between them (and checked after every press, through the burst). Collection behavior `[.canJoinAllSpaces, .fullScreenAuxiliary, .stationary, .ignoresCycle]`, as Phase 0's overlay probe over full-screen Safari used (Spike 1's own test app used `.floating` and no collection behavior). Shown only for full-screen windows for now (windowed mode is Phase 4: `Shell.Options.windowedToo`), only once the page says it's drawn, sized to the window before its page loads. The web view is transparent through WebKit's `drawsBackground` key (as on `feat/native-panel`; there's no public setting on macOS), served from `margin-app://app/` with the CSP in PLAN.md §4 plus `frame-src`, `form-action` and `base-uri` none, and content rules that block every http(s), ws(s), file and ftp load. One web view, one runtime, per window.
- **The page** (`app/overlay/`, built by `scripts/build.js` into `dist/app/`: one script, Safari's platform profile stamped in, committed and checked by a unit test). It asks the app for its window, the stored values and the theme (`hello`), draws with the runtime in the document, and reports the slot after every DOM change, not only at the runtime's animation frame: an overlay off screen gets no frames (the tests showed it), and the page box must follow at once.
- **Pass-through:** the overlay ignores the mouse while the pointer is over the slot (its rounded corners count as the theme's), except over what the theme draws there that takes the pointer: the page reports those boxes (`hitRects`: the theme root's descendants overlapping the slot whose `pointer-events` isn't `none`, with the root itself passing the pointer through, `m-passthrough`, exactly as in pages); more than 64 and the overlay takes the whole window. While a mouse button is held nothing changes (a drag stays where it started).
